For prediction, if the control (punishment disabled) efficiency is known, enabling punishment with equal, stable power is likely to increase efficiency, but enabling punishment with unequal, unstable power is likely to decrease efficiency relative to the equal, stable punishment case. The effect is driven by increased competitive punishment rather than increased cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for settings with 4 players, 10 rounds, continuous contributions, no chat, and full feedback on others' actions.

- source: Masclet, D., Noussair, C. N., & Villeval, M. C. (2013). THREAT AND PUNISHMENT IN PUBLIC GOOD EXPERIMENTS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a linear public goods game with costly punishment, adding a stage where players can issue non-binding threats to punish (conditioned on others' contribution levels) increases average contributions and, in the latter periods, increases group efficiency (payoff) compared to a baseline with punishment but no threats. However, the cost of punishment initially offsets the benefit of higher contributions, so efficiency only improves in later rounds. If a second-order punishment stage is added (where players can punish those who fail to carry out their threats), threats become less severe, contributions and efficiency fall, and welfare is equal to or below the baseline. The effect of threats is robust to changes in punishment effectiveness, but the efficiency gain is less persistent when punishment is less effective. Overall, threats can improve efficiency, but only under certain conditions and mainly in longer interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with punishment, adding a non-binding threat stage can increase efficiency (group payoff) relative to a baseline with punishment but no threats, but only in the latter periods of repeated play. The efficiency gain is modest and depends on the absence of second-order punishment (punishing those who fail to carry out threats). If second-order punishment is possible, efficiency falls to or below baseline. The effect is also sensitive to punishment effectiveness (punishment technology). For prediction, this suggests that enabling threats (structured, non-binding, and observable) can increase efficiency above the control (punishment-only) baseline, but only in longer games and only if threats are not themselves subject to enforcement. The effect is stronger when punishment is more effective (higher punishment technology). The control efficiency (punishment-only) is a good lower bound; threats can improve on this, but only under the right design conditions.

- source: Markussen, T., Putterman, L., & Tyran, J. R. (2014). Self-Organization for Collective Action: An Experimental Study of Voting on Sanction Regimes. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with 5 players, groups could vote between no sanctions (NS), informal sanctions (IS: peer punishment), and formal sanctions (FS: centralized punishment with fixed and variable costs, deterrent or non-deterrent). Both IS and deterrent FS substantially increased group efficiency and earnings relative to NS. IS were highly cost-effective, yielding higher returns per point spent on sanctions than FS in most conditions. IS were especially effective when chosen by vote, with group earnings and efficiency about 30% higher than when IS were imposed exogenously. Non-deterrent FS also increased efficiency when chosen by vote, but less so than IS. High fixed costs for FS reduced both their efficiency and their popularity. Reducing the effectiveness of IS (increasing cost per unit punishment) decreased their efficiency somewhat, but IS remained popular and relatively effective. Allowing IS and FS to coexist did not substantially change these patterns. Overall, IS can be as or more effective than FS in promoting efficiency, especially when adopted democratically.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with 5 players, enabling informal peer punishment (IS) can increase group efficiency to levels comparable to or exceeding those achieved by formal sanctions, especially when IS are chosen by vote. The efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost-effectiveness of the sanction regime, the fixed and variable costs of punishment, and whether the regime is adopted endogenously. High fixed costs for FS reduce their efficiency advantage. IS are most effective when punishment is relatively cheap and well-targeted. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling IS is likely to yield a substantial increase in efficiency over the no-punishment baseline, and that the effect is robust to moderate changes in punishment technology and institutional coexistence. The control (no-sanction) efficiency is typically well below full cooperation, while IS and deterrent FS can approach or reach high efficiency, with IS often being more cost-effective.

- source: Chaudhuri, A., & Paichayontvijit, T. (2017). On the long-run efficacy of punishments and recommendations in a laboratory public goods game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In repeated linear public goods games with groups of four, both peer punishment and exhortative recommendations (public messages) increase efficiency compared to a control with no intervention. Recommendations have a strong and immediate positive effect on efficiency, especially with fixed group composition (partners), but this effect decays over time. Punishment starts with lower efficiency due to the cost of punishing but shows an upward trend over time, eventually catching up to or surpassing recommendations in some conditions, particularly with longer time horizons. However, in these experiments, punishment never outperforms the restart recommendation treatment. The effect of punishment is stronger with fixed matching than with random rematching. The cost-effectiveness of punishment depends on the punishment technology (cost/impact ratio) and the length of the game. The study provides direct evidence on how efficiency changes when punishment is enabled, with detailed efficiency data for control, recommendation, and punishment treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality, directly relevant evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in standard linear public goods games. It reports efficiency (average earnings as a fraction of the social optimum) for control and punishment treatments, with and without recommendations, across different matching protocols and time horizons. The results show that punishment can increase efficiency over time, but the effect is not immediate and depends on the number of rounds, matching protocol, and punishment cost/impact. The efficiency gain from punishment is more pronounced with longer games and fixed matching. The paper also provides detailed information on the punishment cost and technology, allowing for direct mapping to prediction dimensions. For prediction, the control efficiency and the game design dimensions (player count, rounds, matching, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are all reported, making this paper a strong source for model training and validation.

- source: Lowen, A., & Schmitt, P. (2013). Cooperation limitations under a one-time threat of expulsion and punishment. *Journal of Socio-economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a one-time punishment opportunity after a one-time expulsion vote in a 15-round public goods game increases contributions before the punishment phase, but does not improve overall efficiency or average earnings compared to expulsion alone. The threat of punishment raises the standard for avoiding expulsion, leading to more expulsions, including of moderate contributors. The costs associated with punishment (both direct and through increased expulsions) offset the gains from higher contributions, resulting in nearly identical efficiency between the two treatments. Thus, in this design, adding a costly punishment mechanism to a single expulsion opportunity does not yield net efficiency gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with a single expulsion opportunity, adding a one-time, costly punishment mechanism increases contributions but does not increase efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, under similar game parameters (large group, 15 rounds, MPCR 0.2, costly punishment, no communication), the efficiency with punishment enabled will be very similar to the efficiency with punishment disabled, as the costs of punishment offset the increased contributions. The findings are most applicable to settings with infrequent, costly punishment and expulsion opportunities, and may not generalize to games with frequent or low-cost punishment.

- source: Kölle, F. (2015). Heterogeneity and cooperation: The role of capability and valuation on public goods provision. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This experiment compares three public goods game treatments: homogeneous groups, groups with one high-valuation member (valuation heterogeneity), and groups with one high-capability member (capability heterogeneity). Each group plays 20 rounds, with punishment enabled in rounds 11-20. Efficiency (group payoff as % of social optimum) is measured with and without punishment. In homogeneous groups, punishment does not significantly change efficiency. In valuation-heterogeneous groups, punishment does not significantly increase efficiency and increases inequality. In capability-heterogeneous groups, punishment significantly increases efficiency (from 86% to 93% of optimum) and reduces inequality. The effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency is thus conditional on the type of heterogeneity, being most effective when group members differ in capability but not in valuation.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect is not uniform but depends on the type of group heterogeneity. If the game is homogeneous or has only capability heterogeneity (where some players' contributions are more productive but all value the public good equally), enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency, especially in the capability-heterogeneous case. If the group has valuation heterogeneity (where some players value the public good more), punishment is less effective or even counterproductive for efficiency and increases inequality. The control efficiency (without punishment) is also higher in capability-heterogeneous groups than in valuation-heterogeneous groups. Thus, when using game design dimensions and control efficiency to predict treatment efficiency, the type of heterogeneity (capability vs. valuation) is a critical moderator. Other design features (e.g., punishment cost, group size, feedback) are held constant in this study.

- source: Kube, S., Schaube, S., Schildberg-Hörisch, H., & Khachatryan, E. (2015). Institution formation and cooperation with heterogeneous agents. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with three players, the introduction of an institution formation stage (via unanimity voting) that, if implemented, imposes binding contribution obligations enforced by deterrent sanctions, leads to large increases in efficiency (group payoff as a fraction of the maximum possible). For homogeneous groups, a symmetric institution (equal obligations) is almost always implemented and achieves efficiency above 90%. For heterogeneous groups, a symmetric institution is often rejected by low-benefit players due to inequality aversion, but an asymmetric institution (with obligations tailored to equalize payoffs) is much more likely to be implemented, though at a cost to total efficiency. In all cases, efficiency is higher when the institution is implemented than when it is not. The main moderators are the distribution of marginal per-capita returns (homogeneous vs. heterogeneous) and the symmetry of institutional obligations. The study does not manipulate punishment cost or technology directly, as the institution, if implemented, is enforced with sufficiently strong sanctions to ensure compliance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with three players and 20 rounds, the introduction of a binding, deterrent-sanction institution (enabled by unanimity voting) increases efficiency relative to the baseline game without such an institution. The effect is strongest when the institution is both efficient and equitable (i.e., symmetric for homogeneous groups, asymmetric for heterogeneous groups). When the institution is rejected (often due to inequality aversion), efficiency is lower, but still generally higher than in the baseline. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of a strong, binding punishment institution (with high compliance) should be expected to increase efficiency, but the magnitude depends on group heterogeneity and the design of institutional obligations. The study does not provide evidence on the effect of varying punishment cost or technology, as these are not manipulated; the punishment regime is always fully deterrent. Control efficiency (without institution) is reported and can be used as a baseline for prediction.

- source: Engelmann, D., & Nikiforakis, N. (2015). In the long-run we are all dead: on the benefits of peer punishment in rich environments. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with 4 players and 30 rounds, the authors compare three treatments: (1) No Punishment (NP), (2) Standard Punishment (SP, one anonymous punishment stage per round, IDs randomized each round), and (3) Rich Punishment (RP, multiple punishment stages per round, fixed IDs, full information, allowing for retaliation and complex strategies). In SP, group earnings increase significantly over time and surpass NP in the second half of the experiment. In RP, group earnings do not increase significantly over time and are not significantly different from NP, even in later rounds. Contributions are higher in both punishment treatments than in NP, but only in SP do they increase over time. In RP, some groups achieve full cooperation, but others experience breakdowns and feuds, leading to high punishment costs and no net efficiency gain. The results suggest that the efficiency of peer punishment is highly sensitive to the structure of the punishment environment, especially the possibility of retaliation and the richness of available strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency effect of enabling peer punishment in public goods games depends on the design of the punishment mechanism. When punishment is limited to a single, anonymous stage per round (SP), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control. When punishment is unconstrained and allows for retaliation (RP), enabling punishment does not increase efficiency relative to the control, despite higher contributions, due to offsetting punishment costs and feuding. For prediction, the presence of multiple punishment stages, fixed IDs, and full information about who punished whom are critical moderators that can nullify the efficiency gains of punishment. Control efficiency (NP) is similar across treatments, but the treatment effect of punishment depends on these design features.

- source: Rockenbach, B., & Wolff, I. (2016). Designing Institutions for Social Dilemmas. *German Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that when subjects are allowed to design institutions for a repeated public goods game, they initially favor punishment or redistribution mechanisms, but these are associated with lower efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) compared to rules that emphasize communication (framing, appeals) or participative elements (endogenous rule change). Over the course of the experiment, the use of punishment declines, and efficiency increases, suggesting that punishment is not an efficient solution in this context. Communication and participative rule features are positively associated with efficiency. The study provides direct evidence that punishment, as typically implemented by endogenous designers, tends to reduce efficiency, mainly due to its costs, and that designers learn to move away from it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with endogenous institution design, enabling punishment mechanisms tends to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) baseline, especially when punishment is costly and not peer-administered. The effect is robust across multiple rounds and rule sets, with efficiency increasing as designers move away from punishment toward communication and participative mechanisms. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment (especially costly, centralized, or automatic forms) is likely to result in lower efficiency than the control, unless the punishment is highly refined or replaced by redistribution mechanisms with lower waste. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in similar PGG environments.

- source: Kingsley, D. C., & Brown, T. C. (2016). Endogenous and costly institutional deterrence in a public good experiment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a linear public goods game with a central authority that can monitor and punish free-riders, increasing the probability of monitoring (deterrence) increases contributions up to a point, but the benefit to group earnings depends on the cost of monitoring. When monitoring is cheap or moderate, higher deterrence increases both contributions and efficiency (group earnings as a share of the social optimum). When monitoring is expensive, the cost can outweigh the benefit, and efficiency does not improve or may decline. Allowing groups to endogenously select the level of monitoring does not increase contributions or efficiency at non-deterrent (low monitoring) levels, contrary to some previous literature. In fact, endogenous selection of non-deterrent institutions sometimes reduces contributions. The relationship between monitoring, punishment, and efficiency is thus strongly moderated by the costliness of institutional enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-enabled institutions on efficiency in public goods games, especially when the punishment is implemented via a central authority with costly monitoring. The results show that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on both the probability of monitoring (deterrence) and the cost of monitoring. At low and moderate costs, enabling punishment increases efficiency, but at high costs, the efficiency gain is negated or reversed. The endogenous selection of non-deterrent institutions does not provide an efficiency boost. For prediction, the key moderators are the cost of monitoring (punishment_cost), the effectiveness of monitoring (punishment_tech), and whether the institution is deterrent (i.e., the expected cost of free-riding exceeds the benefit). Control efficiency (without punishment) is much lower than with effective, affordable punishment, but high punishment costs can erase the efficiency gain.

- source: Dai, Z. X., Hogarth, R. M., & Villeval, M. C. (2015). Ambiguity on audits and cooperation in a public goods game. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with centralized sanctions triggered by audits, the study manipulates the frequency and regularity of audits (continuous vs. intermittent) and the severity of sanctions. Under ambiguity (unknown audit probability), intermittent audits with high fines (but lower frequency) produce similar or higher contributions and significantly higher efficiency (group payoffs) than continuous audits with low fines. After audits are withdrawn, groups exposed to intermittent audits maintain higher cooperation and efficiency for longer, as their beliefs about audit probability decay more slowly. High frequency-high severity (continuous audits with high fines) also sustains cooperation and efficiency, but at higher cost. Reducing audit frequency or sanction severity too much undermines these effects. The results suggest that, for a given expected cost of sanction, intermittent audits with high severity are more efficient than continuous audits with low severity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a 3-player, 50-round linear PGG with centralized punishment, intermittent (irregular) audit schemes with high severity (punishment effectiveness) and sufficient frequency lead to higher efficiency (group payoffs) than continuous audit schemes with lower severity, especially after audits are withdrawn. The effect is robust as long as audit frequency and severity are not too low. For prediction, this suggests that, holding control efficiency and other game dimensions constant, enabling punishment via intermittent, ambiguous audits with high severity will increase efficiency more than continuous, predictable audits with low severity. The paper also shows that both audit frequency and punishment effectiveness (severity) are key moderators: too few audits or too low severity reduces the efficiency benefit. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in PGG-like environments with centralized punishment.

- source: Hilbe, C., Traulsen, A., Röhl, T., & Milinski, M. (2014). Democratic decisions establish stable authorities that overcome the paradox of second-order punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that second-order punishment institutions (which punish both defectors and those who do not support the punishment institution) result in higher average payoffs and cooperation rates than institutions without second-order punishment. When given a choice, groups using majority voting overwhelmingly select the more efficient second-order punishment institution, while groups using individual migration (foot voting) initially avoid it, resulting in lower payoffs. Over time, some foot-voting groups shift toward the more efficient institution, but not as consistently as with majority voting. The findings highlight the importance of democratic decision-making in establishing efficient punishment institutions that sustain cooperation and maximize group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling second-order punishment in a public goods game increases efficiency (average payoff) compared to a control without such punishment, under otherwise identical game parameters. The effect is robust across both theoretical predictions and experimental results. The findings also show that the method of institutional choice (majority voting vs. individual migration) moderates the realized efficiency, with majority voting more reliably leading to the efficient outcome. For prediction tasks, the presence of second-order punishment, the method of institutional choice, and the group’s prior experience with institutional regimes are important moderators. The paper’s detailed reporting of payoffs under both control and treatment conditions makes it directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Dong, Y. L., Zhang, B. Y., & Tao, Y. (2016). The dynamics of human behavior in the public goods game with institutional incentives. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated four-player PGGs, institutional punishment and combined reward/punishment significantly increase average contributions and group payoffs compared to control and reward-only. The effect is not due to direct behavioral response to being punished or rewarded, but to a change in self-regarding preferences (b1). The group average payoff is strongly positively correlated with b1 (self-regarding preference) in punishment and combined conditions. The effect of varying the intensity of incentives (Up, Const, Down) is minimal. The presence of institutional punishment is the key driver of higher cooperation and payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated PGG with four players, 50 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and known MPCR, adding institutional punishment (with moderate cost and effectiveness) increases group payoffs and average contributions compared to control. The effect is robust to variations in punishment intensity. Reward alone does not significantly increase payoffs. For prediction, the presence of institutional punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially when combined with reward. The effect is mediated by changes in self-regarding preferences, not by direct response to being punished.

- source: Engel, C., & Zhurakhovska, L. (2017). You Are in Charge: Experimentally Testing the Motivating Power of Holding a Judicial Office. *Journal of Legal Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Authorities (randomly assigned, with no personal stake in group outcomes) are willing to incur costs to punish free riders in a repeated linear public goods game. This punishment leads to higher contributions and higher payoffs for active participants than the Nash equilibrium (no contribution, no punishment). The effect is robust to framing the authority as a public official or judge, which further reduces the influence of personal preferences on punishment. Announcing punishment policies, election of judges, and experience all modulate the targeting and sensitivity of punishment, but the basic effect—punishment increases cooperation and group payoffs—remains. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution, punishment), but group profit is reported and is higher than in the absence of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing centralized punishment (by a non-beneficiary authority) in a repeated linear public goods game increases group payoffs and efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust across variations in authority framing, policy announcement, election, and experience. The design dimensions (player count, number of rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contributions, no rewards, full information on others' actions and punishment) are all specified, making the results directly relevant for predicting efficiency in similar lab PGGs with centralized punishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency as a ratio to the social optimum, so the effect must be inferred from reported mean earnings and profit.

- source: Eisenberg, T., & Engel, C. (2014). Assuring Civil Damages Adequately Deter: A Public Good Experiment. *Journal of Empirical Legal Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that damages rules analogous to civil litigation (compensatory, class action, treble damages) can deter free riding and promote cooperation in a repeated public good game. The baseline (no punishment) shows rapid decay of contributions and group payoffs. Compensatory damages (limited to individual harm) slow but do not prevent decay. Class action damages (based on group harm) and treble damages, when sufficiently likely or severe, stabilize contributions and maintain higher group payoffs. The effect is not due to the theoretical threat of punishment, but to participants' actual experience of being punished. The option for the punisher to forfeit rather than retain damages (i.e., to punish without personal gain) is rarely used and does not affect outcomes. Thus, the deterrent effect of damages is robust to whether the punisher benefits personally. The findings suggest that for punishment to sustain cooperation and group welfare, it must be sufficiently severe and/or likely, and that actual experience with sanctions is more important than their mere possibility.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (damages) in a repeated public good game increases group efficiency (payoff) relative to the no-punishment baseline, especially when punishment is severe (treble damages) or based on group harm (class action) and is likely to be imposed. The effect is not simply on average contribution, but on the stability of cooperation and group payoffs over time. For prediction tasks, the presence and design of punishment (severity, scope, probability) are key moderators of efficiency gains. Control efficiency (baseline group payoff) is improved by adding punishment, but the magnitude depends on the punishment regime. The paper also shows that the actual experience of being punished is a stronger driver than the theoretical threat, suggesting that models should account for realized, not just expected, punishment. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially for settings with repeated interaction, group feedback, and endogenous punishment.

- source: Dal Bó, E., & Dal Bó, P. (2014). Do the right thing: The effects of moral suasion on cooperation. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a series of lab experiments using a two-player linear public goods game, the authors show that (1) moral suasion (exposure to a moral message) increases contributions and group payoffs, but the effect is short-lived when punishment is not available; (2) when a punishment stage is added (where players can pay to reduce their partner's payoff), the effect of moral suasion becomes persistent, with higher and sustained contributions and higher group payoffs; (3) punishment alone increases contributions compared to no-punishment, but does not prevent a decline over time; (4) the combination of punishment and moral suasion produces the highest and most persistent cooperation and payoffs; (5) the mechanisms include both changes in expectations about others and changes in preferences. The paper does not report efficiency as a ratio, but does report group payoffs and their changes, which are closely related.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player linear public goods game with random rematching, adding a punishment stage (with a cost to the punisher and a larger cost to the punished) increases group payoffs and sustains higher cooperation, especially when combined with moral suasion. The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive, and the effect is amplified when moral suasion is present. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contributions, etc.) are well specified, making the results directly relevant for predicting efficiency in similar lab PGGs with and without punishment. The paper is less informative about settings with more players, field contexts, or different punishment technologies, but is highly informative for standard lab PGGs.

- source: Barclay, P., & Raihani, N. (2016). Partner choice versus punishment in human Prisoner's Dilemmas. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across four studies, the paper finds that (1) punishers are rated as more trustworthy and group focused than nonpunishers; (2) after repeated PGG rounds, punishers are trusted more and receive more money in trust games, but this effect is absent after a single round; (3) justified punishment (punishing free riders) leads to increased trust and monetary benefits, while unjustified punishment leads to reduced trust and earnings; (4) the reputational and monetary benefits of justified punishment can offset or even exceed the cost of punishing, especially in repeated interactions. The paper does not directly report group efficiency ratios, but does show that punishment can increase individual and group monetary outcomes via increased trust and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated PGGs with punishment, justified punishment can increase the monetary benefits to punishers via enhanced trust and subsequent transfers, partially or fully offsetting the cost of punishment. The effect is contingent on repeated interaction (number of rounds) and the justification for punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in repeated PGGs (with visible identities and reputation) is likely to increase group efficiency and individual payoffs, especially when punishment is justified and recognized as such. In single-round games, these benefits do not materialize. The paper does not provide direct group efficiency ratios, but the monetary outcomes and trust game results are closely related and informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Muthukrishna, M., Francois, P., Pourahmadi, S., & Henrich, J. (2017). Corrupting cooperation and how anti-corruption strategies may backfire. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that allowing bribery in an institutional punishment public goods game (IPGG) substantially reduces public good provisioning (by about 25%). In the standard IPGG (control), stronger leaders (higher punishment multiplier) increase contributions and public good provisioning. However, when bribery is possible (bribery game, BG), stronger leaders actually decrease public good provisioning, as they use their power to extract bribes rather than enforce cooperation. Anti-corruption strategies (partial/full transparency, leader investment) can increase contributions in some contexts (strong leaders or high economic potential), but may have no effect or even reduce contributions further in contexts with weak leaders and poor economic potential. The main outcome is mean contribution to the public good, which is a close proxy for efficiency. The results show that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent and can be reversed or undermined by corruption opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the presence of corruption opportunities (e.g., bribery) can reverse the usual positive effect of institutional punishment on efficiency. The effect of punishment depends critically on whether leaders can be corrupted, the strength of the leader's punitive power, and the economic potential (mpcr). In standard IPGGs, stronger punishment increases efficiency, but in bribery-enabled games, stronger punishment can decrease efficiency. Anti-corruption interventions (transparency, leader investment) can help, but only under certain structural conditions. When using this paper for prediction, it is important to consider whether the game design allows for corruption/bribery, the strength of punishment, and the economic context. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not reported directly, but the relative changes are clear: punishment increases efficiency in standard IPGG, but decreases it when bribery is possible.

- source: Peysakhovich, A., & Rand, D. G. (2016). Habits of Virtue: Creating Norms of Cooperation and Defection in the Laboratory. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Exposure to environments that support cooperation (via repeated PDs with high continuation probability and favorable payoffs) leads to higher prosociality in subsequent one-shot games, including the public goods game, trust game, dictator game, and ultimatum game. In a second experiment, exposure to cooperative environments increases third-party punishment of selfishness and reduces antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment. These effects are strongest among subjects who rely on intuitive (heuristic) decision-making. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is not assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional environments can shape prosocial and punishment behaviors in public-goods-game-like settings, and that these behaviors spill over into new contexts. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation and punishment rates, not efficiency. The design details (e.g., group size, punishment cost/tech, MPCR) are reported and can inform mapping to other studies, but the lack of efficiency outcomes limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Irwin, K., Mulder, L., & Simpson, B. (2014). The Detrimental Effects of Sanctions on Intragroup Trust: Comparing Punishments and Rewards. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Both punishment and reward systems, when present, can increase short-term cooperation (especially when sanctions are moderate rather than extreme), but their removal leads to a significant decline in trust, and in some cases, a decline in cooperation. Strong sanctions do not increase cooperation above control, and their removal reduces cooperation. Weaker sanctions increase cooperation while present, but their removal does not immediately reduce cooperation, though trust is still undermined. The negative effects on trust are similar for both punishment and reward. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings must be interpreted through the lens of contribution/cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the presence and removal of punishment (and reward) systems can have complex effects on cooperation, which is a behavioral proxy for efficiency but not a direct measure. Strong punishment does not increase cooperation above control, and its removal reduces cooperation. Weaker punishment increases cooperation while present, but its removal does not immediately reduce cooperation. The undermining of trust by sanctions is robust, regardless of sanction strength. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on the strength of the sanction and the baseline level of cooperation, but direct efficiency outcomes are not reported. Use caution when extrapolating to efficiency, as only contribution rates are measured.

- source: Kumakawa, T. (2013). Social Rewards and Sanctions: A Voluntary Contribution Mechanism Experiment. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that in a two-player public goods game, the opportunity to send social (free-form) or monetary rewards/sanctions does not increase first-round contributions compared to a baseline with no such opportunity. However, when participants actually experience social sanctions (i.e., receive a negative message from their partner), their contributions in the next round increase significantly. Social rewards (positive messages) do not have a significant effect. Monetary sanctions and rewards are rarely used and do not significantly affect subsequent contributions. The study does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player public goods game with a game-theoretic payoff table and no prior knowledge of a second round, social sanctions (blame via free-form messages) can increase subsequent contributions, but only after they are actually experienced. The mere opportunity for sanctions or rewards does not affect cooperation. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for predicting behavioral (contribution) responses to social sanctions, not efficiency outcomes. The design (two players, two rounds, no chat except for the sanction/reward stage, full information about partner's contribution, and a linear MPCR of 0.7) should be considered when generalizing to other settings.

- source: Krieg, J., & Samek, A. (2017). When charities compete: A laboratory experiment with simultaneous public goods. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that monetary incentives (bonus) increase contributions to the treated public good and, in early rounds, to the untreated public good, resulting in a higher total sum of contributions (i.e., an expanded 'charitable pie'). Recognition and non-monetary sanctions do not significantly increase contributions in the treated game, and recognition leads to a decrease in contributions to the untreated game in later rounds, consistent with expenditure substitution. Sanctions are actively used but do not increase contributions. The findings suggest that monetary incentives are more effective than social incentives in increasing total contributions when multiple public goods are present. However, the study does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, so implications for efficiency must be inferred from contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effect of non-monetary sanctions (costless approval/disapproval) in a simultaneous public goods environment. In this design, punishment is costless and does not affect payoffs, and it does not increase contributions or the sum of contributions. Thus, in similar environments (simultaneous PGGs, costless non-monetary punishment, no chat, n=4, 20 rounds, MPCR=0.4), enabling this form of punishment is unlikely to increase efficiency or group payoff. The paper does not test costly punishment, so its results are most relevant for costless, non-monetary sanctions. The findings also suggest that the presence of alternative opportunities for prosocial behavior (multiple simultaneous PGGs) may reduce the effectiveness of social incentives. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that costless, non-monetary punishment will not improve efficiency in simultaneous PGGs with these parameters.

- source: Gürerk, Irlenbusch, B., & Rockenbach, B. (2014). On cooperation in open communities. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that when subjects can self-select into a punishment community (VF-PUN), average contributions are much higher than in a reward community (VF-REW) or in exogenously assigned punishment communities (EX-PUN, FIX-PUN). The effect is attributed to self-selection rather than slow growth of the community. The presence of a punishment option is critical for high cooperation, and adding reward does not further improve outcomes. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; all main results are in terms of contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that self-selection into punishment-enabled public goods games leads to higher cooperation (contribution) rates than exogenous assignment or reward-based institutions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher contributions likely imply higher efficiency, but actual efficiency or group payoff is not reported. The design details (player count, rounds, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, so the paper is useful for mapping design to expected cooperation, but not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Choi, J. K., & Ahn, T. K. (2013). Strategic reward and altruistic punishment support cooperation in a public goods game experiment. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab public goods game with 3 players, 10 rounds per phase, and a marginal per-capita return (MPCR) of 0.5, both voluntary punishment and voluntary reward options significantly increase average group contributions compared to baseline. In the partner condition (fixed groups), average contributions rise from 8.17 (punishment baseline) to 12.51 (punishment with punishment enabled), and from 9.38 (reward baseline) to 12.31 (reward with reward enabled). In the stranger condition (random rematching), punishment sustains higher contributions (11.38) than reward (9.21). The effect of punishment on cooperation is robust to group reshuffling, while reward is much less effective when strategic reciprocity is not possible. The paper does not directly report efficiency, but the increase in group contribution implies higher group payoff and thus higher efficiency when punishment is enabled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with 3 players, 10 rounds, no communication, and MPCR=0.5, enabling voluntary punishment increases group contribution (and thus group payoff/efficiency) compared to a baseline with no punishment. The effect is robust to random rematching (stranger condition), where punishment remains effective but reward does not. The paper does not report efficiency as a percentage of the social optimum, but the group contribution data can be used to estimate efficiency. For prediction, the findings suggest that, under these design parameters, enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control, especially when group membership is not fixed. The absence of direct efficiency reporting is a limitation, but the contribution data is a strong proxy.

- source: Joffily, M., Masclet, D., Noussair, C. N., & Villeval, M. C. (2014). Emotions, Sanctions, and Cooperation. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that allowing costly punishment in a repeated public goods game leads to a dramatic increase in average contributions, with groups converging to near-full cooperation. Emotional responses (measured by SCR and self-reported valence) are closely linked to both cooperation and punishment: positive emotions are associated with higher contributions, and negative emotions in response to free riding predict punishment behavior. Receiving punishment leads to negative emotions and increased subsequent contributions by free riders. The study provides strong evidence that punishment opportunities can sustain high cooperation, but the main outcomes are emotional and behavioral (contribution rates), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with four players, 20 rounds, and a punishment stage with convex costs and anonymous assignment, the introduction of punishment opportunities leads to a large increase in average contributions (from about 9.3 to 19.2 out of 20). While efficiency is not directly reported, the near-maximal contributions in the punishment condition imply that group efficiency approaches the social optimum. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, known MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no rewards, anonymous punishment, full feedback) are all clearly specified, making this study highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar lab PGG designs. However, the lack of direct efficiency or group payoff reporting is a limitation for precise quantitative prediction.

- source: Paál, T., & Bereczkei, T. (2015). Punishment as a Means of Competition: Implications for Strong Reciprocity Theory. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 10-round, 4-player PGG with a competitive, rank-based payoff and costly punishment enabled from round 6 onward, average contributions declined steadily and did not increase after punishment was introduced. Punishment was not targeted at low contributors but at those with higher accumulated profits, indicating strategic rather than norm-enforcing use. There was no association between contributions and punishment received or imposed. The results suggest that in competitive environments, punishment can become a tool for rivalry rather than for sustaining cooperation, and does not reverse the decline in cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in a PGG with a competitive, tournament-style payoff structure and full information about others' profits, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency or group payoff. Instead, punishment is used for strategic competition, and cooperation continues to decline. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent: in competitive, rank-based settings, punishment may not improve and may even undermine group efficiency. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so this inference is based on contribution behavior and the described use of punishment.

- source: Skatova, A., & Ferguson, E. (2013). Individual differences in behavioural inhibition explain free riding in public good games when punishment is expected but not implemented. *Behavioral and Brain Functions*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both the implementation and the expectation of punishment (even when not actually implemented) increase individual contributions in a one-shot PGG compared to standard PGGs without punishment. Implemented punishment leads to higher contributions than non-implemented punishment. Individual differences in BIS-anxiety (a measure of behavioral inhibition) predict higher contributions and less free riding when punishment is expected but not implemented. FFFS (flight, fight, freeze system) does not predict contributions. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution levels, free riding), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both implemented and expected (but not implemented) centralized punishment increase contributions in a one-shot PGG, with implemented punishment having a stronger effect. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes due to punishment. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in contribution rates under different punishment regimes and for understanding the role of individual differences in behavioral inhibition. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling punishment (even if not always implemented) increases cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains cannot be directly inferred from the reported outcomes.

- source: Villatoro, D., Andrighetto, G., Brandts, J., Nardin, L. G., Sabater-Mir, J., & Conte, R. (2014). The Norm-Signaling Effects of Group Punishment: Combining Agent-Based Simulation and Laboratory Experiments. *Social Science Computer Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when punishment is inflicted by the entire group, human subjects are more likely to cooperate (i.e., contribute to the public good) than when punishment is uncoordinated, even though the material cost to defectors is held constant. The authors attribute this to the norm-signaling function of group punishment, which is hypothesized to activate both instrumental and normative motivations for compliance. Agent-based simulations support the idea that norm salience, as modeled in the EMIL-I-A architecture, can explain the observed human behavior. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (contribution rates).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of punishment (specifically, whether it is coordinated by the group or uncoordinated) can significantly affect cooperation rates in public goods games, even when the material incentives are held constant. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper suggests that group punishment may lead to higher efficiency than uncoordinated punishment, but this is inferred from contribution rates rather than directly measured. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to calibrate quantitative predictions of efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of group punishment structure and norm-signaling on cooperation, which may be relevant for predicting efficiency when combined with other sources.

- source: Pfattheicher, S., Keller, J., & Knezevic, G. (2017). Sadism, the Intuitive System, and Antisocial Punishment in the Public Goods Game. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The studies show that activating the intuitive system increases antisocial punishment among individuals with high sadism, while inhibiting intuition reduces it. This effect is specific to antisocial punishment and not to punishment of defectors or to contribution behavior. The presence of punishment in the game enables antisocial punishment, but the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding the psychological moderators of antisocial punishment but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and cognitive moderators of antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment, but it does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is only indirectly informative: it confirms that antisocial punishment can occur in standard PGGs with punishment, and that its frequency is affected by individual differences and cognitive processing mode. However, it does not quantify the impact of punishment on efficiency or provide control vs. treatment efficiency data. The game design dimensions are well specified, so the paper can help confirm the mapping of design features, but not the efficiency effect of punishment.

- source: Bortolotti, S., Casari, M., & Pancotto, F. (2015). NORMS OF PUNISHMENT: EXPERIMENTS WITH STUDENTS AND THE GENERAL POPULATION. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The opportunity for peer punishment increases cooperation among students but not among the general population. In the student sample, average contributions rise substantially when punishment is available (from 6.8 to 12.8 out of 20), while in the representative sample, contributions are slightly lower with punishment (from 9.1 to 8.9). The general population punishes as much or more than students, but punishment is more often directed at cooperators (antisocial punishment), and recipients are less responsive to punishment. As a result, punishment fails to increase cooperation or efficiency in the general population. Earnings per period decrease with punishment in both groups, but more so in the representative sample. The study highlights that the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency is highly dependent on the subject pool, with punishment only improving cooperation (and likely efficiency) among students.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated PGGs depends critically on the subject pool. In a standard 4-player, 8-round PGG with MPCR=0.5 and a punishment technology of 1 token cost to reduce 4 tokens from another, punishment increases cooperation (and likely efficiency) among students but not among a representative general population. The main moderator is the prevalence of antisocial punishment and the responsiveness to punishment. For prediction, if the game is run with a student sample, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency; with a general population sample, punishment may not improve and could even reduce efficiency due to antisocial punishment and lack of response. The paper does not report efficiency directly, but the pattern of contributions and earnings suggests these conclusions.

- source: Nelissen, R. M. A., & Mulder, L. B. (2013). What makes a sanction stick? The effects of financial and social sanctions on norm compliance. *Social Influence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both financial and social sanctions increase cooperation (measured as average contribution) in a repeated public goods game compared to a no-sanction control. Financial sanctions produce higher contributions than social sanctions while the sanctioning opportunity is present. However, after the removal of sanctions, contributions drop much more in the financial sanction condition than in the social sanction or control conditions. Social sanctions thus create more persistent cooperation norms. The study does not report group payoff or efficiency, but the contribution results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency effects in similar game designs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both financial and social punishment mechanisms increase cooperation in a standard PGG, and that the effect is larger for financial punishment while the mechanism is active. However, the persistence of cooperation after punishment is removed is greater for social sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment will be positive for both financial and social punishment, but the durability of the effect may depend on the type of punishment. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff reporting means that predictions must be inferred from contribution rates, but the mapping is straightforward in standard PGGs. The paper's design and results are highly informative for settings with 4 players, continuous contributions, no communication, and known MPCR.

- source: Kubena, A. A., Houdek, P., Lindová, J., Príplatová, L., & Flegr, J. (2014). Justine Effect: Punishment of the Unduly Self-Sacrificing Cooperative Individuals. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a 12-player PGG, the introduction of punishment leads to a U-shaped relationship between contribution and punishment received: both the lowest and highest contributors are punished, with the most severe punishment falling on the highest contributors (the 'Justine Effect'). Antisocial punishment is primarily carried out by free riders and low contributors. While the paper reports that average contributions nearly double when punishment is available, it does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, nor does it compare these outcomes between the punishment and no-punishment conditions. The focus is on the behavioral dynamics of punishment, not on payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral dynamics of punishment in large-group PGGs, especially regarding antisocial punishment and its targets. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, which are central to the downstream prediction task. The finding that punishment increases average contributions suggests a possible positive effect on efficiency, but the presence of antisocial punishment (which reduces the payoffs of high contributors) could offset these gains. Without explicit efficiency or payoff data, the paper's value for predicting treatment efficiency is limited to indirect inference based on contribution patterns and punishment behavior.

- source: Schroeder, K. B., Nettle, D., & McElreath, R. (2015). Interactions between personality and institutions in cooperative behaviour in humans. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that the opportunity for decentralized punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions to the public good compared to a no-punishment baseline. This effect is especially pronounced for individuals high in extraversion, who tend to free-ride in the absence of punishment but increase their contributions when punishment is possible. Agreeableness is associated with higher initial contributions regardless of institution, while neuroticism is associated with lower contributions in both conditions. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the increase in contributions with punishment suggests a likely increase in efficiency, subject to the cost of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions, particularly among certain personality types (notably extraverts). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be quantified from this paper alone. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the punishment mechanism is standard (costly, decentralized, 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio). The findings support the general prediction that punishment increases cooperation, but do not allow direct prediction of efficiency outcomes for the downstream task.

- source: Bone, J., Silva, A. S., & Raihani, N. J. (2014). Defectors, not norm violators, are punished by third-parties. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment in a one-shot public goods game is overwhelmingly directed at defectors, regardless of whether defection is the norm in the group. The manipulation of norm violation (defection being typical or atypical) does not affect the likelihood of defectors being punished. Cooperators are rarely punished, even when they are in the minority. The cost structure of punishment is fixed, so punishing multiple players is not more expensive than punishing one. Social rejection patterns mirror punishment: defectors are less preferred as future partners, regardless of norm status. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot PGG with third-party punishment and a fixed punishment cost, punishment is targeted at defectors rather than norm violators. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design details (one-shot, third-party punishment, fixed cost, no communication, binary contribution) are informative for mapping to other studies, but the lack of payoff outcomes limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction. The findings suggest that, under these design features, punishment is likely to be used against defectors, but the impact on group efficiency remains unmeasured.

- source: Engel, C., & Kurschilgen, M. (2013). The Coevolution of Behavior and Normative Expectations: An Experiment. *American Law and Economics Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that (1) both norm elicitation and legal framing increase cooperation in a repeated public goods game, but do not differ significantly in the absence of punishment; (2) introducing a costly peer punishment option (1:1 cost-to-impact) increases cooperation and reduces free-riding, but the effect is strongest when the norm is embedded in a legal frame; (3) in the presence of punishment, the legal frame prevents the crowding out of intrinsic motivation and leads to higher contributions and more stable norm perceptions; (4) average earnings are higher in the Law-S (legal frame with sanctions) treatment than in other sanctioning treatments, indicating that punishment can improve group welfare when combined with legal framing. However, the paper does not directly analyze efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average earnings per treatment are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with 4 players, 30 rounds, and a 1:1 punishment cost-to-impact ratio, enabling punishment increases group earnings and cooperation, especially when the norm is legally framed. The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive and most pronounced when combined with a legal frame. The control (no-punishment) efficiency is lower than in the punishment-enabled treatments, particularly Law-S. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, but the magnitude depends on the presence of legal/normative framing. The results are most directly applicable to games with continuous contributions, no chat, full information about others' contributions, and a 1:1 punishment cost-to-impact ratio.

- source: Wu, J. J., Li, C., Zhang, B. Y., Cressman, R., & Tao, Y. (2014). The role of institutional incentives and the exemplar in promoting cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Institutional punishment increases average group contributions above control and reward-only treatments, and the combination of reward and punishment (IRP) is most effective at promoting cooperation. The effect is robust across three incentive schemes (fixed, increasing, decreasing). The study finds that individuals react more strongly to punishment than to reward, especially among high contributors. The presence of 'cooperating-individuals' (exemplars) in a group is associated with higher average contributions, as conformists tend to follow their lead. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff, but the design and behavioral findings are highly relevant for understanding how institutional punishment affects cooperation in repeated PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional punishment (and especially combined reward and punishment) increases average group contributions in repeated PGGs with four players and 50 rounds. While efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the contribution results suggest that punishment is likely to improve efficiency relative to control, especially when combined with reward. The experimental design and manipulation of incentive schemes are informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar lab PGG settings, but direct quantitative prediction of efficiency is not possible from this paper alone due to the lack of payoff/efficiency reporting.

- source: Hopfensitz, A., & Miquel-Florensa, J. (2017). Mill ownership and farmer's cooperative behavior: the case of Costa Rica coffee farmers. *Journal of Institutional Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that, in a public good game, Costa Rican coffee farmers selling to cooperatives do not contribute more than those selling to private mills when paired with their own group. However, cooperative farmers contribute significantly less when paired with private mill farmers, while private mill farmers' contributions are unaffected by partner background. When a punishment mechanism (penalizing lowest contributors, with partial redistribution to the group) is available, contributions by cooperative farmers increase substantially, especially when paired with private mill farmers (over 100% increase in contribution). The effect of punishment on private mill farmers is negligible. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff, but the structure of the game allows inference that group welfare would increase with higher contributions under punishment, especially for cooperative farmers in mixed groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism in a public good game can substantially increase contributions, especially among cooperative farmers interacting with out-group partners. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so the effect on efficiency must be inferred from the increase in contributions. The punishment mechanism is costly (1 unit per participant), and only a fraction of the fine is redistributed, so efficiency gains may be less than the increase in contributions. The design is a one-shot or single-decision public good game with 4 players, MPCR 0.375, and a punishment mechanism that is not peer-to-peer but an impartial rule penalizing the lowest contributor. The findings are most informative for predicting contribution changes (and thus likely efficiency changes) when punishment is enabled in similar settings, especially for groups with strong in-group/out-group distinctions. The lack of direct efficiency analysis is a limitation for precise prediction.

- source: Bruttel, L., & Friehe, T. (2014). Can short-term incentives induce long-lasting cooperation? Results from a public-goods experiment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment used a repeated linear public goods game with punishment, fixed groups of four, and 20 rounds. Treatments manipulated the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) and punishment cost for the first 10 rounds, then reverted to a common, less cooperative-friendly setting for rounds 11-20.
