High mpcr or low punishment cost increased contributions in rounds 1-10, but after reverting to baseline parameters, contributions in these groups declined rapidly, often below the control. The baseline (constant, moderate mpcr and punishment cost) showed stable contributions. The study finds no evidence that temporary strong incentives create persistent cooperation; instead, their removal can backfire. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear public goods game with punishment, temporary improvements in incentive structure (either via higher mpcr or lower punishment cost) do not produce lasting increases in cooperation once incentives revert to baseline. For the prediction task, this suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment (or from temporarily improving punishment effectiveness or mpcr) is not persistent if the incentive is not maintained. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so its value for predicting efficiency is indirect and must be inferred from contribution patterns. The results caution against expecting lasting efficiency improvements from short-term incentive changes.

- source: Weng, Q., & Carlsson, F. (2015). Cooperation in teams: The role of identity, punishment, and endowment distribution. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that (1) strong identity (via human knot) increases contribution rates in heterogeneous teams without punishment, but not in homogeneous teams; (2) strong identity (via online chat) increases contribution rates in homogeneous teams; (3) punishment increases contribution rates when punishment is effective (1:3), even under strong identity, but not when punishment is less effective (1:2) and identity is strong; (4) strong identity does not further enhance contributions or punishment when punishment is available; (5) punishment of deviations from the team average does not depend on the target's endowment. The main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (as measured by contribution rate) depends on the effectiveness of punishment and the strength of identity-building. When punishment is effective (punishment impact per unit cost is high), it increases contributions even under strong identity. When punishment is less effective and identity is strong, punishment does not increase contributions. However, the paper does not report group payoff or efficiency, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. The findings suggest that, for similar public good games, enabling effective punishment is likely to increase efficiency (by increasing contributions), but the effect may be moderated by identity-building and punishment effectiveness. The absence of direct efficiency data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Visser, M., & Burns, J. (2015). Inequality, social sanctions and cooperation within South African fishing communities. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase contribution rates in both equal and unequal endowment groups, with a larger effect in unequal groups. Low endowment players contribute a higher share of their endowment than high endowment players. Punishment is used less in unequal groups, but is more effective at raising contributions. Demand for punishment does not differ significantly between high and low endowment players, despite higher relative costs for low endowment players. Punishment is mainly targeted at free-riders, with antisocial punishment more common in equal groups. Socio-economic status and social attitudes (e.g., distrust in formal institutions) influence punishment behavior. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that peer punishment increases contribution rates in linear public goods games, especially in groups with unequal endowments. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, and the findings about contribution rates and punishment behavior are informative for predicting behavioral responses to punishment. For efficiency prediction, the paper suggests that punishment is likely to increase efficiency in similar settings, especially in unequal groups, but the magnitude cannot be inferred. The absence of payoff/efficiency data is a key limitation for direct prediction.

- source: Vollan, B., Landmann, A., Zhou, Y. X., Hu, B. L., & Herrmann-Pillath, C. (2017). Cooperation and authoritarian values: An experimental study in China. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, linear public goods game with a mild, non-deterrent punishment for under-contribution, exogenously imposed punishment rules (authoritarian/top-down) significantly increase average contributions compared to no law or endogenously chosen (democratic) punishment rules. This effect is strongest among participants (especially workers) with high deference to authority, and weaker or absent among those with low deference to authority (especially students). The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but since the game is linear and contributions are the main outcome, higher contributions imply higher efficiency. There is no evidence of a democracy premium in this Chinese sample; instead, authoritarian implementation is more effective at increasing cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear PGG with a mild, non-deterrent punishment, enabling punishment via exogenous (authoritarian) imposition increases average contributions (and thus efficiency) compared to no punishment. The effect is context-dependent: in this Chinese sample, exogenous punishment is more effective than democratic (endogenous) punishment, especially among those with high deference to authority. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on cultural context and the method of rule implementation (exogenous vs. endogenous). However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must be inferred from contribution rates.

- source: Hoeft, L., & Mill, W. (2017). An experimental investigation of designated punishment behavior in public goods. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated public goods game with a single designated punisher, the punisher contributes significantly less than non-punishers but enforces higher contributions from others through punishment. This double standard grows over time. The results are robust to a full-information treatment where all contributions are public. The paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it is unclear whether the presence of a designated punisher increases, decreases, or leaves unchanged the overall efficiency of the group.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral effects of designated punishment in public goods games, specifically that punishers can enforce higher contributions from others while themselves contributing less. However, because the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, information structure) are well specified and may be useful for matching to other studies, but the lack of efficiency or payoff data limits the direct predictive value for the downstream efficiency prediction task.

- source: Campos-Vazquez, R. M., & Mejia, L. A. (2016). Does corruption affect cooperation? A laboratory experiment. *Latin American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that: (1) exposure to a high-corruption environment (low monitoring) reduces subsequent cooperation in a public goods game; (2) allowing a leader to centrally punish increases cooperation (contributions) compared to no punishment, with a stronger effect in low-corruption groups; (3) allowing group members to counter-punish the leader reduces cooperation compared to leader-only punishment. All findings are based on contribution rates, not on efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment can foster cooperation, but its effectiveness is moderated by prior corruption exposure and is undermined by the threat of counter-punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that centralized punishment by a leader increases contribution rates in a standard linear public goods game, and that this effect is stronger in groups with less prior exposure to corruption. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, and the findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling centralized punishment on cooperation (contribution rates), but not directly on efficiency. The results also highlight that the presence of counter-punishment can reduce the positive effect of punishment on cooperation.

- source: Romaniuc, R., Farrow, K., Ibanez, L., & Marciano, A. (2016). The perils of government enforcement. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that peer disapproval (private enforcement) increases contributions to the public good compared to baseline. Adding a mild, centralized monetary punishment (government enforcement) to peer disapproval stabilizes contributions but does not significantly increase average contributions compared to peer disapproval alone. Critically, if government enforcement is removed after being in place, the effectiveness of peer disapproval is undermined: low contributors become unresponsive to disapproval, and average contributions drop significantly below the level observed when only private enforcement was ever present. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the results on contributions imply that punishment can have both stabilizing and crowding-out effects depending on the sequence of enforcement mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the sequence and combination of enforcement mechanisms (private and government) can have important effects on cooperation (as measured by contributions) in public goods games. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the findings suggest that enabling punishment can stabilize or increase contributions, but that removing punishment after it has been in place can lead to a sharp decline in cooperation, even below the level observed with private enforcement alone. For prediction tasks, this implies that the history of enforcement mechanisms and their removal are important moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bicskei, M., Lankau, M., & Bizer, K. (2016). Negative reciprocity and its relation to anger-like emotions in identity-homogeneous and -heterogeneous groups. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when peer punishment is available in a one-shot PGG, the likelihood and intensity of punishment for low contributions is significantly lower in identity-homogeneous (in-group) groups than in identity-heterogeneous (out-group) or random groups. Anger-like emotions (anger, contempt, irritation) are a strong driver of punishment, but their effect is moderated by group identity: they lead to more punishment in out-groups than in-groups. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but argues that higher punishment in out-groups, being costly, likely reduces social welfare relative to in-groups, where punishment is less frequent and less intense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on behavior in PGGs is moderated by group identity: punishment is less frequent and less intense in in-groups, and anger-like emotions drive punishment more in out-groups. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that in settings with identity-heterogeneous groups, punishment is more likely to be used, which (given the costliness of punishment) may reduce efficiency relative to in-groups. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the behavioral mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Engel, C., Beckenkamp, M., Glöckner, A., Irlenbusch, B., Hennig-Schmidt, H., Kube, S., Kurschilgen, M., Morell, A., Nicklisch, A., Normann, H. T., & Towfigh, E. (2014). First impressions are more important than early intervention: Qualifying broken windows theory in the lab. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated public goods games with and without punishment, the authors find that first impressions (i.e., the initial level of cooperation in the group) have a strong and lasting effect on subsequent contributions. The opportunity to punish freeriders (costly punishment) stabilizes cooperation and slows the decay of contributions over time, compared to games without punishment. However, the effect of early punishment (punishing in the first round) is small and fades over time, especially when punished players can retaliate. The main driver of sustained cooperation is good first impressions, with punishment playing a secondary, stabilizing role. The paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoffs, but higher contributions in the punishment condition imply higher efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in standard linear public goods games, enabling punishment increases and stabilizes contributions compared to no-punishment baselines, especially when initial group behavior is cooperative. While the paper does not report efficiency directly, the close link between contribution rate and efficiency in this design means the results are highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes. The effect of punishment is positive but depends on initial conditions; early punishment per se is less important than the overall opportunity for punishment. The findings are most applicable to 4-player, 10-round, continuous-contribution PGGs with standard punishment technology and no communication.

- source: Balliet, D., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Trust, Punishment, and Cooperation Across 18 Societies: A Meta-Analysis. *Perspectives on Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase cooperation in public goods games, with a moderate average effect (d=0.77, 95% CI [0.63, 0.91]), but with substantial cross-societal variation. The effect of punishment is significantly stronger in high-trust societies. Methodological moderators show that punishment is more effective in partner-matching protocols (fixed groups) and with more iterations (rounds), but not significantly affected by group size. Other societal variables (GDP, democracy, market competition, religious participation, norms of civic cooperation) have weaker or non-significant effects after controlling for trust. The outcome measured is behavioral cooperation/contribution, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the standardized Fehr & Gächter public goods game, enabling peer punishment increases cooperation rates, especially in high-trust societies. However, the outcome is contribution/cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible from this paper alone. The findings suggest that, for lab PGGs with 3-7 players, 6-50 rounds, and standard costly peer punishment (1 MU cost to reduce 3 MUs), punishment increases cooperation, with larger effects in partner-matching and longer games. The effect on efficiency is likely positive if higher cooperation translates to higher group payoffs, but this is not directly measured. The paper is highly informative for predicting behavioral cooperation under different game designs and societal contexts, but less so for direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bell, A., Zhang, W., & Nou, K. (2016). Pesticide use and cooperative management of natural enemy habitat in a framed field experiment. *Agricultural Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment uses a one-shot public goods game with three treatments: (1) Base (no punishment), (2) UCPun (conditional decision maker can be punished by an unconditional decision maker), and (3) CPun (conditional decision maker can punish an unconditional decision maker). The threat of punishment (UCPun) increases the slope of conditional cooperation but reduces the probability of being a conditional cooperator (more free riders). The power to punish (CPun) does not reduce conditional cooperation (no substitution effect on cooperation), but does lead to more sensitive punishment behavior. Responsibility effects (increased cooperation when given punishment power) are stronger than substitution effects. The overall effect of punishment on cooperation is positive, but the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment mechanisms affect conditional cooperation in a one-shot public goods game with four players, MPCR 0.4, and costless punishment. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that enabling punishment increases the responsiveness of cooperators to others' contributions and may increase overall cooperation, but also increases free riding. For prediction tasks, the results imply that punishment is likely to increase cooperation-related behaviors, but the net effect on efficiency is ambiguous due to the rise in free riders. The absence of direct efficiency analysis limits the ability to quantitatively predict treatment efficiency from control efficiency, but the behavioral mechanisms identified are informative for modeling.

- source: Cheung, S. L. (2014). New insights into conditional cooperation and punishment from a strategy method experiment. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both conditional cooperation and punishment in a public goods game are sensitive to the full distribution of others' contributions, not just the average. When punishment is available, more subjects contribute and a substantial fraction are willing to punish, with punishment increasing as the target's contribution falls below the punisher's and as the third player's contribution rises. However, the study does not directly report or analyze group efficiency, welfare, or total earnings as a function of punishment availability. The main findings are about the behavioral patterns of contribution and punishment, and the heterogeneity of types (conditional cooperators, free-riders, others).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment affects contribution and punishment behavior in a one-shot, three-player public goods game with a strategy method. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does support the qualitative expectation that punishment increases contributions and that the effect depends on the distribution of others' contributions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is informative about mechanisms and behavioral responses, but not about the magnitude of efficiency changes.

- source: Nolan, J. M. (2017). Environmental Policies Can Buttress Conservation Norms. *Society & Natural Resources*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a lab public goods game, introducing formal sanctions (either market-based or regulatory) increases contributions in the round where sanctions are present, and increases informal sanctioning (internal, indirect, and some direct measures) toward defectors. However, the effect on contribution does not persist after sanctions are removed. The study does not report group payoff, efficiency, or total earnings, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. The field study supports the finding that formal sanctioning systems are associated with stronger informal sanctioning norms, but again does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing punishment (formal sanctions) in a standard linear public goods game increases contributions while the sanction is present, and increases informal sanctioning. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions (player count, MPCR, punishment cost, etc.) are partially specified and can be inferred for the lab experiment, but the main outcomes are non-payoff behavioral measures. The paper is useful for understanding the psychological and social norm effects of punishment, but not for predicting efficiency changes.

- source: Baldassarri, D. (2015). Cooperative Networks: Altruism, Group Solidarity, Reciprocity, and Sanctioning in Ugandan Producer Organizations. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both communication and the threat of sanctioning increase contributions in a repeated PGG compared to baseline. Communication (reciprocity) has a stronger effect than punishment. However, only cooperation in the communication condition predicts real-life cooperative behaviors in the farmer organizations. The threat of sanctioning increases contributions in the lab, but this does not translate to real-world cooperation among council members. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in small, cohesive groups with repeated interaction, communication (reciprocity) is a more effective and relevant mechanism for promoting cooperation than punishment. While punishment increases contributions in the lab PGG, it does not predict real-world cooperative behavior in this context. The absence of efficiency or payoff data means the paper cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it suggests that in similar settings, punishment may not translate into real-world cooperative gains. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that communication/reciprocity is a key moderator, and that punishment's effect may be context-dependent and less robust in small, high-trust groups.

- source: Seip, E. C., Van Dijk, W. W., & Rotteveel, M. (2014). Anger motivates costly punishment of unfair behavior. *Motivation and Emotion*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across three laboratory experiments, the paper finds that (1) participants assign more costly punishment to less cooperative (unfair) players in a PGG, (2) anger mediates the relationship between perceived unfairness and punishment in both PGG and STG settings, and (3) experimentally induced anger increases costly punishment of unfair players in a subsequent STG. The studies do not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment is motivated by anger in PGG and STG settings, and that non-cooperation reliably triggers costly punishment. However, it does not provide any data or analysis on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, which are the primary outcomes for the downstream prediction task. Therefore, while the paper supports the behavioral mechanism by which punishment is enacted, it does not inform whether punishment increases or decreases efficiency under any game design dimension. The design details (e.g., player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech) are reported and may be useful for mapping to other studies, but no efficiency outcomes are available.

- source: Kamei, K. (2014). Conditional punishment. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that individuals' punishment of non-cooperators increases with the amount of punishment assigned by other group members. Most subjects are 'free-riders' who do not punish, but about 23% are conditional punishers who increase punishment as others do. There is also a distinction between pro-social and anti-social conditional punishers. The study provides evidence that conditional punishment types are heterogeneous and that these types can predict unconditional punishment behavior. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed behavioral evidence on the heterogeneity and conditionality of punishment in a standard PGG with punishment, but does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and types of punishment behavior that may underlie efficiency effects in similar games, but cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, suggesting that the presence of conditional punishers may amplify punishment effects when others also punish, but the net effect on efficiency is not measured.

- source: Stagnaro, M. N., Arechar, A. A., & Rand, D. G. (2017). From good institutions to generous citizens: Top-down incentives to cooperate promote subsequent prosociality but not norm enforcement. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of a centralized punishment institution in a repeated PGG (with 3 players, 10 rounds, and varying inspection probabilities) leads to a substantial increase in contribution rates compared to a no-punishment control. This institutional exposure also increases subsequent prosociality in a Dictator Game, but does not directly increase punishment behavior in a Third-Party Punishment Game. The effect on punishment is only indirect, mediated by increased prosociality. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a centralized punishment institution in a repeated PGG increases contribution rates, which would likely increase efficiency, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly. The design dimensions (player count, number of rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, and the manipulation of punishment is clear. However, for the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with and without punishment), this paper only provides indirect support via contribution rates. It does not allow direct estimation of efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that even minimal punishment is sufficient to substantially increase cooperation, and that further increases in punishment probability do not yield additional gains in contribution. The absence of direct efficiency data is a limitation for prediction.

- source: Dugar, S. (2013). NON-MONETARY INCENTIVES AND OPPORTUNISTIC BEHAVIOR: EVIDENCE FROM A LABORATORY PUBLIC GOOD GAME. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with groups of four, the introduction of non-monetary, costless disapproval points (social sanctions) after each round leads to significantly higher group contributions and group earnings (efficiency) compared to a baseline with no such mechanism. Approval points (social rewards) alone have a small, weakly significant effect on efficiency. The combination of approval and disapproval points produces the highest efficiency, with group earnings and contributions both significantly higher than in any other treatment. The effect is robust over 15 rounds, though some decay is observed. The study demonstrates that even costless, payoff-irrelevant social sanctions can substantially improve group welfare in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a costless, non-monetary punishment mechanism (disapproval points) in a standard linear public goods game increases efficiency (group earnings) relative to a baseline with no such mechanism. The effect is larger than that of a costless reward mechanism (approval points), and the combination of both is most effective. Because the punishment is costless and does not reduce group payoffs, all efficiency gains are realized. For prediction tasks, this suggests that introducing a non-monetary, costless punishment mechanism in a repeated, anonymous, four-player, 15-round linear PGG with MPCR=0.4 will increase efficiency substantially over the control. The effect is likely to be even larger if both approval and disapproval are available. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with costless, anonymous, non-monetary sanctions, and may not generalize to costly or monetary punishment.

- source: Feinberg, M., Willer, R., & Schultz, M. (2014). Gossip and Ostracism Promote Cooperation in Groups. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that in a repeated public goods game with random rematching, the ability to gossip about others' contributions and to ostracize (exclude) group members based on this information leads to a substantial increase in cooperation and, after several rounds, to the highest group and individual earnings. Gossip alone also increases cooperation and earnings compared to a basic game with no reputation or exclusion, but the effect is less pronounced and decays over time. Ostracism is used primarily against low contributors, and those who are ostracized tend to increase their contributions upon returning. The efficiency gains from gossip and ostracism are not immediate, as exclusion temporarily reduces group size and the public good multiplier, but by the final rounds, these groups outperform the others in earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a punishment-like mechanism (ostracism) in addition to reputation/gossip in a public goods game increases efficiency (group and individual earnings) compared to both a basic game and a gossip-only game. The effect is robust despite a temporary efficiency loss due to exclusion. The design dimensions—such as group size (4), number of rounds (6 per game), no chat, continuous contributions, MPCR of 0.5 (or 0.375 when group size drops to 3), and anonymous exclusion—are all specified, making this study highly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment (ostracism) in similar lab PGGs. The results suggest that, even when punishment is implemented as exclusion rather than direct costly punishment, and even when exclusion temporarily reduces group size and multiplier, the long-run effect on efficiency is positive if reputation information is available.

- source: Peeters, R., & Vorsatz, M. (2013). IMMATERIAL REWARDS AND SANCTIONS IN A VOLUNTARY CONTRIBUTION EXPERIMENT. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing costless, immaterial feedback (smilies/frownies) does not significantly increase average group contributions or payoffs in a voluntary contribution public goods game. Under partner matching, feedback treatments show higher variance in group contributions, with some groups sustaining high contributions, but the average effect is not significant. Under stranger matching, feedback treatments do not outperform control and may even reduce contributions. The effect of feedback on future contributions is context-dependent: under partner matching, sending/receiving emoticons can reduce future contributions; under stranger matching, receiving a frowny can increase future contributions for some participants. Overall, immaterial feedback does not reliably improve efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costless, immaterial punishment or reward mechanisms (such as emoticons) do not significantly improve efficiency or group payoff in standard public goods games, regardless of matching protocol. For prediction tasks, the presence of immaterial, non-costly, non-monetary punishment should not be expected to increase efficiency relative to control. The effect of such feedback is highly variable and context-dependent, with no robust positive effect on group payoff or efficiency. The findings suggest that, unless punishment or reward is material (i.e., has real payoff consequences), enabling such mechanisms is unlikely to improve efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Hugh-Jones, D., & Perroni, C. (2017). The logic of costly punishment reversed: Expropriation of free-riders and outsiders. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the threat of group expropriation (a form of punishment) can increase public goods contributions, but only when the expropriation mechanism is sufficiently difficult to coordinate (i.e., requires a large group to act together). When expropriation is too easy (requires only one or two people), it is less targeted at free-riders, more arbitrary, and leads to lower contributions and more frequent expropriation, reducing efficiency. In the baseline (punishment enabled, M=3), contributions are significantly higher than in the control (no expropriation), and expropriation is mostly targeted at unique lowest contributors. When the minimum group size for expropriation is reduced (M=2 or M=1), expropriation increases and contributions fall. In heterogeneous groups, minorities are more likely to be targeted in some treatments, especially when ambiguity or a history of targeting exists, and may respond less to punishment. However, in the main treatments, overall group efficiency is not significantly different between homogeneous and heterogeneous groups. The findings suggest that the design of the punishment mechanism (especially the ease of coordination required for punishment) is a critical moderator of its effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends on the punishment technology, specifically the minimum group size required for successful punishment (expropriation). When punishment is difficult to coordinate (higher M), it is more likely to be used as a credible threat against free-riders, increasing contributions and efficiency. When punishment is too easy (low M), it is used more arbitrarily, contributions fall, and efficiency is reduced. For prediction, the dimension 'punishment_tech' (minimum group size for punishment, or ease of expropriation) is a key moderator: higher coordination requirements for punishment (higher M) are associated with more positive effects of punishment on efficiency, while lower requirements (lower M) can make punishment detrimental. Control efficiency (without punishment) is much lower than with well-targeted, hard-to-coordinate punishment. Heterogeneity (minority status) can moderate targeting and behavioral response, but does not robustly reduce efficiency unless ambiguity or a history of targeting is present. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially punishment_tech, and provide indirect evidence for the importance of targeting and coordination in punishment mechanisms.

- source: Galbiati, R., & Vertova, P. (2014). How laws affect behavior: Obligations, incentives and cooperative behavior. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Obligations (minimum required contributions) significantly increase average contributions to the public good, even in the absence of incentives. Non-binding incentives (probabilistic, low-stakes rewards/punishments) alone do not affect contributions, but when combined with obligations, they substantially increase contributions beyond the effect of obligations alone. The effect of obligations and incentives is complementary. The mechanisms are traced to changes in beliefs about others' contributions and in individuals' willingness to cooperate. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the design implies that higher contributions increase group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot linear public good game with 6 players and no communication, introducing a minimum contribution requirement (obligation) increases average contributions, and adding even weak, non-binding incentives (probabilistic punishment/reward) further increases contributions. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the structure of the game means that higher contributions correspond to higher efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of obligations and (even weak) punishment/reward mechanisms can be expected to increase efficiency relative to a baseline with neither, especially when both are present. However, the absence of direct efficiency data means that quantitative predictions must be inferred from the reported contribution rates.

- source: Gangadharan, L., Nikiforakis, N., & Villeval, M. C. (2017). Normative conflict and the limits of self-governance in heterogeneous populations. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Allowing communication and rewards in a public goods game leads to near-maximal efficiency in homogeneous groups, but only partial efficiency in heterogeneous groups where returns from the public good differ. In homogeneous groups, communication increases group earnings to 94% of the maximum, while in heterogeneous groups, the increase is only 80% of the maximum. The main reason is that most heterogeneous groups prioritize equality or compromise between equality and efficiency, rather than maximizing group earnings. Rewards are not systematically used to offset inequality. The loss in efficiency in heterogeneous groups is due both to lower contributions and to under-use of rewards. The study demonstrates that normative conflict can limit the effectiveness of self-governance mechanisms in promoting efficiency in heterogeneous populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling communication and rewards in public goods games depend critically on group homogeneity. In homogeneous groups, such mechanisms can achieve near-maximal efficiency. In heterogeneous groups, even with communication and rewards, efficiency gains are much smaller due to normative conflict between equality and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of heterogeneity in returns (mpcr differences) is a strong moderator that limits the efficiency benefit of enabling communication and rewards. The absence of punishment means the results are most directly applicable to reward-based or communication-based interventions, but the findings on normative conflict are likely relevant for predicting the limits of punishment as well, especially in heterogeneous settings.

- source: Ozono, H., Kamijo, Y., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Institutionalize Reciprocity to Overcome the Public Goods Provision Problem. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated four-person public goods game, embedding a reciprocal strategy as an institution (institutionalized reciprocity, IR) leads to significantly higher group efficiency (total profit) than a standard control game. The effect is strongest when the institution enforces a strict upper bound on contributions (S-IR), which enables reciprocators to coordinate against free riders and prevents excessive cooperation. In S-IR, group profits are significantly higher than in both the control and a less restrictive IR variant (NL-IR). The IR mechanism operates as a collective punishment system: if any player fails to meet the target, the group's future benefit is reduced for all. The study does not implement standard peer punishment, but the IR mechanism provides a collective sanction that is effective at increasing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutionalized, rule-based collective punishment (via reduction of future group benefit after a deviation) can substantially increase efficiency in repeated public goods games, especially when the institution strictly limits contributions to a target. While the study does not test standard peer punishment, it shows that collective, institutionally enforced sanctions can outperform standard repeated games without punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that introducing a credible, enforced institutional sanction (even if not peer punishment) can raise efficiency well above control, particularly when the rules are clear and binding. The results are most directly applicable to games with four players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions (in increments), and full feedback on others' contributions and payoffs.

- source: Charness, G., & Yang, C. L. (2014). Starting small toward voluntary formation of efficient large groups in public goods provision. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that endogenous group formation with exit, exclusion, and merger options produces very high contribution rates and group efficiency in public goods games. In the main treatment, where the marginal per-capita return (MPCR) increases with group size, groups tend to grow large and stable, and efficiency is maximized. When the efficiency gain from larger groups is capped, groups remain smaller and less stable, and efficiency is lower, but still substantially higher than in fixed-group baselines. Exclusion (a form of sanction) is used to remove low contributors, which protects cooperators and sustains high efficiency. Redemption (the ability for excluded individuals to rejoin) further boosts efficiency. The results suggest that voluntary group formation with exclusion and merger mechanisms can solve the free-rider problem and achieve high efficiency in public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games, mechanisms allowing voluntary group formation, exit, exclusion (as a sanction), and merger can dramatically increase efficiency compared to fixed groups. The effect is especially strong when economies of scale are present (i.e., when MPCR increases with group size). The exclusion mechanism functions as a sanction, but is not a direct costly punishment; rather, it is a group-level sanction that removes low contributors. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of exclusion/exit/merger mechanisms and increasing MPCR with group size are strong positive moderators of the efficiency effect of sanction-like mechanisms. However, the effect is not directly about costly punishment, but about exclusion as a sanction. The results are less informative for standard costly punishment, but highly informative for exclusion-based sanctions and endogenous group formation. Control efficiency (fixed group) is much lower than in the endogenous group treatments.

- source: Otto, P. E., & Bolle, F. (2016). The advantage of hierarchy: Inducing responsibility and selecting ability?. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares three regimes: standard PGG (voluntary contributions), AG (randomly assigned allocator determines all contributions), and PAG (principal selects allocator each round). Both AG and PAG significantly increase group production and average incomes relative to PGG. PAG (principal selection) yields the highest efficiency, with further increases in satisfaction and fairness ratings. The effect is not due to learning or order effects. The mechanism works by centralizing responsibility and leveraging selection, not by explicit punishment. In treatments with heterogeneous productivity, the most productive worker is more likely to be appointed allocator, but does not exploit the position to reduce own effort. The findings show that hierarchy can substitute for punishment in sustaining high efficiency in public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing hierarchical control (an allocator or principal-allocator structure) in a public goods game can substantially increase efficiency, even in the absence of explicit punishment mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the presence of a central authority who can determine or influence contributions should be expected to yield efficiency gains similar to or greater than those typically attributed to punishment. The results are robust across group size (n=3), number of rounds (10 per regime), and both homogeneous and heterogeneous productivity. The findings suggest that, in environments where punishment is not available but hierarchy is, efficiency can be high. For prediction, if a game design includes a mechanism where one player can set others' contributions (with or without selection by a principal), expect efficiency to be much higher than in standard PGG, even if punishment is absent.

- source: Nosenzo, D., & Tufano, F. (2017). The effect of voluntary participation on cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares three main treatments: Baseline (forced participation), Unconditional (voluntary participation before knowing partner's contribution), and Conditional (voluntary participation after seeing partner's contribution). In Baseline and Unconditional, average contributions and efficiency are nearly identical (about 22% of endowment contributed; efficiency ~22%). In Conditional, average contributions and efficiency are much higher (47% of endowment; efficiency up to 81.9% among those who agree to play, and 46.6% overall). The positive effect of voluntary participation on efficiency is only observed when participation can be used as a threat against free-riding. There is little evidence that unconditional voluntary participation leads to assortative matching of cooperators, even when the outside option is increased. The main mechanism for increased efficiency is the threat of non-participation disciplining free-riders, not selection. However, frequent withdrawals can reduce realized efficiency gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-person one-shot PGG, enabling voluntary participation only increases efficiency if players can condition their participation on knowledge of the partner's intended contribution (i.e., if non-participation is a credible threat against free-riding). Unconditional voluntary participation (without information) does not improve efficiency, even with a higher outside option. For prediction, the presence of a conditional participation mechanism (a form of endogenous partner exclusion or exit threat) is a strong moderator of punishment-like effects on efficiency, even in the absence of explicit costly punishment. The results suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling conditional exit can substitute for explicit punishment in raising efficiency, but unconditional exit does not. Control efficiency (forced participation) is a good predictor of efficiency in unconditional exit games, but not in conditional exit games, where efficiency is much higher.

- source: Brick, K., Visser, M., & Van der Hoven, Z. (2016). Cooperation and Climate Change: Can Communication Facilitate the Provision of Public Goods in Heterogeneous Settings?. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Communication (chat) between group members in a heterogeneous public good game increases average contributions and the likelihood of meeting a collective target, but does not fully solve the free-rider problem. Even with communication, many groups fail to reach the target, and free-riding remains common, especially among high marginal cost players. The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so no direct evidence is provided on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff outcomes. The paper suggests, based on prior literature, that punishment opportunities may be necessary to ensure compliance and improve efficiency, especially in heterogeneous settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of communication (chat) in a heterogeneous public good game, but does not provide any experimental evidence on punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is not directly informative, as no punishment treatment is implemented. However, it does provide a useful control case: in a 4-player, one-shot, linear PGG with MPCR=0.42, no punishment, and no feedback on others' contributions, average group contributions are well below the social optimum, and communication increases but does not maximize efficiency. The paper's discussion and literature review suggest that punishment is likely to further increase cooperation and efficiency, but this is not tested here. Thus, this paper is best used as a control or baseline for settings without punishment.

- source: Riedel, N., & Schildberg-Hörisch, H. (2013). Asymmetric obligations. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that both high and low minimum contribution obligations (even when only weakly enforced) increase contributions in the initial periods compared to no obligation, but this effect is not sustained in the long run. Asymmetric obligations are as effective as symmetric ones: for a given own obligation, the partner's obligation does not affect behavior. The effect of obligations is interpreted as arising from an emotional cost of disobeying, not from expectations about others' behavior. No direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that weakly enforced minimum contribution obligations can temporarily increase contributions in public goods games, regardless of whether obligations are symmetric or asymmetric. However, since the study does not include a standard punishment institution (where players can assign costly punishment to others), and the 'punishment' here is a very weak, probabilistic, externally imposed fine for not meeting a minimum, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The findings suggest that expressive law (weak obligations) can have a short-term effect on cooperation, but do not inform about the efficiency effects of endogenous peer punishment. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of a standard punishment treatment limits direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency under peer punishment.

- source: Greiff, M., & Paetzel, F. (2015). INCOMPLETE INFORMATION STRENGTHENS THE EFFECTIVENESS OF SOCIAL APPROVAL. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enabling subjects to express social approval (assigning 0-10 stars to each group member after observing contributions) significantly increases contributions to the public good compared to a control with no approval. This effect is stronger when subjects have only partial information (see only others' contributions) than when they have full information (see both contributions and endowments). The study isolates the consumption value of social approval by using stranger matching and anonymity, ruling out reputation effects. No direct measures of efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings are reported; all main results are in terms of contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that non-monetary, symbolic social approval can increase contributions in public good games, but the effect is sensitive to the information structure. However, because the study does not include monetary punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff, it is only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is otherwise closely matched to standard PGGs, so the behavioral findings may be relevant for understanding how non-monetary incentives (like approval) interact with information, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes from punishment interventions.

- source: Akpalu, W., Abidoye, B., Muchapondwa, E., & Simbanegavi, W. (2017). Public disclosure for carbon abatement: African decision-makers in a PROPER public good experiment. *Climate and Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that public disclosure of individual pollution levels (via visible color-coded cards) reduces pollution (i.e., increases cooperation) in a linear public good game. The effect persists even after disclosure is withdrawn. The study does not use monetary punishment or reward, but rather a reputational/shame mechanism. The main outcome is the number of red cards retained (pollution), not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that public disclosure can be an effective non-monetary sanction to promote cooperation in public goods settings, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that public disclosure (a reputational/shame-based sanction) can reduce free-riding in a linear public good game, even among experienced policy-makers. However, it does not test monetary punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on the effect of monetary punishment on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that non-monetary sanctions can increase cooperation, but does not quantify efficiency gains or compare to monetary punishment. The game design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, known MPCR) are well described, but the absence of monetary punishment means the results are not directly transferable to settings with explicit punishment mechanisms.

- source: Peysakhovich, A., Nowak, M. A., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Humans display a 'cooperative phenotype' that is domain general and temporally stable. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that individuals' cooperative behavior is consistent across different cooperation games (including the PGG, Dictator Game, and Trust Game) and over time, supporting the existence of a 'cooperative phenotype.' However, this cooperative tendency does not extend to norm-enforcing punishment games (Ultimatum Game, 2PP, 3PP), as there is little to no correlation between cooperation and punishment behaviors. The study also finds that cooperation in games predicts real-world helping and generalized trust, but not self-reported cooperative behaviors. The research is correlational and does not examine the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in PGGs or related games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as it does not manipulate the presence or absence of punishment in PGGs, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that cooperative and punishment behaviors are distinct at the individual level in one-shot games, suggesting that the presence of punishment may not be strongly linked to underlying cooperative tendencies. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers little direct guidance on how punishment affects efficiency, but it does caution against assuming that those who cooperate are also those who punish, or that punishment and cooperation are tightly coupled in one-shot settings.

- source: Gneezy, U., Leibbrandt, A., & List, J. A. (2016). ODE TO THE SEA: WORKPLACE ORGANIZATIONS AND NORMS OF COOPERATION. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Fishermen in group-based (sea) societies exhibit higher trust, trustworthiness, pro-sociality, and cooperation in economic games—including the public goods game—than those in individual-based (lake) societies. These differences are robust to controls for selection and other confounds. The study provides evidence that workplace organization (group vs. individual work) is a key driver of cooperative norms. However, the public goods game does not include punishment or reward options, and the main outcomes are behavioral (contributions, trust, etc.), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the effect of group-based workplace organization on cooperation and contribution behavior in public goods games, but it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff outcomes. It does not manipulate or include punishment, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in public goods games. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR) are reported and can be extracted, but the absence of punishment means this paper is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency with punishment enabled.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Hendriks, A., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2016). Think global, act local: Preserving the global commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when participants can observe their neighbors' contributions to the public good and can condition their pairwise cooperation (in a PD) on this information, contributions to the public good are sustained at high levels, even in very large groups (up to 1,000 participants). In the control condition, where such information is not available, contributions decline over time. The mechanism is not direct punishment but rather the withholding of cooperation in a separate pairwise game, which acts as a social sanction. The effect is robust to group size and does not require participants to know the overall level of public good provision. The main outcome is sustained contribution, not measured efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms allowing for targeted social sanctions (withholding cooperation in a pairwise PD) based on observed public good contributions can sustain high contribution rates in large groups. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to punishment (withholding cooperation as a sanction) but does not involve costly punishment or explicit payoff reduction. The findings suggest that enabling targeted, observable social sanctions—even if not costly—can have a strong positive effect on contribution rates, which may translate to higher efficiency in similar designs, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Greiff, M., & Paetzel, F. (2016). Second-order beliefs in reputation systems with endogenous evaluations - an experimental study. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a repeated two-player public good game, providing information about both one's own and the partner's evaluation (reputation) increases contributions compared to control and to the treatment where only the partner's evaluation is shown. When only the partner's evaluation is available, contributions do not increase significantly relative to control. The effect is explained by the role of second-order beliefs in conditional cooperation. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but higher contributions in the BOTH-INFO treatment imply higher group payoffs. There is no punishment or costly sanctioning; evaluations are costless, non-binding signals.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of reputation-based information (not costly punishment) on contribution rates in repeated two-player public good games. It suggests that simply providing information about others' evaluations is insufficient to increase cooperation, but providing both own and partner's evaluation can increase contributions. However, because the study does not include a costly punishment mechanism, its direct relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for settings where social image or reputation feedback (not material punishment) is the main intervention. For prediction tasks involving costly punishment, this paper provides only adjacent evidence.

- source: Reigstad, A. G., Stromland, E. A., & Tinghög, G. (2017). Extending the Cooperative Phenotype: Assessing the Stability of Cooperation across Countries. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that individuals' cooperative behavior is positively correlated across different economic games (Public Goods Game, Dictator Game, Prisoner's Dilemma) and is stable over time and across countries (Norway, Sweden, Austria, United States). Punishment behavior (measured in a Dictator Game with Punishment) is not correlated with prosociality in other games. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate punishment in a PGG context, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that punishment behavior and cooperation are uncorrelated at the individual level, suggesting that the presence of punishment may not be strongly linked to prosociality. However, it does not inform the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms in PGG-like environments.

- source: Tan, L. J., & Wei, L. J. (2014). VOLUNTARY CONTRIBUTION MECHANISM PLAYED OVER AN INFINITE HORIZON. *Pacific Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that, in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, average contributions in infinitely repeated VCM games do not monotonically decline as in finitely repeated games. Instead, they show non-monotonic patterns, often sustained by the presence of 'contribution makers' who lead reciprocators to increase their contributions. There is a strong restart effect in finitely repeated games but not in infinitely repeated games. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but notes that groups with contribution makers achieve higher average contributions and, by implication, higher payoffs than groups without them.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide evidence on baseline contribution dynamics in standard VCMs with continuous contribution choices, fixed groups of 5, MPCR of 0.5, and no communication or punishment. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency (proxied by contribution rates) is sustained at moderate levels in infinitely repeated games, especially when 'contribution makers' are present. For prediction tasks, this paper is best used as a control/baseline for VCMs without punishment, not for estimating the effect of punishment.

- source: Aimone, J. A., Iannaccone, L. R., Makowsky, M. D., & Rubin, J. (2013). Endogenous Group Formation via Unproductive Costs. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing subjects to self-select into groups with lower private returns (sacrifice) leads to higher contributions and, crucially, higher group and individual payoffs in high-sacrifice groups compared to no-sacrifice groups. The mechanism works by screening out free-riders and encouraging conditional cooperators to join and contribute more. Infiltration by free-riders is limited, so high-sacrifice groups do not suffer reduced payoffs. When group assignment is random (control), the effect is much weaker. The paper does not study punishment or sanctions, but provides strong evidence that endogenous sorting via unproductive costs can increase efficiency in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public goods games with endogenous group formation and self-imposed unproductive costs, but it does not inform about punishment or sanction mechanisms. If a game design allows for endogenous sorting into groups with different private returns (sacrifice), expect higher efficiency in high-sacrifice groups due to screening and substitution effects. However, the findings do not transfer to settings where punishment is the treatment of interest, as no punishment or sanctioning is present in the design. The control (random assignment) condition shows that endogenous sorting is key to the efficiency gains.

- source: Keuschnigg, M., & Schikora, J. (2014). The dark side of leadership: An experiment on religious heterogeneity and cooperation in India. *Journal of Socio-economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that religious heterogeneity does not reduce efficiency or contributions in a standard simultaneous PGG. However, introducing a leader (sequential move, 'leading by example') reduces both contributions and efficiency, especially in religiously mixed groups. In the control (no leader) treatment, net profit is about 58-60% of endowment in both pure and mixed groups. In the leadership treatment, net profit drops to 52.5% in pure groups and 39.4% in mixed groups. The negative effect of leadership is driven by a lack of reciprocity in mixed groups: followers do not respond to leader contributions, and leaders anticipate this, leading to lower contributions overall. Thus, the introduction of a hierarchical structure (leadership) in heterogeneous groups can be detrimental to efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning, introducing a sequential move structure (leadership) in a linear PGG can reduce efficiency, especially in culturally heterogeneous (religiously mixed) groups. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling a leadership/first-mover mechanism (without punishment) is likely to decrease efficiency relative to the simultaneous-move baseline, particularly when group heterogeneity is high. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the only institutional change is the introduction of a leader, not punishment or reward. The results do not inform about punishment effects, as no punishment or sanctioning is present.

- source: Bouma, J., & Ansink, E. (2013). The role of legitimacy perceptions in self-restricted resource use: A framed field experiment. *Forest Policy and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that label framing (contextualizing the game as protected area management) does not significantly reduce extractions (increase efficiency) on average. However, for participants who trust enforcement officers or feel their community can influence park management, framing leads to lower extractions (higher efficiency). The effect is not significant for the sample as a whole, but is significant for these subgroups. The explanatory power of legitimacy indicators is low, and the sample size is small. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or reward mechanisms are present. However, it does show that contextual framing and legitimacy perceptions can moderate extraction (and thus efficiency) in PGG-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that institutional context and perceived legitimacy may interact with other design features to affect efficiency, but it does not inform the effect of adding punishment.
