It is not directly useful for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, but may be informative for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment, especially in field or framed settings.

- source: van Soest, D., Stoop, J., & Vyrastekova, J. (2016). Toward a delineation of the circumstances in which cooperation can be sustained in environmental and resource problems. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game (VCM), average net group contributions are positive and significant, leading to substantial group payoffs and efficiency. In contrast, when the action space is extended to allow negative contributions (claims/taking from the public fund), average net group contributions are not significantly different from zero, and group payoffs are much lower. This holds even for subjects who initially display prosocial behavior. The ability to undo others' contributions destroys cooperation and efficiency, and the effect is not explained by framing or subject pool differences. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the action space—specifically, whether players can undo others' contributions—has a dramatic effect on efficiency in public goods games. In standard VCMs (with only non-negative contributions), efficiency is much higher than in Claim Games (where negative contributions are allowed). However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, establish a strong baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is high in standard VCMs and very low in Claim Games. For prediction tasks, this paper helps set expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency under different action space designs, but does not inform the effect of punishment.

- source: Fosgaard, T. R., & Piovesan, M. (2015). Nudge for (the Public) Good: How Defaults Can Affect Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that while most subjects do not simply accept the default contribution values, the presence of a default (either free rider or perfect conditional cooperator) significantly influences their stated cooperation strategies and actual contributions in a subsequent repeated public goods game. Specifically, exposure to a cooperative default increases later contributions, while a free rider default decreases them. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning is present. It does, however, show that non-binding defaults can shift contribution behavior, which may indirectly inform models of cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.

- source: Cone, J., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Time Pressure Increases Cooperation in Competitively Framed Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that time pressure increases cooperation (measured as contribution to the public good) in a competitively framed public goods game, but not in the standard cooperative frame. The effect is robust across two datasets (the authors' and a prior study). The results support the Social Heuristics Hypothesis, suggesting that intuitive responses favor cooperation, especially in novel or less familiar contexts. However, the study does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding moderators of cooperation (such as time pressure and framing), but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design dimensions are well specified, but the absence of punishment and lack of payoff-based outcomes mean this paper should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Vermeer, A. B. L., Heerema, R. L., & Sanfey, A. G. (2016). Third-Party Cooperation: How Reducing Material Involvement Enhances Contributions to the Public Good. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that reducing personal material involvement in a public goods game increases cooperation rates: participants contribute more when making decisions for a third party than for themselves. This effect is further enhanced when the group consists of human players rather than computer players, suggesting that social motives drive third-party cooperation. The main outcome is the proportion of tokens contributed, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how personal involvement and social context affect cooperation rates, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. The design and results may be contextually informative for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Bigoni, M., Bortolotti, S., Casari, M., Gambetta, D., & Pancotto, F. (2016). Amoral Familism, Social Capital, or Trust? The Behavioural Foundations of the Italian North-South Divide. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds a persistent and significant North-South divide in cooperation in both the public good game and the trust game, with higher cooperation in the North. This gap is not explained by differences in risk aversion, concerns for equality or efficiency, or standard proxies for social capital. The experimental design uses a standard PGG (N=4, 8 rounds, MPCR=0.5, no punishment or reward, no communication, continuous contribution choices from a discrete set, full feedback on others' contributions and earnings). The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution rates, trust frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that regional differences in social norms and expectations, rather than incentives or individual preferences, underlie the observed cooperation gap.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on baseline cooperation rates in a standard PGG with no punishment or reward, using a representative sample and a well-described design. It is directly informative for predicting control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar PGGs, especially in the Italian context. However, it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatment, so it provides no direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. It is not informative for predicting the efficiency change when punishment is enabled, except as a control benchmark. The detailed reporting of game parameters (player count, rounds, MPCR, feedback, no chat, etc.) makes it useful for mapping design dimensions, but not for the core prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., El Mouden, C., & West, S. A. (2017). Social learning and the demise of costly cooperation in humans. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that providing social information about other groups' behaviors or payoffs does not increase cooperation in repeated public goods games; in most cases, it leads to a significant decline in cooperation. The decline is strongest when individuals can observe the behavior and payoffs of the most successful individuals, supporting the idea of payoff-biased or prestige-biased social learning. There is no evidence for conformity bias (copying common behaviors) or group-prestige bias (copying successful groups). The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design. Its findings are relevant for understanding how social learning and information about others' behavior/payoff affect cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as not providing evidence about punishment effects, but may be informative about baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment.

- source: Lamba, S. (2014). Social learning in cooperative dilemmas. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individuals do not show a clear tendency to conform to the modal contribution or to copy the highest earner's contribution in public goods games. There is significant individual and village-level variation in social learning strategies, and these are influenced by individual and contextual factors. Overall, contributions decreased in the second round, and there is no evidence that social learning increases cooperation. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present in the experimental design. The findings are relevant for understanding baseline behavior in the absence of punishment, especially regarding the role of social learning, but do not inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may help characterize control conditions (no punishment, no reward, no communication), but does not provide treatment effect estimates or moderators for punishment.

- source: Park, S. A., Jeong, S., & Jeong, J. (2013). TV programs that denounce unfair advantage impact women's sensitivity to defection in the public goods game. *Social Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a 10-round, 4-player, binary (all-or-nothing) threshold PGG, women are more cooperative than men in early rounds, but this difference disappears after both genders watch investigative TV programs about free-riding. After media exposure, women become more likely to free-ride in response to others' defection, adopting a tit-for-tat strategy. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and EEG measures; no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported. There is no punishment or reward mechanism in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It does, however, provide detailed information about a binary, threshold PGG with 4 players and 10 rounds, and shows how external context (media exposure) can modulate cooperation rates, especially among women. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, but it does confirm that in the absence of punishment, cooperation rates (and thus likely efficiency) can be sensitive to social context and gender. The absence of punishment and the focus on behavioral (not payoff) outcomes limit its direct relevance.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., El Mouden, C., & West, S. A. (2016). Conditional cooperation and confusion in public-goods experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a standard linear public-goods game (4 players, 1.6 multiplier, no punishment or reward), most participants do not understand that the income-maximizing strategy is to contribute nothing, regardless of others' actions. Many participants classified as 'conditional cooperators' are actually confused, not prosocial. The same behavioral patterns are observed when playing with computers (where cooperation cannot benefit others) as with humans. There is no evidence that a subpopulation of 'comprehenders' (those who understand the game) are more cooperative with humans than with computers. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides no direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatment. However, it is highly informative about baseline (control) PGGs without punishment: it shows that observed cooperation in such games may be largely due to misunderstanding, not prosocial preferences. For prediction tasks, this suggests that control group efficiency in standard PGGs may overestimate the true baseline level of prosociality, especially if participants misunderstand the payoff structure. The paper does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency, nor does it provide evidence on moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., & West, S. A. (2013). Prosocial preferences do not explain human cooperation in public-goods games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that (1) cooperation levels in public-goods games are not explained by prosocial preferences, as participants contribute similarly even when unaware their actions affect others; (2) providing enhanced information about others' earnings reduces cooperation; (3) even when cooperation is profitable, participants do not reach full cooperation. The main outcome is contribution level, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. However, it does provide high-quality evidence on how information and framing affect cooperation rates in standard and variant PGGs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does reinforce the importance of information structure and participant understanding as moderators of cooperation, which may interact with punishment in other studies.

- source: Horita, Y., Takezawa, M., Inukai, K., Kita, T., & Masuda, N. (2017). Reinforcement learning accounts for moody conditional cooperation behavior: experimental results. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that moody conditional cooperation (MCC) patterns are present in both repeated multiplayer PDG and PGG, though the specific form differs from previous literature. In the PGG, conditional cooperation is observed in both fixed and mixed group treatments. Reinforcement learning models (Bush-Mosteller and Roth-Erev) fit the observed behavioral data as well as or better than CC and MCC models, especially when direct reciprocity is absent. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It is useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics and the explanatory power of behavioral models in repeated PGGs with and without stable group composition. For the prediction task, this paper may inform expectations about baseline (control) cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but provides no evidence about the effect of punishment or moderators of punishment efficacy.

- source: Rand, D. G., Peysakhovich, A., Kraft-Todd, G. T., Newman, G. E., Wurzbacher, O., Nowak, M. A., & Greene, J. D. (2014). Social heuristics shape intuitive cooperation. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across 15 studies, time pressure (intuitive decision-making) increases cooperation in one-shot public goods games, but this effect is moderated by prior experience with similar experiments. Naïve subjects show a significant increase in cooperation under time pressure, while experienced subjects do not. The effect of time pressure on cooperation decayed over time as the subject pool (especially on MTurk) became more experienced. The outcome is always the fraction contributed (or not extracted), not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanction mechanisms are present. However, it provides strong evidence that cooperation rates (and thus, by implication, group payoffs) can be increased by promoting intuitive decision-making, especially among naïve subjects. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight the importance of subject experience and cognitive framing as moderators of cooperation. It also provides detailed information about standard PGG design parameters in the absence of punishment.

- source: Chaudhuri, A., Paichayontvijit, T., & Smith, A. (2017). Belief heterogeneity and contributions decay among conditional cooperators in public goods games. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that belief heterogeneity among conditional cooperators explains persistent differences in contribution levels and the observed decay in contributions over time. In treatments without feedback, contributions remain stable and reflect initial beliefs. When feedback is provided, optimists reduce their contributions more than pessimists increase theirs, leading to overall decay. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the mechanisms behind contribution decay but do not directly inform the effects of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. However, it does provide detailed evidence on how information feedback and belief heterogeneity affect contribution dynamics. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline contribution decay in the absence of punishment.

- source: Oprea, R., Charness, G., & Friedman, D. (2014). Continuous time and communication in a public-goods experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a challenging public-goods environment (MPCR = 0.3, 4 players), continuous time by itself does not significantly increase contributions compared to discrete time. However, when free-form chat is allowed, continuous time enables groups to coordinate and sustain full contributions, with median rates at 100%. Discrete time with chat also increases contributions, but less so and with more heterogeneity. Limited communication (pre-programmed messages) does not improve contributions over no communication. The findings suggest that the combination of continuous time and rich communication is critical for achieving high cooperation, but the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms means the results are specific to communication as a coordination device.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward, enabling free-form communication (chat) in a public-goods game with continuous time can dramatically increase contribution rates, which likely translates to higher efficiency. However, without communication, continuous time alone does not improve efficiency much over discrete time. The results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in games with and without chat, and with continuous versus discrete time, but do not inform about punishment effects. The findings are less relevant for settings where punishment or reward is available, as those mechanisms are not present in this study.

- source: Gong, X., & Sanfey, A. G. (2017). Social rank and social cooperation: Impact of social comparison processes on cooperative decision-making. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that social comparison, operationalized as being assigned a high or low rank in a prior task, significantly affects subsequent contribution decisions in a standard public goods game. High rank leads to increased contributions, low rank to decreased contributions, compared to a baseline with no ranking. These effects are robust to whether the PGG group is the same as the ranking group, and are more pronounced in individuals with high social comparison orientation. The experiment does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are limited to how social comparison affects contribution behavior. While the game design (standard PGG) is relevant, the absence of punishment means it cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The paper may be useful for understanding baseline contribution behavior or the impact of social comparison, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Lotz, S. (2015). Spontaneous Giving under Structural Inequality: Intuition Promotes Cooperation in Asymmetric Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that priming participants to use intuition (vs. reflection) increases their contributions to the public good in a one-shot, four-player PGG with asymmetric MPCRs (structural inequality). The effect of intuition is robust to controls for age, gender, comprehension, and paragraph length. There is no significant main effect of MPCR (high vs. low) on cooperation, nor an interaction between intuition and MPCR. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant as a baseline for understanding cooperation rates in asymmetric, one-shot PGGs without punishment or reward. The findings may inform the expected control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar asymmetric games, but cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on contribution rates (not payoffs) limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., Nax, H. H., & West, S. A. (2015). Payoff-based learning explains the decline in cooperation in public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in repeated public goods games, participants' contribution behavior is best explained by payoff-based learning: individuals adjust their contributions in response to changes in their own payoffs, rather than out of concern for others' payoffs or by matching others' contributions. Pro-social learning is not supported and, if anything, participants appear to weight others' payoffs negatively. Conditional cooperation is only weakly supported and disappears when the consequences of cooperation are made clearer. The decline in cooperation over time is thus attributed to individuals learning to maximize their own income, not to social preferences or conditional cooperation. The paper does not study punishment or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. It is useful for understanding baseline dynamics of contribution decline in repeated public goods games without punishment, showing that such decline is best explained by payoff-based learning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper can inform the expected control (no-punishment) efficiency trajectory, but provides no evidence on how punishment would alter efficiency. It does, however, reinforce that in the absence of punishment, efficiency is likely to decline over time due to learning, not social preferences.

- source: Cárdenas, J. C., Janssen, M. A., Ale, M., Bastakoti, R., Bernal, A., Chalermphol, J., Gong, Y. Z., Shin, H., Shivakoti, G., Wang, Y. B., & Anderies, J. M. (2017). Fragility of the provision of local public goods to private and collective risks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when the returns from the public good are risky (collective risk), participants invest less in the public good compared to when only private returns are risky or when there is no risk. Communities more integrated with the market (higher share of crops sold externally, more members with outside income) show lower cooperation, especially under collective risk. Risk aversion is associated with higher cooperation in the baseline, but not under risk treatments. The main outcome is the rate of investment in the public good; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. It does provide evidence on how risk and market integration affect cooperation rates in public good games, but not on efficiency or payoff outcomes, and not on the effect of punishment. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is not directly relevant.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Metcalfe, R., & Powdthavee, N. (2015). Can priming cooperation increase public good contributions?. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that priming participants with cooperative concepts (via a word search) leads to significantly higher contributions in a one-shot public goods game compared to a neutrally primed control group. The effect is robust to controls for gender, nationality, and field of study, and does not appear to be mediated by beliefs about others' contributions. The main outcome is contribution level; average earnings are reported but not analyzed in detail. No punishment, reward, or sanction mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. Its findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with or without punishment. However, it does provide evidence that subtle environmental interventions (like priming) can increase contributions in a standard linear PGG, which may be contextually relevant for understanding other non-incentive-based interventions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not informative about punishment effects.

- source: Martinsson, P., Villegas-Palacio, C., & Wollbrant, C. (2015). Cooperation and social classes: evidence from Colombia. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that both high and medium-low social class groups contribute about 40% of their endowment in a one-shot public goods game, with no significant difference in unconditional contributions or beliefs about others' contributions. The distribution of contributor types (conditional cooperators, free riders, etc.) is also similar between groups. However, medium-low social class members are more likely to contribute when others contribute nothing (i.e., higher baseline conditional cooperation). The study does not include punishment, reward, or repeated interaction, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide a clear description of a standard one-shot linear public goods game with four players, continuous contributions, and MPCR of 0.4, which may be useful as a control or baseline for comparison with studies that do include punishment. The findings on contribution behavior by social class may inform expectations about baseline cooperation in similar environments, but cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Rand, D. G., Kraft-Todd, G., & Gruber, J. (2015). The Collective Benefits of Feeling Good and Letting Go: Positive Emotion and (dis)Inhibition Interact to Predict Cooperative Behavior. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across two large-sample studies using one-shot PGGs (and some continuous PDs), the presence of positive emotion in participants' narratives predicts higher contributions, while the presence of inhibition predicts lower contributions. There is a significant interaction: the positive effect of emotion is strongest when inhibition is absent. These effects are robust to controls for negative emotion and demographic variables. The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or any efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or analyze any game design dimensions related to punishment, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. Its findings on psychological predictors of cooperation may be of indirect interest for understanding baseline cooperation rates in one-shot games, but are not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Martinsson, P., Nam, P. K., & Villegas-Palacio, C. (2013). Conditional cooperation and disclosure in developing countries. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that public disclosure of individual contributions in a one-shot public goods game leads to a small, statistically insignificant increase in average unconditional contributions in both Colombia and Vietnam. The distribution of contributor types (free-riders, conditional cooperators, etc.) is largely stable across disclosure and no-disclosure treatments, with only minor shifts. The effect of disclosure is much smaller than the effects typically found for monetary punishment in the literature. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. It does, however, provide evidence that public disclosure (a form of social image incentive) has only a small and statistically insignificant effect on average contributions in highly collectivist developing country contexts. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about the efficiency effects of punishment, but it does suggest that non-monetary social incentives (like disclosure) may have limited impact on contributions and, by extension, on efficiency in similar settings.

- source: Barr, A., Packard, T., & Serra, D. (2014). Participatory accountability and collective action: Experimental evidence from Albania. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is a robust positive correlation between cooperative behavior in a one-shot, binary public goods game and participation in both school-level and national accountability systems. At both the individual and district level, higher rates of cooperation in the game are associated with higher rates of participation in parent representative elections and higher voter turnout. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in the public goods game, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or discuss punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are limited to the relationship between cooperative tendencies and real-world participation in collective action. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods game environments.

- source: Nishi, A., Christakis, N. A., & Rand, D. G. (2017). Cooperation, decision time, and culture: Online experiments with American and Indian participants. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that American participants are more likely to cooperate than Indian participants in a repeated public goods game, both in the first round and in subsequent rounds, regardless of the social environment. Decision time for cooperation versus defection varies by culture: Americans decide faster when cooperating (except in non-cooperative environments), while Indians decide faster when defecting. The experiment does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any related payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or efficiency outcomes are present. It does, however, provide contextual evidence about baseline cooperation rates in repeated public goods games with different cultural groups, which may be useful for understanding baseline control conditions in similar designs. However, it cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment or any moderators of that effect.

- source: Lierl, M. (2016). Social sanctions and informal accountability: Evidence from a laboratory experiment. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment studies a public goods game with two beneficiaries and one provider, where the provider can embezzle contributions. The main manipulation is the structure of costly, mutual sanctioning (punishment and reward) relationships: (1) no sanctioning, (2) only among beneficiaries, (3) between provider and one or both beneficiaries, and (4) combinations thereof. The key finding is that mutual sanctioning among beneficiaries increases public goods provision and efficiency compared to no-sanctioning control, but this effect disappears or reverses when the provider is also part of the sanctioning network. The highest efficiency is observed when only beneficiaries can sanction each other. When both beneficiaries and the provider can mutually sanction, efficiency is not improved and can be lower than control. Actual punishment/reward is rare; the effect is mainly anticipatory. The results challenge the idea that denser or more encompassing sanctioning networks always improve efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the network structure of sanctioning relationships. If only beneficiaries can punish each other, enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to control. If the provider can also punish or be punished, the effect is reduced or negative. For prediction, the presence and direction of punishment effects should be conditioned on who can punish whom. The game is a close variant of a standard PGG, with the addition of a third-party provider who can embezzle, so results are most directly applicable to co-production or agency settings. Control efficiency (no-sanction) is reported, and treatment efficiency (with punishment) varies by network structure. The findings suggest that simply enabling punishment is not always efficiency-improving; the details of the punishment network matter.

- source: Bravo, G., & Squazzoni, F. (2013). Exit, Punishment and Rewards in Commons Dilemmas: An Experimental Study. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a modified public goods game with six players per group and ten rounds per treatment, the introduction of punishment (a 5 MU fine for non-cooperation) significantly increased cooperation and efficiency compared to the control (no incentives). However, the highest efficiency (profit as a proportion of the theoretical optimum) was achieved when both voluntary participation (exit option) and positive incentives (5 MU reward for cooperation) were present. Punishment alone (No-Neg) led to 67% of the optimum, while reward plus exit (Ex-Pos) led to 69%. The control (No-Null) and punishment plus exit (Ex-Neg) both yielded 58%. Thus, while punishment increases efficiency over the control, it is less effective than the reward-plus-exit combination. The paper also finds that exit alone has little effect, and that the interaction between exit and incentives is important for maximizing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with six players, ten rounds, no communication, and fixed institutional punishment (5 MU fine per defection), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control (no punishment), but the efficiency gain is smaller than that achieved by combining rewards and voluntary participation. The efficiency effect of punishment is positive but not maximal. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, fixed MPCR, fixed punishment cost/tech, no reward in punishment-only treatments, no identification of punishers, and full information about group outcomes) are all specified, making this paper highly informative for predicting efficiency under similar conditions.

- source: Dekel, S., Fischer, S., & Zultan, R. (2017). Potential Pareto Public Goods. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a linear public goods game with two majority players (who benefit from the public good) and one minority player (who is harmed by the public good), punishment institutions do not increase contributions or efficiency. Punishment is used ineffectively, with minority players sometimes punishing contributors, and majority players reluctant to punish free riders. As a result, efficiency is significantly lower in the punishment treatments compared to the baseline. Communication does not improve outcomes in the punishment condition. In contrast, when a reward institution is available and communication is allowed, majority players compensate the harmed minority, which increases majority contributions and efficiency. The presence of a harmed minority undermines the usual positive effects of punishment and communication on cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in public goods games with a harmed minority, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency and may even reduce it, relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of communication. The game design features—three players, 30 rounds, continuous contributions, known group composition, and a high punishment impact (3:1 ratio)—do not rescue the effectiveness of punishment in this context. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar settings (especially with harmed minorities), enabling punishment is likely to have a neutral or negative effect on efficiency, even if control efficiency is moderate. The paper also shows that reward institutions, especially with communication, can increase efficiency by enabling compensation.

- source: van Miltenburg, N., Przepiorka, W., & Buskens, V. (2017). Consensual punishment does not promote cooperation in the six-person prisoner's dilemma game with noisy public monitoring. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three punishment institutions (individual decision rule, CDR2, CDR3) in a six-person prisoner's dilemma, with and without 20% noise in public monitoring of contributions. In the absence of noise, all punishment institutions increase cooperation and earnings relative to the baseline, with collective decision rules (especially CDR3) performing best. With noise, however, all punishment institutions result in lower earnings than the baseline (no-punishment) condition. The individual decision rule (IDR) produces slightly higher cooperation than collective rules under noise, but still does not improve earnings. The main reason is that noise leads to both mistaken punishment of cooperators and missed punishment of defectors, so resources are wasted and cooperation is not effectively enforced. Thus, punishment does not promote efficiency in noisy environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a six-person all-or-nothing prisoner's dilemma with 20% noise in public monitoring, enabling punishment (regardless of whether it is individual or requires consensus) reduces group efficiency compared to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is robust across different punishment decision rules. The findings suggest that, when noise is present, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be lower than the control efficiency. The results are directly relevant for settings with similar group size, binary contribution, and noisy monitoring, and less so for linear PGGs or settings with perfect information.

- source: Abbink, K., Gangadharan, L., Handfield, T., & Thrasher, J. (2017). Peer punishment promotes enforcement of bad social norms. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a linear public goods game with 4 players and either zero (MPCR=0.25) or negative (MPCR=0.2) social returns to contribution, the availability of peer punishment increases contributions. However, in the negative-return condition, this leads to lower group earnings, i.e., punishment enforces a destructive norm. Survey and coordination game evidence show that subjects perceive non-contribution as socially inappropriate, even when it is efficient. Punishment stabilizes these inefficient norms. Thus, punishment does not always improve efficiency; its effect depends on the underlying norm and the payoff structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends critically on the MPCR and the prevailing social norm. In standard PGGs with MPCR > 1/n, punishment can increase efficiency by promoting cooperation. However, when MPCR < 1/n (as here, with MPCR=0.2 and n=4), punishment increases contributions but reduces efficiency, as contributions are socially harmful. For prediction, this means that the sign of punishment's effect on efficiency is not always positive: it can be negative in games where cooperation is inefficient. The paper also provides clear parameter values for player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, and information structure, making it highly informative for prediction tasks.

- source: Javaid, A., & Falk, T. (2015). Incorporating local institutions in irrigation experiments: evidence from rural communities in Pakistan. *Ecology and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implemented a sequential common-pool resource (CPR) game with five players per group, modeling irrigation dilemmas with asymmetric access. In the baseline (no institutional intervention), groups achieved high efficiency (average group earnings at 74% of the social optimum, group investment at 98% of the optimum). When external punishment (costly, probabilistic fines) was introduced, group earnings and efficiency significantly decreased (regression coefficient for group earnings: -9.03, p<0.1). The punishment mechanism reduced over-extraction by head-users but did not increase tail-user earnings; instead, it led to more water being wasted and lower group welfare. Communication and traditional authority treatments did not significantly affect group earnings or investment. The high baseline efficiency is attributed to strong local norms and the public revelation of individual extraction decisions. The main effect of punishment was to create additional coordination problems, not to improve efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments with high baseline efficiency and strong social information (public feedback on individual actions), introducing an external punishment mechanism can reduce group efficiency and welfare. The punishment treatment in this design (costly, probabilistic, not directly linked to rule violations) led to more resource wastage and lower group earnings. For prediction tasks, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is already high and social information is available, enabling punishment may decrease efficiency. The effect is specific to the punishment design used here (costly, probabilistic, not directly rewarding the punisher). Other institutional interventions (communication, traditional authority) had no significant effect. The findings are most applicable to sequential CPR games with asymmetric access, public feedback, and close-knit communities.

- source: Kosfeld, M., & Rustagi, D. (2015). Leader Punishment and Cooperation in Groups: Experimental Field Evidence from Commons Management in Ethiopia. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This study uses a field experiment with real-world leaders of Ethiopian forest user groups, combining a third-party punishment public goods game (TPPG) with observational data on group performance in commons management. Leaders are classified by their punishment motives: non-punishers, equality-driven, equality-and-efficiency-driven, and antisocial. The main finding is that groups led by equality-and-efficiency-driven punishers achieve substantially higher efficiency (as measured by young trees per hectare) than those led by non-punishers, while groups led by antisocial punishers perform significantly worse. Equality-only punishers have no significant effect in the first assessment but a positive effect in the second. The effect sizes are large relative to the mean outcome. The results are robust to a wide range of controls and alternative specifications. The study highlights that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not uniform: it depends on the leader's punishment motive and implementation.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect is not universally positive or negative, but depends on the nature of punishment. When punishment is targeted at free riders (motivated by equality and/or efficiency), efficiency increases. When punishment is antisocial or indiscriminate, efficiency decreases. The experimental design (two-player linear PGG with third-party punishment, MPCR=0.75, punishment cost=1, punishment impact=3, no chat, continuous contributions) is close to standard PGGs, but the real-world outcome is a field measure of group welfare. The findings suggest that the presence of punishment can increase or decrease efficiency depending on the implementation and motives, and that heterogeneity in punishment type is a key moderator. For prediction, the effect of enabling punishment should be conditioned on whether punishment is likely to be prosocial (targeting defectors) or antisocial (targeting cooperators or indiscriminately).

- source: Cason, T. N., & Gangadharan, L. (2016). Swords without covenants do not lead to self-governance. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment tested four treatments (baseline, punishment only, communication only, both) in a repeated nonlinear common pool resource game with groups of six. Peer punishment (costly, 3:1 fine-to-fee ratio, up to 5 points per target) did not significantly increase group yield or efficiency relative to baseline. Communication (anonymous chat, twice per session) led to dramatic and statistically significant increases in yield/efficiency, with most groups achieving near the social optimum. When both mechanisms were available, communication made punishment largely redundant and unused. The inefficacy of punishment is attributed to the complexity of the nonlinear environment and insufficient use of strong punishment. The findings challenge the generalizability of positive punishment effects from linear public goods games to more realistic, nonlinear settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in nonlinear common pool resource environments (with concave private benefits and endogenous group costs), enabling peer punishment does not improve efficiency over baseline. Therefore, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar nonlinear games, one should expect little or no efficiency gain, even with a punishment technology that is effective in linear games. The presence of communication is a much stronger moderator, with large positive effects on efficiency. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the payoff structure (linear vs nonlinear), and that control efficiency alone is not sufficient to predict treatment efficiency without considering the game's nonlinearity and the availability of communication.

- source: Aakre, S., Helland, L., & Hovi, J. (2016). When Does Informal Enforcement Work?. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that enabling punishment of both nonparticipants and noncompliant participants (i.e., enforcing both participation and compliance) leads to a substantial increase in total group contributions (efficiency) when punishment is introduced. In contrast, if only one type of free riding can be punished (either only insiders or only outsiders), introducing punishment does not increase efficiency. In the forced participation (FP) control, punishment increases efficiency as in standard PGGs. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is conditional: it is positive only when both participation and compliance can be enforced, and negligible or even negative otherwise.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on whether the game design allows for both participation and compliance enforcement. If the design allows punishment of both nonparticipants and noncompliant participants (or, in standard PGGs, if participation is forced), enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency. If only one type of free riding can be punished, enabling punishment will not increase efficiency. For prediction, the presence or absence of both enforcement channels is a critical moderator. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not sufficient to predict treatment efficiency unless the enforcement regime is specified.

- source: Eisenkopf, G., & Bächtiger, A. (2013). Mediation and Conflict Prevention. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a three-round common-pool resource game with two players, the addition of a third-party mediator who can both communicate and credibly threaten punishment leads to a substantial increase in group efficiency (combined payoff), with most groups achieving the efficient outcome. Communication alone (either direct or via mediator) increases efficiency relative to no mediation, but can also result in greater inequality if one player exploits the other's cooperation. Punishment alone, when costly and not paired with communication, does not improve efficiency. The effectiveness of the punishment-plus-communication treatment is robust to changes in the mediator's interests (impartial, biased, or no-stakes). The study provides direct evidence that communication and punishment are complementary tools for conflict prevention and efficiency in common-pool settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated common-pool resource game with two players, enabling both communication (even indirect, via a mediator) and credible punishment substantially increases efficiency compared to control conditions (no mediation or only one tool enabled). The effect is robust to mediator bias and to the mediator's economic stake. For prediction tasks, the presence of both punishment and communication should be expected to yield much higher efficiency than control, while punishment alone (especially if costly) may not improve efficiency. The game is not a standard linear PGG but is a close variant with continuous extraction and a well-defined efficient outcome, making the findings highly relevant for similar environments.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Gangadharan, L. (2013). Empowering neighbors versus imposing regulations: An experimental analysis of pollution reduction schemes. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment simulating a non-point source pollution problem with heterogeneous agents, four treatments were compared: baseline (no intervention), group tax only, punishment only, and group tax plus punishment. The group tax mechanism (formal regulation) led to large and statistically significant increases in efficiency (from ~55% in baseline to ~87% with tax, and ~90% with tax plus punishment). Peer punishment alone increased efficiency only slightly and not significantly. When combined, punishment reduced variance and helped keep pollution below the target more often, but the efficiency gain was offset by the deadweight loss from punishment costs. Punishment was used less frequently and less intensely than in standard public goods games, limiting its effectiveness. The results suggest that formal regulatory mechanisms are more effective than informal peer punishment in increasing efficiency, but peer punishment can play a complementary role.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public-goods-like environment with a negative externality (pollution), enabling peer punishment alone yields only modest and inconsistent efficiency gains, while a formal group penalty (tax) mechanism yields large and robust efficiency improvements. Adding punishment to the group tax further reduces damages and increases the likelihood of meeting the target, but does not significantly increase efficiency due to the costliness of punishment. For prediction, the presence of a formal group penalty is the key driver of efficiency, and the marginal effect of enabling peer punishment is small or negligible unless punishment is much more frequent or severe than observed here. The game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment tech, group size, rounds, information) are well-documented, making this study highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in similar settings.

- source: De Geest, L. R., Stranlund, J. K., & Spraggon, J. M. (2017). Deterring poaching of a common pool resource. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing insiders to observe and punish outsiders (poachers) has a non-monotonic effect on efficiency. With zero monitoring (no punishment possible), outsiders show some restraint and insiders achieve moderate efficiency. With partial monitoring (randomly able to punish one outsider), poaching increases over time, sanctions are low and random, and efficiency for insiders drops to Nash equilibrium levels or below. With full monitoring (all outsiders observable and punishable), poaching is lower and more stable, insiders coordinate better, and efficiency is highest, though still far from the social optimum. Sanctions are generally insufficient to fully deter poaching. The results suggest that imperfect punishment opportunities can backfire, reducing efficiency, while full monitoring and punishment can improve but not maximize efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment in common-pool-resource-like games depends critically on the monitoring structure. If punishment is only partially possible (imperfect monitoring), efficiency can decrease relative to no punishment at all, due to increased poaching and poor coordination of sanctions. If punishment is fully enabled (full monitoring), efficiency increases relative to both the control and partial monitoring, but does not reach the social optimum. For prediction, the presence and effectiveness of punishment, the monitoring structure, and the ability to coordinate sanctions are crucial moderators. Control efficiency (no punishment) is not always a lower bound: enabling punishment can reduce efficiency if monitoring is imperfect. Game design dimensions such as punishment cost, punishment tech, group size, communication, and information structure (who sees what) are all relevant. The results caution against assuming that adding punishment always increases efficiency.

- source: Ozono, H., Jin, N., Watabe, M., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Solving the second-order free rider problem in a public goods game: An experiment using a leader support system. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when leaders are given the ability to punish followers using resources provided by follower support (support-present condition), both the frequency and effectiveness of punishment increase compared to a no-support condition. This leads to a higher probability of groups achieving high cooperation (as measured by contribution rates) and higher average profits for both leaders and followers, especially under 'linkage' punishment leaders who punish both non-cooperators and non-supporters. The no-support condition, where leaders have externally provided punishment resources, results in weaker punishment and lower group cooperation. The findings suggest that the structure of punishment funding and the type of leader punishment strategy are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling leaders to punish using resources provided by group members (rather than external resources) increases the efficiency of punishment in public goods games, as measured by group and individual profits and the frequency of high-cooperation groups. The effect is especially pronounced when leaders punish both first- and second-order free riders. For prediction tasks, the presence of a leader support system, the type of punishment strategy, and the source of punishment capital are key moderators. The control (no-support) condition provides a baseline for efficiency, and the support-present condition demonstrates a substantial positive shift in efficiency when punishment is enabled and properly incentivized.

- source: Gächter, S., Mengel, F., Tsakas, E., & Vostroknutov, A. (2017). Growth and inequality in public good provision. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a dynamic public good game with endogenous endowments, contributions and group wealth increase over time even without punishment. Introducing punishment does not increase group wealth or efficiency; in some cases, it reduces them, particularly in longer games. Punishment increases contributions but destroys resources, leading to lower or unchanged group wealth. Early inequality is strongly negatively correlated with later group wealth in the punishment condition, and anti-social punishment (shirkers punishing contributors) is more prevalent in unequal groups, further reducing efficiency. Removing inequality experimentally restores the positive effect of punishment on contributions and wealth.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in dynamic public good games with endogenous endowments, enabling punishment does not improve and may reduce efficiency compared to control (no punishment). The negative effect is especially likely when early inequality is high, as anti-social punishment becomes more common and destructive. For prediction tasks, if the game design includes endogenous endowments and dynamic wealth accumulation, the presence of punishment should not be expected to increase efficiency, and may decrease it, especially as the number of rounds increases. The effect of punishment is context-dependent: in standard (static, exogenous endowment) PGGs, punishment increases efficiency, but in dynamic, endogenous settings, it does not.

- source: Robbett, A. (2016). Sustaining cooperation in heterogeneous groups. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with heterogeneous, private or observable incentives, peer punishment (sanctions) does not significantly increase group contributions or efficiency above the baseline. This holds both when group members' types are private and when they are observable. In the private information setting, punishment is rarely used except against unambiguous free-riders, and the magnitude is too low to change incentives. When types are observable, punishment is more accurately targeted but still fails to reliably increase contributions, as some high-demanders resist increasing their contributions even when punished. In contrast, both unrestricted communication and a centralized bonus/fine mechanism (Falkinger) substantially increase efficiency, with communication allowing groups to coordinate on the socially optimal contribution vector. The results suggest that punishment is a weak tool for promoting efficiency in heterogeneous groups, especially when contribution norms are ambiguous.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with heterogeneous and possibly private incentives, enabling peer punishment does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff above the baseline. The effect of punishment is weak or null, even when punishment is relatively effective (3:1 impact/cost ratio) and types are observable. Therefore, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar heterogeneous PGG-like environments, one should expect little or no increase in efficiency relative to the control. The results also highlight the importance of communication and centralized mechanisms as more effective alternatives. The findings are directly relevant for settings with 3 players, 10 rounds, no chat (in punishment treatments), continuous contributions, and a non-linear payoff structure with interior optima.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2013). If Cooperation Is Likely Punish Mildly: Insights from Economic Experiments Based on the Snowdrift Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This study uses repeated group snowdrift games (a close variant of public goods games) with and without punishment to test the effects of mild versus severe punishment on cooperation and average payoff. In groups of five, with 25 rounds per session, the authors manipulate the cost-to-benefit ratio (r = 0.2 or 0.8) and the punishment fine (p = 2.0 or 4.0, with constant cost to punisher). When cooperation is likely (r = 0.2), both mild and severe punishment increase cooperation, but severe punishment does not further increase cooperation and reduces average payoff compared to mild punishment. When cooperation is unlikely (r = 0.8), only severe punishment substantially increases cooperation and average payoff; mild punishment is less effective and can even reduce average payoff compared to no punishment. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: mild punishment is optimal when cooperation is already likely, while severe punishment is only justified in highly defection-prone environments.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the underlying difficulty of cooperation (cost-to-benefit ratio, analogous to MPCR in PGGs). If the control game (no punishment) already achieves high efficiency, adding severe punishment will not improve and may reduce efficiency; mild punishment is sufficient. If the control game has low efficiency (cooperation is rare), only severe punishment can increase efficiency. The cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the baseline cooperation rate, are critical moderators. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency in snowdrift- or PGG-like games with peer punishment.

- source: Khadjavi, M., Lange, A., & Nicklisch, A. (2017). How transparency may corrupt - experimental evidence from asymmetric public goods games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in an asymmetric public goods game (one official can embezzle, others can only contribute), transparency alone (without punishment) increases embezzlement by the official and reduces public good provision. Introducing a peer punishment stage (where group members can spend resources to reduce others' payoffs) increases public good provision and curbs embezzlement, but only if the official's identity is known. When both identity and actions are transparent (high transparency), punishment is more accurately targeted, reducing stigmatization and further increasing cooperation. Mean income is higher in punishment treatments, and income inequality between officials and citizens is reduced when punishment is possible and the official's identity is known.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling peer punishment in a public goods game with asymmetric roles (embezzlement possible for one player) increases group payoff and public good provision, especially when the official's identity is known. The effect is robust across both low and high transparency, but high transparency reduces erroneous punishment (stigmatization) and allows more targeted sanctions. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio), group size of 4, 10 rounds, and no communication, is associated with a substantial increase in efficiency-related outcomes compared to control. The effect is strongest when both identity and actions are transparent, but even low transparency with punishment is effective. Control efficiency (without punishment) is lower under high transparency due to increased embezzlement. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in asymmetric PGG-like environments with similar design features.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & van Dijk, E. (2016). The impact of personal responsibility on the (un)willingness to punish non-cooperation and reward cooperation. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that people are more reluctant to punish non-cooperators when they feel personally responsible (i.e., when deciding individually), but this reluctance is attenuated when responsibility is shared in a group.
