This effect is specific to punishment and does not apply to rewarding cooperation. The effect is mediated by feelings of personal responsibility and entitlement, not by external accountability. The experiments use a common resource dilemma with sanctioning opportunities, but do not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs. Thus, the findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral moderators of punishment willingness, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to punish non-cooperation is higher when punishment decisions are made jointly by groups rather than by individuals, due to reduced personal responsibility and increased entitlement. However, since the study does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or common resource games. The results are useful for predicting when punishment will be more or less likely to be used, but not for predicting the efficiency consequences of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the structure of the punishment decision (individual vs. group) may moderate the behavioral use of punishment, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Noussair, C. N., van Soest, D., & Stoop, J. (2015). Punishment, reward, and cooperation in a framed field experiment. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing punishment or reward opportunities does not increase cooperation compared to baseline: average catch and effort are statistically indistinguishable across treatments. Punishment is directed at non-cooperators, but does not induce greater cooperation in subsequent periods. Reward is used more frequently than punishment, but is not targeted based on recipient cooperation. The results suggest that, in this field setting, peer punishment and reward are ineffective at increasing cooperation, in contrast to many laboratory PGG studies. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported, but the behavioral proxies (catch and effort) do not improve with punishment or reward.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a field setting with a common-pool resource framed as a social dilemma, enabling peer punishment (and reward) does not increase cooperation or its behavioral proxies, compared to a baseline with no sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency observed in many lab PGGs may not generalize to field environments with similar incentive structures but different context, framing, or real-world constraints. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited; it is most informative as a caution about generalizability and as evidence that punishment may not always improve group outcomes, even when the game structure is similar to a PGG.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & van Dijk, E. (2014). On the willingness to costly reward cooperation and punish non-cooperation: The moderating role of type of social dilemma. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that participants are more likely to reward cooperative behavior than to punish non-cooperative behavior, and that this tendency is stronger in public good dilemmas (where cooperation involves giving up private property) than in common resource dilemmas (where cooperation involves refraining from taking collective property). When given a choice, most participants prefer to reward rather than punish. The size of rewards is also larger than the size of punishments, especially in public good dilemmas. The study does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that the preference for reward over punishment may have implications for collective welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good and common resource games. It shows that, when given a choice, people are more willing to reward than to punish, and that punishment is used less frequently and less intensely, especially in public good dilemmas. However, because the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that the mere availability of punishment may not lead to high usage, especially if reward is also available, and that the type of social dilemma (public good vs. common resource) moderates willingness to punish. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment may be less effective at increasing efficiency in public good dilemmas if participants are reluctant to use it, particularly when reward is also an option.

- source: Mussweiler, T., & Ockenfels, A. (2013). Similarity increases altruistic punishment in humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that perceived similarity (induced by a cognitive priming task) increases the willingness to engage in costly punishment of uncooperative behavior in a two-person cooperation game with a punishment phase. In contrast, group identity (in-group vs. out-group) reduces altruistic punishment toward in-group members. The effect of similarity is not explained by group identity, and the two have opposite effects on punishment. The study does not directly measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the cooperation phase is single-shot, so there is no opportunity for punishment to feed back into increased cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that psychological framing (similarity focus vs. group identity) can significantly alter the frequency and intensity of costly punishment in a cooperation game with a punishment phase. However, it does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or report most of the structural game design dimensions relevant for predicting efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding psychological moderators of punishment behavior, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design parameters. Thus, its direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design is limited.

- source: Diekmann, A., & Przepiorka, W. (2015). Punitive preferences, monetary incentives and tacit coordination in the punishment of defectors promote cooperation in humans. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when only one group member is needed to punish a defector (step-level public good), groups with punishment cost heterogeneity (one strong, low-cost punisher) achieve higher rates of punishment and lower rates of defection than symmetric groups. Tacit coordination on the strong punisher leads to more efficient punishment (i.e., punishment is more often carried out by only one group member). The presence of a penalty for defectors (i.e., punishment that also harms the defector) is necessary to reduce defection rates. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment cost heterogeneity can improve the effectiveness and efficiency of norm enforcement in such games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment cost heterogeneity (i.e., having a designated or focal punisher with lower costs) increases the effectiveness and efficiency of punishment in step-level public good games, as measured by behavioral outcomes (punishment and defection rates). While it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, the findings suggest that game designs enabling tacit coordination on a single, low-cost punisher are likely to yield higher efficiency when punishment is enabled, especially when a penalty for defectors is present. The results are most directly relevant for step-level or volunteer's dilemma variants of public goods games, rather than standard linear PGGs. The absence of explicit efficiency or payoff data limits direct quantitative prediction, but the qualitative pattern is clear: punishment is more effective and less wasteful when cost heterogeneity allows for coordination on a single punisher.

- source: Delton, A. W., Nemirow, J., Robertson, T. E., Cimino, A., & Cosmides, L. (2013). Merely Opting Out of a Public Good Is Moralized: An Error Management Approach to Cooperation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people who opt out of contributing to public goods are moralized and sometimes punished, even if they have not yet free ridden. This preventive moralization is stronger in public goods than in club goods, and is mediated by the perceived likelihood that the non-contributor will free ride in the future. The effect is robust across implicit, explicit, and behavioral measures, and is observed in both student and nationally representative adult samples. However, the studies do not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total earnings, and thus do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment and moralization are triggered by non-contribution in public goods contexts, even before actual free riding occurs. It demonstrates that costly punishment is more likely in public goods than in club goods, but does not report on group efficiency or total payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms can increase the likelihood of punishment being used in public goods games, especially when free riding is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence about how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to informing about the behavioral context in which punishment is likely to be applied.

- source: de Melo, G., & Piaggio, M. (2015). The perils of peer punishment: Evidence from a common pool resource framed field experiment. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Nonmonetary peer punishment (social disapproval) reduces resource extraction (increases cooperation) in mixed-community groups but has no significant effect in in-group (same community) groups. The effect is measured as a reduction in the average number of nets used per person. There is substantial antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators), which can undermine the effectiveness of punishment. The study does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but lower extraction implies higher efficiency. The presence of antisocial punishment and lack of a unique social norm limit the positive effect of punishment. The findings suggest that the context (group composition) moderates the effect of punishment on cooperation, and that coordination is needed for punishment to be effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling peer punishment (social disapproval) can reduce resource extraction (increase cooperation) in common pool resource games, especially in mixed-group settings. However, the effect on efficiency is not directly measured; it must be inferred from extraction levels. The presence of antisocial punishment and lack of norm consensus can limit or even reverse the positive effect of punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency in mixed-group contexts than in homogeneous groups, but the risk of antisocial punishment and unclear norms can reduce or negate efficiency gains. The specific game design (field context, nonmonetary punishment, no communication, continuous extraction choice) should be considered when generalizing.

- source: Gatiso, T. T., & Vollan, B. (2017). Democracy and cooperation in commons management: experimental evidence of representative and direct democracy from community forests in Ethiopia. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Democratic procedures (either direct or representative) for rule or leader selection significantly increase cooperation in a dynamic CPR extraction game compared to imposed rules or leaders. The effect is not due to the presence of sanctions per se, but to the legitimacy conferred by democratic procedures. Sanctions only increase cooperation when democratically chosen. There is no significant difference in cooperation between direct and representative democracy. The main outcome is behavioral (harvest ratio), not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or total group earnings, but the design allows for such calculation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that democratic procedures for rule or leader selection increase cooperation in CPR-like environments, and that sanctions only increase cooperation when democratically chosen. However, the main outcome is behavioral (harvest ratio), not efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation (lower HR) likely implies higher efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this. The game is a dynamic CPR extraction game, not a standard linear PGG, but is closely related. The findings suggest that the legitimacy of the process (democratic choice) is a key moderator of punishment/sanction effectiveness. The paper does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency, so its value for efficiency prediction is indirect and should be weighted accordingly.

- source: Gatiso, T. T., Vollan, B., & Nuppenau, E. A. (2015). Resource scarcity, and democratic elections in commons dilemmas: An experiment on forest use in Ethiopia. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that resource scarcity (lower initial stock) leads to less cooperative behavior (higher HR), and that the way sanctions are implemented matters: democratically elected sanctions increase cooperation (lower HR) both under abundance and scarcity, while imposed sanctions can reduce cooperation (higher HR) under abundance (crowding-out effect), but not under scarcity. The main outcome is behavioral (HR), not efficiency or group payoff. Theoretical efficiency levels are provided, but not empirically analyzed as outcomes. The findings suggest that participatory rule-making is more effective than top-down sanctions for sustaining cooperation, especially when resources are not extremely scarce.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how the method of sanction implementation (election vs. imposition) moderates the effect of punishment on cooperation in a common-pool resource game with real-world framing. However, because the main outcome is behavioral (harvest ratio) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results must be mapped with caution to efficiency prediction tasks. Theoretical efficiency levels are given, but empirical results focus on HR. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, no explicit MPCR, probabilistic punishment, no rewards, summary info shown, anonymous punishment) are well described, supporting mapping to prediction models. The main implication is that democratically elected sanctions are more likely to increase cooperation (and thus efficiency) than imposed sanctions, especially under resource abundance. Under scarcity, the difference is smaller, but elections still outperform imposition.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2013). Individual heterogeneity and costly punishment: a volunteer's dilemma. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that costly punishment, when implemented as a volunteer's dilemma, is effective in deterring defection (stealing) in groups. When only one group member needs to punish and punishment costs are heterogeneous, punishment is more efficiently coordinated (mainly by the lowest-cost punisher), and deterrence of defection is as strong as with much higher penalties in a symmetric-cost setting. The introduction of punishment reduces the stealing rate by about 50 percentage points overall, with greater reductions in conditions with high penalty or with cost heterogeneity. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment can sustain cooperation and that heterogeneity in punishment costs facilitates more effective and efficient norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong behavioral evidence that introducing punishment in a volunteer's dilemma setting (where only one punisher is needed) greatly reduces defection rates, especially when punishment costs are heterogeneous. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment will likely increase efficiency (by reducing defection) in similar settings, and that cost heterogeneity among punishers can substitute for higher penalty size in deterring defection. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes, the quantitative effect on efficiency must be inferred indirectly from the large reductions in defection rates. The findings are most applicable to step-level public goods games with punishment opportunities and cost heterogeneity among punishers.

- source: Inglis, R. F., West, S., & Buckling, A. (2014). An experimental study of strong reciprocity in bacteria. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in bacterial populations, cooperative punishers (who both produce a public good and punish non-cooperators) can invade a population of cheats only when the traits for cooperation and punishment are tightly linked. When the linkage is broken (by mixing punishing and non-punishing cooperators), invasion fails. Non-punishing cooperators alone also cannot invade. The main outcome is the frequency and survival/extinction of the different strains, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results support theoretical predictions that linkage is necessary for the evolutionary success of strong reciprocity in this context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (and by implication, group efficiency) depends critically on the linkage between cooperation and punishment. However, the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff directly, and the outcomes are evolutionary (strain invasion/extinction) rather than economic payoffs. The findings suggest that in systems where punishment and cooperation are not tightly linked, punishment may not improve group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the importance of linkage or coupling between cooperation and punishment, but less so about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency in standard public goods games.

- source: Chierchia, G., Lesemann, F. H. P., Snower, D., Vogel, M., & Singer, T. (2017). Caring Cooperators and Powerful Punishers: Differential Effects of Induced Care and Power Motivation on Different Types of Economic Decision Making. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Inducing a Care motive increases a latent cooperation factor (which includes public goods contributions), while Power induction increases a punishment factor (punishment in 2nd/3rd party punishment games, ultimatum, impunity). The two factors are largely orthogonal. Care increases cooperation relative to control, but does not increase punishment. Power increases punishment relative to Care, but not relative to control. No direct efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment and cooperation are distinct behavioral tendencies, and that motivational context (Care vs. Power) can differentially affect them. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does suggest that punishment behavior can be increased by Power motives, but this is not linked to group payoff or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by clarifying that punishment and cooperation are separable and can be independently manipulated.

- source: Gelcich, S., Guzman, R., Rodríguez-Sickert, C., Castilla, J. C., & Cárdenas, J. C. (2013). Exploring External Validity of Common Pool Resource Experiments: Insights from Artisanal Benthic Fisheries in Chile. *Ecology and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: This paper reports a field-lab experiment with Chilean artisanal fishers playing a 20-round common pool resource game. In the first 10 rounds (open access, no enforcement), overharvesting increased and efficiency declined, especially among low-performance unions and nonunionized fishers. In rounds 11-20, a weak enforcement regime was introduced: two players per round were randomly inspected, and if caught overharvesting, their entire round's harvest was confiscated. This sanction led to a sharp reduction in overharvest and a large increase in group earnings (efficiency) among unionized fishers, especially those from high-performance unions, who sustained cooperation and high efficiency through the end of the game. Low-performance unions showed a temporary improvement but cooperation eroded over time. Nonunionized fishers did not respond to enforcement and remained inefficient. The paper provides direct evidence that even weak sanctions can improve efficiency in CPR settings, but the effect is strongly moderated by group social capital and prior cooperative norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment/sanction mechanism (even if weak and probabilistic) in a repeated CPR game with continuous contribution/extraction choices and no communication can substantially increase group efficiency, especially when baseline efficiency is not already at floor. The effect is strongest in groups with high social capital or prior cooperative norms. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment in similar CPR/Pgg-like games will increase efficiency, with the magnitude depending on group composition and baseline cooperativeness. The design features—5 players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous extraction, no rewards, and a probabilistic, high-impact punishment—are all relevant for mapping to prediction. The control (no punishment) condition yields low efficiency, while the treatment (punishment enabled) yields much higher efficiency for unionized groups.

- source: Zhosan, D., & Gardner, R. (2013). Problems of the commons: group behavior, cooperation and sanctioning in a two-harbor experiment. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a laboratory experiment simulating a two-commons (two-harbor) resource dilemma, groups of 8 participants (students and professional fishermen) repeatedly chose how to allocate effort between two commons and a safe alternative. The experiment varied institutional features across blocks: baseline (no restrictions), enforcement (costly movement between commons), communication, and communication plus sanctioning. Efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) was the primary outcome. Communication led to large, significant increases in efficiency for all groups, with average net efficiency rising from ~0.8 to ~0.98. The addition of a sanctioning mechanism (warnings, then monetary penalties for repeated defection) further increased efficiency in most groups, sometimes reaching 1.0. The effect of sanctioning was generally positive but smaller than the effect of communication, and in one group, sanctioning led to a breakdown in cooperation. The results show that communication and sanctioning can both be effective in increasing efficiency in commons dilemmas, with communication having the largest and most consistent effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication among group members in a commons dilemma can dramatically increase efficiency, even before any formal or monetary sanctioning is introduced. The addition of a sanctioning mechanism (warnings and monetary penalties) yields further, though more modest, efficiency gains. For prediction tasks, the presence of communication and/or sanctioning should be expected to increase efficiency substantially relative to a control with neither. The effect of communication is robust and large; the effect of sanctioning is positive but may be context-dependent and sometimes less pronounced. The experiment's design and reporting allow for direct mapping to game design dimensions relevant for prediction.

- source: Freytag, A., Güth, W., Koppel, H., & Wangler, L. (2014). Is regulation by milestones efficiency enhancing? An experimental study of environmental protection. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This laboratory experiment tests whether adding intermediate contribution targets ('milestones') to a cumulative threshold public goods game increases efficiency, measured by group payoffs and the probability of reaching a final target. The game is played in groups of 5 over 6 rounds, with treatments varying the strictness of milestones, the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), and the probability of group loss if targets are missed. The main findings are: (1) In a scenario with no continuous efficiency gains (i.e., only reaching the target matters), strict milestones increase both contributions and the probability of reaching the target, thus enhancing efficiency. (2) In a scenario with continuous efficiency gains (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones actually reduce average contributions and efficiency, as groups focus on meeting milestones rather than maximizing payoffs. (3) In an intermediate scenario, the positive and negative effects cancel out. The effect of milestones is thus context-dependent, and their efficiency impact is not universally positive.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment-like milestone mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the underlying payoff structure. When the only way to generate group payoffs is to reach a threshold (no continuous efficiency gains), strict milestones (punishment for missing intermediate targets) can improve efficiency by increasing coordination and contributions. However, when continuous efficiency gains are present (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones can reduce efficiency, as they may distract from maximizing group welfare. For prediction, this means that the presence and structure of milestone/punishment mechanisms, the marginal per-capita return, and the risk of group loss must all be considered. Control efficiency (without milestones/punishment) is a strong moderator: if control efficiency is already high, milestones may not help and can even hurt. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions in cumulative threshold public goods games with milestone/punishment features.

- source: Sääksvuori, L. (2014). Intergroup conflict, ostracism, and the evolution of cooperation under free migration. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: This paper experimentally studies a dynamic public goods game with endogenous group formation, free migration, and treatments varying the presence of intergroup competition and ostracism (social exclusion by majority vote). The main findings are: (1) Ostracism increases average group size and contributions to the public good, resulting in higher net payoffs and aggregate returns, both with and without intergroup competition. (2) Intergroup competition alone does not increase group size or contributions, and leads to more imbalanced group structures, which reduces aggregate returns. (3) The combination of ostracism and competition still produces higher cooperation and payoffs than competition alone. (4) The threat of ostracism reduces the number of free-agents and increases the desirability of joining groups, outweighing the effect of involuntary exclusion. (5) Intergroup competition reduces the likelihood of ostracism, as groups are less willing to expel members when facing external threats.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling a social sanctioning mechanism (ostracism) in a dynamic, migration-allowed public goods environment increases both cooperation and group-level payoffs (i.e., efficiency), regardless of whether intergroup competition is present. The effect of ostracism is robust and positive for efficiency-related outcomes. Intergroup competition alone does not improve efficiency and may even reduce it by fragmenting groups. The design features—such as endogenous group formation, free migration, and ostracism by majority vote—are important moderators. The paper does not use classic costly punishment (with explicit cost per punishment point), but ostracism functions as a group-level sanction. Thus, for environments with similar group formation and migration, the presence of ostracism is a strong positive predictor for efficiency gains from sanctioning.

- source: Hsu, L. C. (2013). TAX AUDITING AS A PUBLIC GOOD GAME: AN EXPERIMENTAL STUDY ON PUNISHMENT AND COMPLIANCE. *Pacific Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The endogenous-audit (collective punishment) treatment produces much higher compliance rates than both the no-audit and random-audit treatments. In the final 10 rounds, compliance rates are 0.297 (no audit), 0.758 (random audit), and 0.921 (endogenous audit). However, average earnings are lower in the endogenous-audit treatment (85.2 points) than in the random-audit treatment (92.1 points), due to the distribution of fines and discovered taxes. Audit productivity (e.g., reported tax per audit) is higher in the endogenous-audit treatment in the latter half of the game. The main effect of punishment is to increase compliance, but the effect on efficiency (group payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly reported and may be ambiguous due to the cost of monitoring and the way fines are pooled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a collective punishment mechanism (endogenous audit) in a public-goods-like tax game increases compliance rates compared to both no-punishment and exogenous punishment (random audit) conditions. However, the effect on efficiency (group payoff relative to the full cooperation benchmark) is not directly reported and may be ambiguous, as average earnings are lower in the endogenous-audit treatment than in the random-audit treatment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that punishment can increase compliance but may not always increase efficiency, depending on the cost structure and how punishment/fines are redistributed. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, audit cost structure, etc.) are well specified and can be mapped to the prediction task, but the lack of direct efficiency analysis is a limitation.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Nosenzo, D., & Sefton, M. (2017). Team incentives and leadership. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Introducing a leader with discretionary reward power in a repeated team production game (a close variant of the public goods game) increases group efficiency from about 30-33% to 72-76% of the maximum possible. Leaders use their power to reward high contributors and withhold rewards from low contributors, effectively incentivizing effort. This effect holds for both homogeneous and heterogeneous teams. However, the distribution of gains is highly unequal, with leaders capturing most of the surplus. When workers can choose how much power to delegate to the leader, they typically restrict it to less than half the team output, and the potential efficiency gains are not fully realized. The main reason is that workers do not benefit much from leadership, as leaders only compensate them for effort costs and keep most of the residual output.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in VCM-like team production games, introducing a central authority with discretionary reward power (a form of positive sanctioning) can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when the leader is self-interested. The effect is robust to team heterogeneity. However, the efficiency gains are distributed unequally, and when delegation is endogenous, reluctance to empower the leader limits the realized gains. For prediction tasks, the presence of a leader with reward power (as opposed to equal sharing) should be expected to increase efficiency substantially, but the effect may be moderated if the leader's power is limited or if workers can restrict delegation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where the 'punishment' dimension is implemented as positive reward power rather than costly punishment, but the mechanism (centralized sanctioning) is closely related.

- source: Croson, R., Fatas, E., Neugebauer, T., & Morales, A. J. (2015). Excludability: A laboratory study on forced ranking in team production. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that excludability (the ability to exclude the lowest contributor from group benefits) dramatically increases both contributions and efficiency in linear public goods (VCM) and best-shot (BSM) games, but not in weakest-link (WLM) games. In the VCM, efficiency rises from 65% (baseline) to 83% (excludability) and 99% (excludability with redistribution). In the BSM, efficiency rises from 81% (baseline) to 86% (excludability), but redistribution reduces efficiency to 75%. In the WLM, excludability alone does not improve efficiency (52% in both baseline and excludability), but redistribution increases efficiency to 72%. Thus, the effect of excludability on efficiency is highly dependent on the production function, and redistribution is necessary for efficiency gains in environments with strong complementarities (WLM).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment-like exclusion mechanisms on efficiency depends critically on the production function. In standard linear public goods games (VCM), enabling exclusion of the lowest contributor increases efficiency substantially, and redistribution of the excluded share further boosts efficiency. In weakest-link games, exclusion alone does not improve efficiency, but redistribution is necessary for efficiency gains. In best-shot games, exclusion increases efficiency, but redistribution actually reduces it. For prediction tasks, the production function (average, minimum, or maximum) is a key moderator of the efficiency effect of punishment/exclusion. Control efficiency is a strong predictor, but the treatment effect of exclusion is highly context-dependent.

- source: Vollan, B., Prediger, S., & Frölich, M. (2013). Co-managing common-pool resources: Do formal rules have to be adapted to traditional ecological norms?. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This field-lab experiment with communal farmers in southern Africa shows that the efficiency of formal, democratically chosen rules for managing a common-pool resource depends on their alignment with local ecological norms. The quota rule (limiting extraction) increases group earnings and resource availability in both high and low sustainability groups, but is unpopular. The rotation rule only increases efficiency in low sustainability groups (who previously failed to coordinate), but is ineffective or even counterproductive in high sustainability groups (who already tacitly coordinated for sustainability), due to high rates of rule breaking. The lottery rule is ineffective in both types of groups. Rule compliance is lower when rules conflict with local norms, and rule breaking is not mainly motivated by profit. Thus, the effect of rule-based interventions on efficiency is highly context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of introducing a formal rule (akin to a punishment or restriction mechanism) on efficiency in a common-pool resource game, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect depends on the compatibility of the rule with pre-existing group norms. Rules that align with local sustainability norms (e.g., quotas in groups that value sustainability) can increase efficiency, while rules that conflict with these norms (e.g., rotation in high-norm groups) may reduce efficiency due to non-compliance. The presence of a sanctioning mechanism (imperfect monitoring and penalty) is not sufficient to ensure compliance or efficiency gains. Thus, the effect of punishment-like interventions on efficiency is moderated by group-level norms and prior behavior, not just by the formal game design. This paper is highly informative for settings where the main intervention is a formal rule or restriction, but less so for classic peer-punishment PGGs.

- source: Gaudeul, A., Crosetto, P., & Riener, G. (2017). Better stuck together or free to go? Of the stability of cooperation when individuals have outside options. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment varied the ease of exit from a public project (barriers to exit) in a repeated, two-player, stochastic public-good-like game. When exit was easier (lower barriers), participants exited more frequently, but this did not increase efficiency or average payoffs. In fact, average individual welfare was lower in treatments with lower barriers to exit. The main reason was excessive exit, driven by pessimistic beliefs about the peer's likelihood of leaving, rather than accurate cost-benefit analysis. The study finds that exit is used both as a response to perceived exploitation and as a punishment for failure, but these mechanisms do not improve group efficiency. The findings suggest that making exit easier undermines group stability and reduces welfare, contrary to some theoretical expectations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-good-like environments with imperfect monitoring and stochastic outcomes, making exit easier (i.e., lowering barriers to exit) reduces efficiency and average payoffs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of exit options (a form of sanction or punishment via exclusion/self-exclusion) on efficiency. The results suggest that, unless exit is tightly controlled or costly, its availability will likely decrease group welfare due to excessive and inefficient use. For prediction tasks, the presence and ease of exit should be treated as a negative moderator of efficiency, even when the control (no-exit) game already has moderate efficiency. The study does not test classic costly punishment (e.g., deduction points), but its findings on exit as a sanction are highly informative for settings where exit or exclusion is a possible response to non-cooperation.

- source: Lévy-Garboua, L., Montmarquette, C., Vaksmann, J., & Villeval, M. C. (2017). Voluntary Contributions to a Mutual Insurance Pool. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Contribution-based coverage, which ties insurance payouts to individual contributions, significantly increases the mean coverage rate (efficiency) compared to equal coverage, where payouts are independent of contribution. In the experiment (n=12, 50 rounds), the mean coverage rate is about 26% under equal coverage and 47% under contribution-based coverage for the full sample, with even higher rates for risk-averse (high-contributing) subgroups. However, full insurance is not achieved due to coordination failures and heterogeneity in risk attitudes. The endogenous 'punishment' of low contributors (via low coverage) is effective at raising efficiency, but only partially. Aggregate earnings are similar across treatments, but the distribution of earnings is more equitable under contribution-based coverage, with reduced transfers from high to low contributors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms which endogenously punish low contributors (by reducing their insurance coverage) can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments with risk and voluntary contributions. The effect is robust but incomplete: efficiency rises from about 26% to 47% of the optimum when moving from equal to contribution-based coverage, but does not reach full efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing a punishment-like mechanism (here, endogenous sanctioning via coverage rules) in similar games. The results are most applicable to settings with continuous contributions, no explicit communication, and group sizes around 12. The paper does not study explicit costly punishment, but the contribution-based coverage mechanism functions as an endogenous, payoff-based sanction. Control efficiency (equal coverage) is low, and the treatment (contribution-based) nearly doubles efficiency. Heterogeneity in risk attitudes is a key moderator.

- source: Perez, I., Yu, D. J., Janssen, M. A., & Anderies, J. M. (2015). Social roles and performance of social-ecological systems: evidence from behavioral lab experiments. *Ecology and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that group success in a lab irrigation game is best explained by the presence of certain combinations of emergent social roles, rather than any single role. All groups had leaders, knowledge generators, and followers, but successful groups also had connectors and either moralists or observers. The enforcer role (which involves punishing others' behavior, e.g., via threats to withhold investment) was not necessary for group success and was more common in unsuccessful groups. The presence of enforcers appears to be a symptom of conflict and lack of cooperation, not a cause of improved efficiency. The experiment does not include a formal punishment mechanism (e.g., costly punishment with explicit payoff consequences), so findings about 'enforcer' roles are based on communication and threats rather than actual sanctioning actions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling formal punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public goods or common-pool resource games. Instead, it provides evidence that informal enforcement roles (as expressed in chat) are not sufficient or necessary for group success, and that their presence may indicate underlying conflict. For prediction tasks about the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper suggests that the mere presence of enforcement or sanctioning behavior (in the absence of formal mechanisms) does not guarantee improved efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings with communication and emergent informal roles, not for games with explicit, costly punishment options.

- source: Hashim, M. J., Kannan, K. N., Maximiano, S., & Ulmer, J. R. (2014). Digital Piracy, Teens, and the Source of Advice: An Experimental Study. *Journal of Management Information Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Advice from parents, especially when they are punished for their child's piracy, increases purchasing and group profit and reduces piracy compared to other advice sources. Advice from record labels is the least effective and can even reduce group profit. Advice from a regulator is largely ignored. The effect of advice is strongest when the adviser has both a social tie and a stake in the outcome. The study does not include standard peer punishment or reward mechanisms; the only 'punishment' is an exogenous penalty to parents in one treatment. The game is a close variant of a public goods game, but not a standard PGG. Efficiency-related outcomes (group profit, purchasing) are reported, but not as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that exogenous punishment of a third party (parents) for a player's free-riding can increase public good provision and group profit, especially when combined with a social tie. However, it does not test endogenous peer punishment or standard PGG punishment institutions. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms may be more effective when they are socially salient and affect someone with a close tie to the player, but direct transfer to standard PGG with peer punishment is limited. Control efficiency (group profit without punishment) is reported, and the effect of introducing a punishment-like mechanism (parent penalty) can be inferred, but the mechanism differs from standard punishment. The absence of peer punishment, reward, or communication means the results are only adjacent for direct prediction of PGG punishment effects.

- source: Howe, E. L., Murphy, J. J., Gerkey, D., & West, C. T. (2016). Indirect Reciprocity, Resource Sharing, and Environmental Risk: Evidence from Field Experiments in Siberia. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment found that voluntary sharing was substantial and directed toward those in need, especially when reputations could be built. Sharing was conditioned on cooperative behavior when such information was available. However, the presence of sharing, rewards, and reputation mechanisms did not increase contributions to the public good (i.e., did not increase cooperation in the production domain). The expected returns from contributing to the group activity, even with the possibility of receiving sharing, were not high enough to incentivize greater cooperation. Thus, the mechanisms studied did not improve group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in environments where sharing is voluntary and not highly efficient, and where rewards/punishments are not strong or direct, the introduction of sharing and reputation mechanisms may not increase group efficiency or cooperation in public goods games. The findings imply that unless the payoff structure makes rewards or punishments highly effective, simply enabling sharing or reputation is unlikely to boost efficiency. For prediction, if the control game has low efficiency and the only change is the addition of a voluntary, non-committal sharing/reward mechanism (with no direct punishment and no high-impact reward), the efficiency in the treatment is unlikely to increase. The paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms depends on their efficiency and observability.

- source: Dickson, E. S., Gordon, S. C., & Huber, G. A. (2015). Institutional Sources of Legitimate Authority: An Experimental Investigation. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that citizens are more willing to help salaried (fixed-compensation) authorities than those who personally benefit from collected penalties, but only when transparency is high. When transparency is low, support for salaried authorities collapses, while support for appropriator authorities is low regardless of transparency. Contribution rates and average earnings are reported, but do not differ significantly between treatments under full information; under limited information, contribution rates are lower for salaried authorities. The main outcome is a behavioral measure of legitimacy (citizen intervention), not efficiency or group payoff. The deterrence index (successful punishment of noncontributors minus punishment of contributors) is associated with higher contributions, but the causal effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly estimated.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how institutional features (transparency, authority incentives) affect citizen support for enforcement and, through deterrence, contribution rates. However, it does not directly estimate the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency or payoff. The design is a binary (all-or-nothing) public goods game with centralized, probabilistic punishment and a unique intervention stage. The findings suggest that the legitimacy of punishment (as measured by citizen support) is sensitive to transparency and authority incentives, which may in turn affect deterrence and contributions, but the direct impact on efficiency is not quantified. For prediction tasks, this paper is informative about mechanisms and moderators but does not provide direct treatment effect estimates for efficiency.

- source: Cacault, M. P., Goette, L., Lalive, R., & Thoenig, M. (2015). Do we harm others even if we don't need to?. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when participants are given the option to harm an out-group (even at a cost or with no additional benefit to their own group), a significant minority choose to do so, and this behavior is often motivated by a desire to benefit the in-group at the expense of the out-group. The presence of anti-social options (aggression) increases non-selfish contributions and stabilizes in-group cooperation over repeated rounds, compared to a standard public good game where cooperation declines over time. The effect is especially pronounced in later rounds. However, the paper does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it is unclear whether the increased cooperation offsets the welfare losses from aggression. The findings are relevant for understanding the behavioral complementarity between in-group cooperation and out-group aggression, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the availability of anti-social options (aggression) can increase in-group cooperation and non-selfish contributions in a public good game variant, especially in later rounds. However, because the anti-social options reduce the payoffs of out-groups and the paper does not report group efficiency or total earnings, it is not possible to determine whether the net effect on efficiency is positive or negative. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper suggests that behavioral responses to punishment-like options can increase cooperation, but may also introduce welfare-reducing behaviors. The design is adjacent to standard punishment mechanisms (since aggression is not used to enforce norms but to harm others), so caution is needed in transferring these findings to standard PGG punishment settings.

- source: McIntosh, C., Sadoulet, E., Buck, S., & Rosada, T. (2013). Reputation in a public goods game: Taking the design of credit bureaus to the lab. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that both group-level and individual-level reputation mechanisms (used for reassigning ejected players) increase average contributions in a repeated public goods game with ejection, compared to a random reassignment baseline. The effect size is about a 7.5% increase in average contribution (from ~75% to ~82% of the endowment). There is no significant difference in average contribution between group and individual information treatments. Ejection is more sensitive to individual contribution in the group information treatment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the contribution data can be mapped to efficiency. The ejection mechanism is not a standard punishment technology (no direct costly reduction of others' payoffs), but rather exclusion from play. The findings suggest that group-based reputation is at least as effective as individual-based reputation in sustaining cooperation, and that transitioning microfinance credit bureaus from group to individual reporting may not yield substantial gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with ejection (exclusion) as the sanction mechanism, both group-level and individual-level reputation mechanisms increase average contributions compared to random reassignment. The effect is similar for both information types. While the study does not report efficiency directly, the increase in average contribution implies a corresponding increase in efficiency. The ejection mechanism is not a standard punishment technology (no direct costly punishment), so the results are most relevant for environments where exclusion or ostracism is the main sanction. The findings suggest that, for games with endogenous group selection and ejection, introducing a reputation-based reassignment mechanism (either group or individual) can improve cooperation and implied efficiency, but the marginal benefit of individual-level over group-level information is minimal. The results are less directly informative for standard PGGs with costly punishment, but may be useful for predicting the effect of exclusionary sanctions.

- source: D'Exelle, B., & van den Berg, M. (2014). AID DISTRIBUTION AND COOPERATION IN UNEQUAL COMMUNITIES. *Review of Income and Wealth*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The introduction of a representative who allocates aid (selected as the highest contributor) increases group contributions compared to equal sharing, due to both competition for the representative role and anticipation of punishment (i.e., receiving less aid for low contributions). However, representatives keep a large share of the aid for themselves, so the increased total aid does not translate into higher payoffs for the rest of the group. Representatives do consider fairness, giving more to low-endowment and higher-contributing members, but still capture more than their proportional share. Endowment inequality increases contributions under equal sharing but not under representative sharing. The study does not report group efficiency or total group payoff as a ratio to the social optimum, so the effect of the representative mechanism on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, representative allocation with the ability to reward or punish via aid shares) on group contributions and the distribution of payoffs. The representative mechanism increases contributions (a proxy for cooperation), but the efficiency gain for the group as a whole is ambiguous because the representative captures a disproportionate share of the aid. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency (total group payoff relative to the maximum), this study suggests that mechanisms allowing targeted allocation can increase contributions, but may not improve average group payoffs for all members unless the allocation is constrained. The absence of direct efficiency analysis limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Shirado, H., Fu, F., Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2013). Quality versus quantity of social ties in experimental cooperative networks. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing dynamic partner selection in a networked cooperation game leads to the highest levels of cooperation at intermediate rates of network rewiring. At low rewiring rates, players accumulate many ties, including to defectors, which undermines cooperation. At very high rewiring rates, defectors can re-attach to cooperators faster than cooperators can detach, also undermining cooperation. The ability to break ties with defectors (interpreted as a form of social punishment) helps maintain cooperation, but only when the rate of change is not too high or too low. The main outcome is the cooperation rate; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of dynamic partner selection (ostracism-like social punishment) on cooperation in networked public goods games. It suggests that enabling players to break ties with defectors can sustain cooperation, but only at intermediate rates of network change. However, because the study does not include explicit costly punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGGs is limited. The findings are most relevant for settings where social exclusion or partner choice is the main sanctioning mechanism, rather than costly punishment. The results suggest that the design dimension of network dynamism (rewiring rate) can have a non-monotonic effect on cooperation, which may translate to efficiency if payoff outcomes were measured.

- source: Yoeli, E., Hoffman, M., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Powering up with indirect reciprocity in a large-scale field experiment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that making participation in a voluntary demand response program observable to neighbors nearly triples participation rates compared to an anonymous condition. The effect of observability is much larger than that of a $25 monetary incentive. The effect is strongest in contexts where reputational concerns are likely to matter more (e.g., apartment buildings, homeowners vs. renters, and when the program is framed as a public good). The results support the theory that indirect reciprocity and reputational incentives can drive large-scale cooperation in real-world public goods dilemmas. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing observability (reputational incentives) can dramatically increase participation in a real-world public goods context, especially when social ties and public good framing are salient. However, because the outcome is participation rate (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms. It does, however, suggest that non-monetary, reputational incentives can be more effective than small monetary incentives in increasing cooperation.

- source: Bernard, M., Dreber, A., Strimling, P., & Eriksson, K. (2013). The subgroup problem: When can binding voting on extractions from a common pool resource overcome the tragedy of the commons?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares four treatments in a repeated static common pool resource (CPR) game: (1) Autonomy (individual extraction), (2) Global Democracy (all vote, binding for all), (3) Local Democracy (subgroup voting, binding within subgroup), and (4) Delegation (random subgroup leader votes, binding for all). Efficiency, measured by mean profit per round and mean extraction, is highest in Global Democracy and Delegation (close to social optimum), lowest in Autonomy (close to Nash equilibrium), and intermediate in Local Democracy (closer to Nash than to optimum, and deteriorating over time). The results show that only globally binding voting (direct or via delegation) reliably overcomes the tragedy of the commons, while local/subgroup voting is insufficient. No explicit punishment or sanctioning is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in CPR games with the design features described, introducing a binding group-level voting mechanism (even via randomly selected leaders) can dramatically increase efficiency compared to individual decision-making. However, binding voting limited to subgroups is less effective and does not achieve full efficiency. The absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means the results are not directly transferable to settings where punishment is the treatment of interest, but the findings are highly informative for predicting the effect of institutional design (voting scope) on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that group-level binding rules (even without punishment) can substitute for or complement punishment in achieving high efficiency, while subgroup-level rules are less effective.

- source: Sadowski, J., Spierre, S. G., Selinger, E., Seager, T. P., Adams, E. A., & Berardy, A. (2015). Intergroup Cooperation in Common Pool Resource Dilemmas. *Science and Engineering Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The studies found that, in a complex, multi-group common-pool resource game with real stakes and open communication, groups were able to achieve cooperative outcomes and avoid the tragedy of the commons, even in the absence of explicit punishment mechanisms. Cooperation was facilitated by the emergence of ethical leaders and effective intergroup communication. In both studies, groups coordinated to ensure equitable resource distribution, resulting in moderate but sustainable payoffs for all. The game design allowed for a variety of strategies, but did not include punishment or reward options. The findings suggest that leadership and communication can substitute for formal sanctions in promoting cooperation and efficiency, but do not provide evidence on the effect of punishment per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods or common-pool resource games, as it does not include or manipulate punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide evidence that, in the absence of punishment, cooperation and moderate efficiency can be achieved through communication and leadership. For prediction tasks, this paper may help estimate baseline efficiency in control conditions without punishment, especially in games with communication and group identity. It does not provide evidence on how efficiency would change if punishment were enabled.

- source: Majolo, B., & Maréchal, L. (2017). Between-group competition elicits within-group cooperation in children. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that simulated between-group competition (BGC) increases within-group cooperation among children aged 3-10, as measured by the number of stickers shared with group members (experiment 1) and the number of stickers contributed to a public good (experiment 2). The effect is robust to age (especially for children 6+), and is not explained by gender, kinship, or familiarity. However, the experiments do not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The main dependent variables are behavioral measures of cooperation, not payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does provide indirect evidence that between-group competition can increase cooperative behavior in children, but since punishment is not manipulated or measured, and efficiency or group payoff is not reported, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, MPCR) are partially reported, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means this paper should not be weighted heavily for the prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Messer, K. D., Suter, J. F., & Yan, J. B. (2013). Context Effects in a Negatively Framed Social Dilemma Experiment. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The experiment finds that pre-play communication and voting significantly increase contributions to the public account in a negatively-framed VCM. The effect of the status quo (initial endowment in private vs. public account) is reversed compared to positively-framed games: with communication, a private account status quo leads to higher and more persistent contributions than a public account status quo. Voting alone does not significantly affect contributions beyond communication. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and all quantitative results are reported as contribution rates, not direct efficiency or payoff measures. The findings highlight the importance of context and framing in social dilemmas, but do not provide evidence on the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games or their variants. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, as no punishment mechanism is present in any treatment. However, it does provide evidence on how communication, voting, and status quo framing affect contribution rates (and thus implied efficiency) in a negatively-framed VCM. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not relevant. For tasks focused on communication, voting, or framing, it is informative.

- source: Diekmann, A., & Przepiorka, W. (2016). Take One for the Team! Individual Heterogeneity and the Emergence of Latent Norms in a Volunteer's Dilemma. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in repeated volunteer's dilemma games, efficient public good provision emerges through different behavioral patterns depending on group structure. In symmetric groups with fixed partners, turn-taking emerges, leading to high efficiency and equal payoffs. In asymmetric groups, especially when the lowest-cost actor is much stronger or when roles are randomized each round, solitary volunteering by the strong actor dominates, also leading to high efficiency. When distributional conflict is present (fixed roles), efficiency is somewhat lower due to reluctance of strong actors to always volunteer. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and thus provides no evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any condition. However, it is informative about baseline efficiency levels in repeated volunteer's dilemma games under various structural conditions (symmetry/asymmetry, matching protocol). For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions for step-level public good games with similar parameters, but cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. It is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Skatova, A., Spence, A., Leygue, C., & Ferguson, E. (2017). Guilty repair sustains cooperation, angry retaliation destroys it. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated social dilemma game without punishment, group efficiency (measured as group earnings and energy savings) declines over time due to increased defection and retaliation. Retaliation (increasing usage after being exploited) is associated with lower group payoffs, while repair (reducing usage after overusing) is associated with higher group payoffs. Groups with more prosocial members (who are more likely to repair and less likely to retaliate) achieve higher efficiency. Emotional responses (anger and guilt) mediate these behaviors: anger leads to retaliation, guilt leads to repair. The absence of punishment means that emotional self-regulation, especially guilt-driven repair, is the main mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated public-goods-like games without punishment, efficiency tends to decline over time due to retaliation and lack of repair. The presence of prosocial individuals who engage in guilty repair can partially sustain efficiency, but overall, the lack of punishment leads to lower group payoffs. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that in the absence of punishment, efficiency will be lower and decline over time, especially in groups with more selfish individuals. The findings are most relevant for control conditions (no punishment), and highlight the importance of emotional and social preference heterogeneity. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment, so it cannot inform the incremental effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Mak, V., Zwick, R., Rao, A. R., & Pattaratanakun, J. A. (2015). Pay what you want as threshold public good provision. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that in a repeated threshold public good environment, pre-payment chat (communication) among players enables highly efficient coordination, resulting in sustained provision of the public good (PWYW) for most rounds. Without chat, groups fail to coordinate and the public good is rarely sustained.
