The effect of chat is robust to variations in feedback (full vs. partial information about others' payments) and market information (knowledge of player types). The main outcome is group efficiency, operationalized as the proportion of rounds in which the threshold is met and the public good continues. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism; instead, efficiency is achieved through communication and social contracting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in threshold public good games without punishment, enabling pre-play communication (chat) can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when other forms of information are limited. For prediction tasks, the presence of chat should be treated as a major moderator of efficiency in repeated threshold public good environments. The absence of punishment means the results are not directly informative about the effect of punishment per se, but they do establish a high-efficiency baseline for games with communication and no punishment. Control efficiency (no chat) is low; treatment efficiency (with chat) is high. Other design dimensions (player count, MPCR, etc.) are held constant, so the main actionable dimension is chat.

- source: Efferson, C., Roca, C. P., Vogt, S., & Helbing, D. (2016). Sustained cooperation by running away from bad behavior. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing players to move away from their current neighborhood (endogenous movement) in a spatial social dilemma leads to higher cooperation rates and, crucially, eliminates the average payoff disadvantage of cooperation compared to defection. This is achieved through positive assortment: cooperators cluster together, shielding themselves from defectors. In the control condition (assign location), where movement is exogenous, cooperation remains costly and less frequent. The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so the results are about migration/partner choice as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Instead, it demonstrates that allowing migration/partner choice can eliminate the payoff disadvantage of cooperation, even without punishment. For prediction tasks about punishment effects, this paper is not directly informative, but it does highlight that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., migration) can also support efficiency. If a game design includes migration but not punishment, this paper is highly relevant; if the focus is on punishment, it is not.

- source: Feltovich, N., & Grossman, P. J. (2015). How does the effect of pre-play suggestions vary with group size? Experimental evidence from a threshold public-good game. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that pre-play cheap-talk communication (suggestions) significantly increases cooperation, the frequency of efficient (all-cooperate) outcomes, and group payoffs in a one-shot threshold public-good game, but only for small groups. As group size increases, the positive effect of communication on efficiency and payoffs declines rapidly, becoming statistically insignificant or negative for groups of size 7 and 15. This pattern holds for both one-way (random leader) and all-way (full broadcast) communication treatments. The results are robust to controls for risk preferences and other covariates. The study highlights the importance of group size as a moderator of communication's effectiveness in promoting efficient outcomes in public-good-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of communication (pre-play suggestions) on efficiency in threshold public-good games is highly dependent on group size. For small groups (n=2,3), enabling communication can substantially increase efficiency relative to control. For larger groups (n=7,15), the effect is minimal or even negative. When predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, group size should be treated as a key moderator of communication's impact. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means these results are most applicable to settings where only communication is varied. The findings suggest that extrapolating positive communication effects from small-group experiments to larger groups is not warranted.

- source: McEvoy, D. M., Cherry, T. L., & Stranlund, J. K. (2015). Endogenous Minimum Participation in International Environmental Agreements: An Experimental Analysis. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when the efficient coalition size is the full group (6 of 6), groups adopt the efficient minimum membership requirement about 77% of the time, and efficient coalitions form in over 90% of those cases, resulting in high efficiency (87.4%). When the efficient coalition is a subset (3 of 6), the efficient threshold is also adopted about 77% of the time, but efficient coalitions form only about half the time, and efficiency is lower (79.6%). Blocking of efficient coalitions is observed, especially in the partial coalition treatment, and is attributed to inequality aversion. The endogenous minimum participation mechanism is effective at facilitating cooperation and efficiency when full participation is required, but less so when partial participation allows for free riding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, so it does not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. However, it provides high-quality evidence on the effects of endogenous minimum participation requirements as a coordination device. The results suggest that such mechanisms can achieve high efficiency, especially when the efficient outcome requires full participation. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding alternative institutional mechanisms for promoting cooperation and efficiency.

- source: Becchetti, L., Conzo, P., & Degli Antoni, G. (2015). Public disclosure of players' conduct and common resources harvesting: experimental evidence from a Nairobi slum. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a repeated CPRG with fixed groups and no punishment, providing public feedback on individual withdrawals and payoffs (vs. private feedback only) increases average withdrawal rates by about 12 percentage points (from 62.7% to 74.3%), significantly reducing group efficiency and total payoff. The divergence between conditions grows over rounds. The effect is robust to controls for individual and group characteristics. The authors attribute the effect to conditional cooperation/conformity: when information is public, individuals tend to match the group's average behavior, but this leads to convergence at higher withdrawal (lower cooperation) levels. The results support Ostrom's hypothesis that, without formal sanctions, monitoring alone can undermine cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated public goods or common pool resource games without punishment, increasing transparency or monitoring (public feedback on individual actions and payoffs) can reduce efficiency and group payoff, especially in low social capital environments. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, when punishment is not available, enabling public information about individual behavior is likely to decrease efficiency relative to a private information baseline. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with and without public feedback, but do not inform the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in either condition. The results highlight that monitoring alone is not a substitute for punishment in sustaining cooperation.

- source: Brekke, K. A., Konow, J., & Nyborg, K. (2017). Framing in a threshold public goods experiment with heterogeneous endowments. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The experiment finds that high-endowed subjects contribute more in absolute terms than low-endowed subjects, but both groups contribute similar proportions of their endowments except when the contribution metric is framed in absolute terms, in which case low-endowed subjects contribute significantly more (about 80% of their endowment). The vast majority of groups reach the threshold for public good provision, often in the first period. Framing effects are present for low-endowed subjects but not for high-endowed subjects. Distributive preferences explain some of the variation in contributions, especially for high-endowed subjects, but do not explain the framing effect for low-endowed subjects. No punishment or sanctioning is present or studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it does provide informative context on how framing and endowment heterogeneity affect contribution behavior and project success rates in threshold public good games with communication. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline contribution behavior and the role of framing in similar environments without punishment.

- source: Capraro, V., Jordan, J. J., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Heuristics guide the implementation of social preferences in one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma experiments. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main findings are that in a one-shot, continuous-strategy PD, cooperation is trimodally distributed (none, half, all). Increasing the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c) increases the average amount of cooperation by shifting people from no cooperation to full cooperation, but does not affect the proportion of 50% cooperators. The results suggest that both efficiency concerns and heuristic reasoning (such as fairness heuristics) guide cooperation. There is no analysis of punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The study is relevant for understanding how b/c affects cooperation in two-player, one-shot, continuous public goods settings, but does not inform about punishment or efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide informative evidence about how the marginal per-capita return (b/c) affects cooperation rates in a two-player, one-shot, continuous-contribution setting without punishment. It shows that higher b/c increases cooperation, but a substantial fraction of participants use heuristics (e.g., giving half) regardless of b/c. Since there is no punishment or efficiency outcome reported, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation as a function of b/c in the absence of punishment.

- source: Antinyan, A., Corazzini, L., & Neururer, D. (2015). Public good provision, punishment, and the endowment origin: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The presence of two less privileged (LP) members—either with lower productivity (lower MPCR) or with no feedback information—reduces average group contributions compared to the baseline with no LPs. The type of LP affects the pattern of contribution decay and spillovers: less productive LPs contribute least and trigger lower contributions in the group, while LPs without feedback information are exploited by their neighbors but themselves contribute more. The positioning of LPs (close vs. distant) also affects contribution dynamics, especially in the lower productivity treatment. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare directly, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. Its findings on heterogeneity and feedback information may be contextually informative for understanding moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The main outcomes are contribution rates, not efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Chung, D., Yun, K., & Jeong, J. (2015). Decoding covert motivations of free riding and cooperation from multi-feature pattern analysis of EEG signals. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that neural oscillations measured by EEG immediately after result presentation can predict whether a participant will cooperate or free ride in the next round, across three different payoff structures that manipulate motivations (fear and greed). Behaviorally, free riding rates are highest in the standard condition, lower in the 'no fear' condition, and lowest in the 'no greed' condition. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. It may provide some indirect context about how payoff structure (fear/greed) affects cooperation rates, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Mussel, P., & Hewig, J. (2016). The life and times of individuals scoring high and low on dispositional greed. *Journal of Research in Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that individuals with higher dispositional greed are more likely to behave selfishly in a common goods game (taking more from a shared resource) and in a dictator game (keeping more money for themselves). Greed also predicts higher risk-taking, stronger affective responses to monetary outcomes, and higher financial aspirations. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it analyze group efficiency, welfare, or surplus. The focus is on individual differences in greed and their behavioral manifestations, not on institutional or design features of the games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related institutional features. Its findings are limited to the relationship between dispositional greed and selfish behavior in social dilemma games. Therefore, it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding the impact of punishment on efficiency as a function of game design dimensions.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Rand, D. G., Arbesman, S., Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2013). Contagion of Cooperation in Static and Fluid Social Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in fixed and viscous networks, both cooperation and defection are contagious: individuals are more likely to switch to cooperation (or defection) if their neighbors did so in the previous round. In fluid networks, where players can more frequently change their partners, cooperation is not initially contagious—players are likely to cooperate regardless of their neighbors' behavior, likely to attract new cooperative partners. As the network stabilizes and becomes less fluid, contagion of cooperation emerges. Defection remains contagious in all network types. The paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show how network structure and partner choice affect the contagion of cooperation and defection in binary cooperation games. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as context for how network fluidity and partner choice may interact with behavioral contagion. It does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Cardador, M. T., Northcraft, G. B., Rockmann, K. W., & Grant, B. C. (2016). Characteristics of affected third parties and cooperative behavior in social dilemmas. *Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The presence of a needy third party increases individual cooperative behavior (contributions) in a public-goods-like game. If the third party is seen as unreliable, this effect is diminished, and cooperation returns to baseline (no third party) levels. The effect is mediated by prosocial motivation and, in some cases, intra-group trust. The amount of money raised for the third party (group total contributions) is higher when the third party is needy and reliable. No punishment or reward mechanisms are studied, and efficiency or group payoff relative to the optimum is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public-goods games. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide evidence that non-structural, prosocial motivational interventions (making third-party beneficiaries salient) can increase cooperation in the absence of punishment. The game design is a standard linear public goods game with four players, one-shot, no punishment, no reward, and no communication. The findings may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation rates in control conditions without punishment, but not for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Boosey, L. A. (2017). Conditional cooperation in network public goods experiments. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a network public goods game, average contributions decline over time, with the rate and extent of decay influenced by the type of information feedback and the composition of the group. Feedback about payoffs (rather than contributions) leads to lower contributions. The presence of a free-rider accelerates the decay of contributions, especially for players closer to the free-rider in the network. Unconditional full contributors ('good eggs') can slow the decline in contributions for their immediate neighbors, but do not induce convergence to full cooperation. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is informative about the baseline dynamics of contribution decay and the influence of information feedback and group composition in networked PGGs. For prediction tasks, this paper can help establish expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency in networked environments, especially regarding the impact of feedback format and the presence of free-riders or full contributors. It does not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Frey, U. J. (2017). Cooperative strategies outside the laboratory - evidence from a long-term large-N-study in five countries. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a context-rich, long-term, repeated threshold public goods environment (an online game), the distribution of cooperative strategies differs from laboratory public goods games: free riding is as common as in the lab, but high cooperation is less frequent and conditional cooperation is somewhat less common. These strategies are stable over time and across different group contexts. The results suggest that context matters for the prevalence of cooperative strategies, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor on the effects of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the baseline prevalence and stability of cooperative strategies in a long-term, context-rich threshold public goods environment without punishment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that in the absence of punishment, free riding remains common, high cooperation is rare, and conditional cooperation is less prevalent than in some lab settings. The findings may help calibrate expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar environments, but do not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Balliet, D., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Trust, Conflict, and Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis. *Psychological Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both state trust (expectations of others' cooperation) and dispositional trust are positively correlated with cooperation in social dilemmas. The strength of this relationship is moderated by the degree of conflict in the game: trust is a stronger predictor of cooperation when the conflict of interest is higher (lower index of cooperation). The type of social dilemma (public goods, prisoner's dilemma, resource dilemma) does not significantly moderate the trust-cooperation relationship. Other moderators (iterations, group size, country, intergroup vs. individual) are also explored. The paper does not analyze or report on punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide informative context about how the structure of the game (degree of conflict, as measured by the index of cooperation) moderates the relationship between trust and cooperation. Since efficiency is not measured, and punishment is not studied, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, but may be useful for understanding how baseline cooperation varies with game structure in the absence of punishment.

- source: dos Santos, M., Braithwaite, V. A., & Wedekind, C. (2014). Exposure to superfluous information reduces cooperation and increases antisocial punishment in reputation-based interactions. *Frontiers in Ecology and Evolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In reputation-based helping games (without punishment), cognitive disturbance (superfluous words) reduces cooperation and lowers group payoffs, indicating that efficiency is sensitive to cognitive constraints. In reputation-based punishment games, disturbance increases antisocial punishment and reduces punishment of defectors, but does not significantly change overall cooperation or mean final payoffs. The link between reputation and payoff is weakened under disturbance. The study highlights that experimental outcomes in reputation and punishment games are highly sensitive to environmental and cognitive factors, and that the expected efficiency gains from punishment or reputation mechanisms may not materialize under cognitive load.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency effects of punishment and reputation mechanisms in indirect reciprocity games are sensitive to cognitive context. For prediction tasks, it suggests that efficiency gains from punishment (or reputation) may be undermined or nullified if cognitive load is high, even when game design parameters are otherwise favorable. The results also show that the presence of punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency: the effect depends on how punishment is used (e.g., antisocial vs. prosocial punishment) and on whether players can process reputational information. When using control efficiency to predict treatment efficiency, one should consider that cognitive or informational disturbances can reduce or eliminate the positive effects of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Xiao, E., & Kunreuther, H. (2016). Punishment and Cooperation in Stochastic Social Dilemmas. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In two-person repeated prisoner's dilemma games, both deterministic and stochastic (with outcome uncertainty), the restricted punishment mechanism (only cooperators can punish defectors) increases cooperation rates compared to no-punishment and unrestricted punishment. However, the efficiency gains (measured by average earnings) from restricted punishment are significant only in deterministic environments; in stochastic environments, restricted punishment does not significantly increase average earnings compared to no-punishment. Unrestricted punishment leads to frequent retaliation and antisocial punishment, resulting in lower average earnings than the baseline in both environments. The effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and efficiency is reduced by outcome uncertainty, as noncooperators are less likely to be punished when outcomes are stochastic.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on both the structure of the punishment mechanism (restricted vs unrestricted) and the presence of outcome uncertainty (stochastic vs deterministic). In games with two players, repeated for 10 rounds, with perfect information and no communication, restricted punishment can increase efficiency in deterministic settings but not in stochastic ones. Unrestricted punishment can reduce efficiency due to retaliation and antisocial punishment. For prediction, the presence of outcome uncertainty and the type of punishment mechanism are key moderators: restricted punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when outcomes are deterministic and less so when outcomes are stochastic. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is similar in both environments, but the efficiency boost from punishment is context-dependent.

- source: Kimbrough, E. O., & Rubin, J. (2015). Sustaining Group Reputation. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated trust game with random rematching and group reputation, the introduction of an in-group punishment mechanism (jury) leads to a significant increase in the amount sent by senders, which directly translates to higher total welfare/efficiency. Information sharing about group members' experiences also increases welfare, but mainly by reinforcing positive or negative group reputations (path dependence). The effects of punishment and information sharing are additive rather than superadditive or substitutive. The presence of punishment increases both the amount sent and the amount returned, with the largest gains in the treatment where both punishment and information sharing are present. The effect of punishment is primarily due to deterrence—the threat of being punished is enough to change behavior, not just actual punishment events.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments with group reputation and random rematching, enabling a punishment mechanism (with moderate cost and effectiveness) increases efficiency (total welfare) relative to a control with punishment disabled. The effect is robust and additive with information sharing. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be higher than with punishment disabled, especially when punishment is feasible and not prohibitively costly. The results are most directly applicable to trust games but are relevant for adjacent public-goods-like settings where group reputation and in-group punishment are possible. The paper also provides evidence that the marginal per-capita return (here, the tripling of the amount sent) is important for welfare, and that communication (cheap talk) alone does not have a significant effect. The design dimensions most critical for prediction are the presence and cost of punishment, information sharing, and the structure of the trust game.

- source: Lergetporer, P., Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., & Sutter, M. (2014). Third-party punishment increases cooperation in children through (misaligned) expectations and conditional cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that the threat of third-party punishment in a one-shot PD with children increases cooperation rates from 25% (control) to 58% (punishment treatment). This leads to an increase in average payoffs from 2.49 tokens (control) to 2.98 tokens (punishment), a 20% improvement in efficiency. The effect is not due to frequent actual punishment (which is rare), but rather to children's (over-)expectation of punishment and to conditional cooperation based on higher expected partner cooperation. The effect is robust across age cohorts (7-11 years).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment in a two-player, one-shot PD with children substantially increases efficiency, even when actual punishment is rare. The effect is mediated by beliefs and conditional cooperation. For prediction, the presence of a third-party punishment mechanism (with a strong, binary punishment technology) in a simple, anonymous, one-shot, two-player game with no communication and known payoffs can be expected to increase efficiency by a substantial margin (here, ~20%). The effect is robust to age (within 7-11), and is not dependent on actual punishment frequency. The design is adjacent to a PGG, but the payoff structure and mechanism are closely related.

- source: Wang, Z., Jusup, M., Wang, R. W., Shi, L., Iwasa, Y., Moreno, Y., & Kurths, J. (2017). Onymity promotes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma with three available actions (cooperate, defect, punish), the use of costly punishment is negatively correlated with individual payoffs in both anonymous and onymous conditions. Onymity (making opponents identifiable) increases cooperation and median payoffs, but punishment does not promote efficiency or cooperation. Instead, those who punish more tend to earn less ('winners don't punish' effect). Control trials without punishment show similar qualitative effects of onymity, suggesting that punishment is not necessary for increased cooperation or efficiency. The paper provides direct evidence that punishment, as implemented here, does not improve group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma games with costly punishment, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency or group payoff. In fact, punishment is associated with lower payoffs for those who use it. The findings suggest that, all else equal, adding a costly punishment option to a similar game design is unlikely to increase efficiency, and may even reduce it. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, discrete-choice social dilemmas with costly punishment, and should be interpreted with caution when generalizing to multi-player public goods games. However, the negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust in this setting.

- source: Roux, C., & Thöni, C. (2015). Collusion among many firms: The disciplinary power of targeted punishment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The availability of targeted punishment in Cournot oligopoly experiments leads to significantly higher group profits and lower total output (closer to the collusive benchmark) for all group sizes tested (2, 4, 6, 8). The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive and increases with group size beyond duopoly: in 6- and 8-firm markets, mean profits nearly double with punishment. Punishment is used in a targeted and proportional way, with defectors facing harsher penalties as group size increases. The study provides direct evidence that targeted punishment can sustain high-efficiency collusion even in large groups, contrary to standard theory and previous experimental results without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that enabling targeted punishment in repeated Cournot (oligopoly) games leads to large increases in group efficiency (profit), especially as group size increases. The design is not a standard public goods game but is closely related in structure and in the punishment mechanism. The results suggest that, for games with similar punishment technology (high impact, targeted, known cost), the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be much higher than in the control (no-punishment) condition, and the effect is larger for larger groups. The findings are highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions, especially for settings with targeted, high-impact punishment and no communication.

- source: Moser, S., & Musshoff, O. (2016). Ex-ante Evaluation of Policy Measures: Effects of Reward and Punishment for Fertiliser Reduction in Palm Oil Production. *Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, holding expected income effects constant, the design of incentives (reward vs. punishment, certain vs. uncertain) significantly affects both behavioral response and efficiency. Certain rewards are the most efficient (i.e., minimize profit loss for a given reduction in fertiliser use), while uncertain punishments are the least efficient. Certain punishments and uncertain rewards are intermediate. Punishment is less efficient than reward, and uncertainty reduces efficiency. The findings suggest that policymakers aiming for efficient reduction of negative externalities should prefer certain rewards over punishments or uncertain incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public-good-like field context, the efficiency impact of punishment (vs. reward) depends strongly on the design: punishment is less efficient than reward, and uncertainty in the incentive (probabilistic punishment or reward) further reduces efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment (especially uncertain punishment) will not increase efficiency as much as enabling certain rewards, even if the expected value is held constant. The results are most applicable to field settings with individual decision-making and externalities, but less so to classic multi-player PGGs with peer punishment. The efficiency outcome is measured as profit difference, directly supporting payoff-based prediction.

- source: Grimalda, G., Pondorfer, A., & Tracer, D. P. (2016). Social image concerns promote cooperation more than altruistic punishment. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, two-player prisoner's dilemma, the presence of a local authority (Big Man) as an observer increases cooperation and leads to the highest average payoffs, exceeding baseline. Introducing a costly punishment stage (where players can spend to reduce the other's payoff) does not increase cooperation above the social image condition and results in lower average payoffs than baseline, due to the costs incurred by both punisher and punished. When both social image and punishment are present, efficiency is still lower than with social image alone and remains below baseline. The presence of an external (outgroup) authority does not increase cooperation or payoffs. Thus, punishment is not an efficient mechanism for promoting cooperation in this setting, and social image concerns are more effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot, two-player PD with costly punishment, enabling punishment reduces efficiency compared to baseline (no punishment). The effect is robust: both punishment alone and punishment combined with social image concerns yield lower payoffs than baseline. The design is not a standard public goods game but is closely related (two-player PD with all-or-nothing contribution). The findings suggest that, for similar game designs (one-shot, small group, all-or-nothing, high punishment cost), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency, even if it increases cooperation rates. The control (baseline) efficiency is higher than the punishment treatment efficiency. This evidence should be weighted heavily when predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Wilson, A. J., & Wu, H. (2017). At-will relationships: How an option to walk away affects cooperation and efficiency. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Introducing a termination (walk-away) option in repeated PD games increases cooperation and efficiency when the outside option is low, as termination is rarely used and serves as a credible threat. However, as the outside option rises above the in-relationship minmax, subjects begin to use termination more frequently as a punishment, leading to a sharp drop in efficiency. If the outside option increases further, efficiency rises again, as the cost of termination is less severe. Asymmetric division rules for termination payoffs have dramatic effects: if the party who terminates gets the higher payoff, almost all relationships dissolve immediately, resulting in very low efficiency. If the more cooperative party is rewarded, cooperation and efficiency are maximized. The paper provides direct evidence on how the structure and value of punishment options (termination) affect efficiency in repeated social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment (here, the option to terminate a relationship) on efficiency is highly sensitive to the value and distribution of the punishment's payoff. When punishment is costly (low outside option), its mere availability increases efficiency, but if it becomes too attractive (high outside option), it is overused and efficiency drops. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the presence and value of the punishment option, the symmetry/asymmetry of its payoff, and the underlying game structure (here, repeated PD with imperfect monitoring). The findings suggest that the efficiency impact of enabling punishment depends non-monotonically on the cost/benefit of punishment, and that institutional details (who gets what on punishment/termination) can have very large effects. For prediction, the control efficiency (without punishment) and the specific design of the punishment option are critical moderators.

- source: Kopányi-Peuker, A., Offerman, T., & Sloof, R. (2017). Fostering cooperation through the enhancement of own vulnerability. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing players to endogenously make themselves vulnerable to punishment (i.e., to enable the partner to punish them at a cost) increases cooperation and efficiency in a repeated prisoner's dilemma compared to a control with no punishment. The Gradual mechanism, where vulnerability can be increased stepwise and conditionally, leads to higher mutual cooperation and efficiency than the Leap mechanism, where vulnerability is chosen in one step. Efficiency gains are only observed when the vulnerability choice is endogenous and can be used as a signal; exogenous assignment of punishment possibilities does not improve and may even reduce efficiency. The effect is driven by the signaling value of voluntary vulnerability, not by the mere presence of punishment. The results suggest that the design of punishment mechanisms—especially the ability to signal intent and coordinate—critically moderates their effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling endogenous, voluntary punishment (where players can signal intent by making themselves vulnerable) increases efficiency in repeated prisoner's dilemma games compared to no-punishment controls. The effect is stronger when the mechanism allows gradual, conditional increases in vulnerability (i.e., more opportunity for signaling and coordination). The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of endogenous, voluntary punishment mechanisms—especially those allowing conditional, stepwise commitment—should be expected to yield higher efficiency than both no-punishment and exogenous punishment settings. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated prisoner's dilemma games, but the mechanism is adjacent to public goods games. The effect is contingent on the ability to signal intent; mere punishment possibility without signaling does not improve efficiency.

- source: Wright, J. (2013). Punishment strategies in repeated games: Evidence from experimental markets. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that the vast majority of subjects do not use harsh, disproportionate punishment strategies. Instead, most use graduated or lenient strategies, such as Tit-for-tat, which respond proportionally to deviations. Regression analysis shows that adopting Tit-for-tat leads to significantly higher present discounted value of earnings than harsher punishment strategies, even after controlling for past earnings. Communication between subjects increases the likelihood of cooperation (high prices) but does not significantly increase the use of harsh punishment. The findings suggest that in repeated games with rich action spaces, softer punishment strategies are more effective for sustaining cooperation and maximizing group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in repeated games with a rich action space (such as price competition), enabling punishment does not require harsh, maximal punishment to achieve high efficiency. Instead, proportionate, graduated punishment (e.g., Tit-for-tat) is more effective for maximizing group payoffs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a punishment mechanism will likely increase efficiency, but the effect depends on the nature of punishment strategies adopted. Communication further supports cooperation but does not necessarily increase the use of harsh punishment. The findings are most directly relevant to repeated duopoly/oligopoly settings, but the payoff-based results are informative for adjacent public-goods-like environments.

- source: De Dreu, C. K. W., Gross, J., Méder, Z., Giffin, M., Prochazkova, E., Krikeb, J., & Columbus, S. (2016). In-group defense, out-group aggression, and coordination failures in intergroup conflict. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Peer punishment increases contributions to out-group aggression but does not improve the aggressor group's success rate or overall group payoffs; it leads to more wasted resources and lower individual wealth. In contrast, sequential decision-making (a coordination mechanism) increases coordination within aggressor groups, nearly doubles their success rate, and reduces wealth loss for aggressors (but increases it for defenders). The results suggest that punishment can motivate higher contributions but does not solve the coordination problem or improve efficiency, while coordination mechanisms can enhance group success and payoffs in competitive intergroup settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in intergroup contest games with costly contributions and the possibility of peer punishment, enabling punishment increases contributions but does not improve group efficiency or payoffs—indeed, it can reduce them due to the direct costs of punishment. Coordination mechanisms (like sequential decision-making) are more effective at improving group success and payoffs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment (with the cost/impact structure used here) should not be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, and may even decrease it. The effect of punishment is highly sensitive to the coordination context and the cost/impact structure of punishment.

- source: dos Santos, M., Rankin, D. J., & Wedekind, C. (2013). HUMAN COOPERATION BASED ON PUNISHMENT REPUTATION. *Evolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the ability to punish defectors, combined with the visibility of punishment reputation, leads to higher cooperation and higher group payoffs. Punishers are compensated for their costs through increased cooperation from others, and in some groups, punishers even achieve net benefits. When punishment or its reputation is removed, cooperation and payoffs decline. The effect is driven by the threat of punishment and the reputational benefits it brings, not by the mere display of past punishment behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment, especially when punishment reputation is visible, increases group payoffs and compensates punishers, thus improving efficiency-related outcomes. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment and the visibility of punishment reputation are key moderators. The game is not a standard public goods game but a helping game with punishment and reputation, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs should be cautious, but the qualitative effect of punishment on efficiency is robustly positive when reputation is present.

- source: Zhao, K., Ferguson, E., & Smillie, L. D. (2017). Politeness and Compassion Differentially Predict Adherence to Fairness Norms and Interventions to Norm Violations in Economic Games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that politeness and honesty-humility predict fair allocations in the dictator game, while compassion predicts third-party recompensation (helping the victim) and, to a lesser extent, third-party punishment (punishing the offender). Most third-party punishers are 'prosocial' (they also behave fairly as dictators), and both punishment and recompensation increase with the degree of unfairness observed. However, the study does not analyze or report group efficiency, total welfare, or surplus, and the game structure is not a public goods game but a dictator/third-party punishment/recompensation design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it offers insight into the psychological predictors of punishment and recompensation behaviors in third-party games, it does not report group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze public goods game design dimensions such as MPCR, player count, or rounds. Its findings are only indirectly relevant for understanding the motivations behind punishment and helping behaviors, not their impact on group efficiency.

- source: Fiedler, M., & Haruvy, E. (2017). The effect of third party intervention in the trust game. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a trust game, third party monitoring increases investment and return rates compared to no third party. Adding punishment or reward capabilities to the third party does not significantly increase aggregate cooperation or efficiency beyond monitoring alone, especially in random partner settings. In fixed partner settings, punishment may have a somewhat greater effect, but the difference is not large. Individual players are responsive to punishment and reward, but the overall aggregate effect is muted because the actual use of punishment/reward is infrequent and the absence of punishment/reward can itself act as reinforcement. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, but the design allows for inference about total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in trust game environments (which are adjacent to public goods games), third party punishment does not substantially increase efficiency or aggregate payoff beyond what is achieved by third party monitoring alone, especially when partners are randomly matched. The effect of punishment is more pronounced in fixed partner settings, but still not dramatically higher than monitoring. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar trust/investment games, enabling punishment may not yield large efficiency gains over monitoring, particularly with random matching. However, the results may not transfer directly to public goods games, and efficiency is not directly measured.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., Kurzban, R., & McCullough, M. E. (2013). Do humans really punish altruistically? A closer look. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a lab setting where third-party punishment is possible but demand characteristics and audience effects are minimized, witnesses of unfairness toward others rarely engage in costly punishment. When they do, it is not motivated by moralistic anger but by envy. Victims of unfairness (those personally harmed) do punish at nontrivial rates. In hypothetical scenarios, people forecast much more punishment and anger than is observed in real behavior. The results challenge the robustness of altruistic third-party punishment in humans and suggest that previous findings may be due to experimental artefacts. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in dictator/third-party punishment games where the punisher is not personally harmed, actual costly punishment is rare and mild, and not motivated by norm-enforcing anger. This suggests that enabling punishment in similar games is unlikely to increase efficiency or group payoff, since punishment is rarely used and not directed at norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, and the findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is only available to third parties who are not personally affected. The results caution against assuming that punishment will improve efficiency in such designs.

- source: Konishi, N., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2015). Does dishonesty really invite third-party punishment? Results of a more stringent test. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, even after removing five key methodological confounds (including adding a reward option and ensuring anonymity), a significant minority of third-party observers are willing to incur a personal cost to punish a dishonest trustee, even when the trustee's allocation is fair. The rate of punishment is lower than in previous studies (27% vs. 53%), but still significantly higher in the dishonesty condition than in the honesty condition. Participants were more likely to punish than reward in response to dishonesty. The main outcomes are the frequency and cost of punishment/reward, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that third-party punishment of norm violations (specifically dishonesty) can occur even when methodological confounds are controlled. However, the design is a modified trust game with third-party punishment and reward, not a public goods game or direct group efficiency context. The outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward rates and costs), not efficiency or group payoff. Thus, while the paper supports the robustness of third-party punishment as a behavioral phenomenon, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main value is in showing that punishment can persist even when alternative explanations are minimized, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on group efficiency.

- source: Whitson, J. A., Wang, C. S., See, Y. H. M., Baker, W. E., & Murnighan, J. K. (2015). How, when, and why recipients and observers reward good deeds and punish bad deeds. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party observers are more likely to punish bad deeds (e.g., deception) than direct recipients, and that direct recipients are more likely to reward good deeds (e.g., honesty) than observers. These effects are mediated by feelings of obligation: observers feel a stronger obligation to punish, while recipients feel a stronger obligation to reward. The studies use one-shot, controlled scenarios with real monetary stakes, but do not involve repeated interaction, group provision, or public goods. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward assigned), not group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and behavioral drivers of punishment and reward in one-shot, dyadic or triadic settings, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that the presence of observers may increase the likelihood and intensity of punishment for norm violations, but do not inform how this affects group payoffs or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly about who is likely to punish and under what circumstances, not about the payoff consequences of punishment.

- source: Leibbrandt, A., & López-Pérez, R. (2014). Different carrots and different sticks: do we reward and punish differently than we approve and disapprove?. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that monetary punishment is used more often and more intensely than non-monetary disapproval, but monetary reward and non-monetary approval are used at similar rates. The determinants of monetary punishment/reward are primarily payoff-based (inequity aversion), while non-monetary approval/disapproval is more strongly driven by reciprocity and norm-based considerations. Strict payoff equality reduces disapproval and increases approval. The experiment is not a repeated public goods game but a set of one-shot, two-player allocation games, so efficiency or group payoff outcomes are not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and motivational underpinnings of punishment and reward, distinguishing between monetary and non-monetary forms. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes in a repeated public goods context, nor does it manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. Its main value is in clarifying that the impact of punishment/reward on behavior may depend on whether the mechanism is monetary or non-monetary, and that motivations differ. For direct prediction of efficiency effects in PGGs, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Bone, J. E., Wallace, B., Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2016). Power Asymmetries and Punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma with Variable Cooperative Investment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma with variable cooperative investment and a punishment option, punishment did not increase the likelihood that targets would increase their cooperation, regardless of whether the punisher was strong or weak, or whether the game was symmetric or asymmetric. Both weak and strong players retaliated against punishment, and the threat of punishment from strong players may have led to higher baseline cooperation from weak players, but actual punishment had no effect on subsequent cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral responses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in two-player games with variable investment and a punishment option, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency, even when punishment is more effective for some players (power asymmetry). However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The behavioral results suggest that, in similar two-player designs, enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency and may provoke retaliation. The findings are most relevant for adjacent game designs (iterated PD with variable investment) and may be less transferable to standard public goods games or multiplayer settings.

- source: Martin, J. W., & Cushman, F. (2015). To Punish or to Leave: Distinct Cognitive Processes Underlie Partner Control and Partner Choice Behaviors. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a dyadic allocation game with stochastic outcomes, punishment (partner control) is influenced by both the allocator's intent and the actual outcome, while partner choice (switching partners) is influenced almost exclusively by intent. The design allows for the separation of intent and outcome by having allocators choose between dice with different probabilities of fair/selfish outcomes. Responders in the punishment condition could add or subtract money (costlessly, with low probability of effect), while in the partner choice condition they could choose to stay or switch partners. The main dependent variables are the behavioral responses and their sensitivity to intent/outcome. No group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games. Its main contribution is to clarify the psychological determinants of punishment and partner choice behaviors, showing that punishment is sensitive to both intent and outcome, while partner choice is intent-driven. The game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency or total payoff is not analyzed. Thus, the paper offers little direct support for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, but may inform understanding of when punishment is likely to be used and what cues drive it.

- source: FeldmanHall, O., Sokol-Hessner, P., Van Bavel, J. J., & Phelps, E. A. (2014). Fairness violations elicit greater punishment on behalf of another than for oneself. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that, contrary to the assumption that punishment is the default response to fairness violations, individuals strongly prefer compensation over punishment when both options are available. This preference holds even when punishment is costless. However, when participants make decisions on behalf of others (third-party perspective), they are more likely to choose punitive options that both compensate the victim and punish the transgressor. The findings suggest that the context and available options significantly shape justice-restoration preferences. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its findings are not directly usable for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in environments where participants can choose between punishment and non-punitive justice-restoration options, punishment is not the preferred response to unfairness. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the game is a modified ultimatum game rather than a public goods game. Therefore, while the findings suggest that the presence of alternative options may reduce the use of punishment, the paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in public goods or similar games. Its main value is in highlighting that behavioral responses to punishment options are context-dependent and that efficiency predictions should consider the available choice set.

- source: Liu, J., & Riyanto, Y. E. (2017). Information transparency and equilibrium selection in coordination games: an experimental study. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Revealing information about the existence and structure of centralized punishment or reward mechanisms in a stag hunt game increases the rate of mutual cooperation (the payoff-dominant equilibrium) compared to a control where only final payoffs are shown. The effect is stronger for punishment than for reward. The mechanism works both by increasing beliefs in others' cooperation and, for punishment, by directly increasing cooperative choices. In contrast, when the underlying game remains a prisoner's dilemma (i.e., the punishment/reward is too small to change the equilibrium), revealing the mechanism does not increase cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the increase in mutual cooperation implies higher group welfare in the context of the stag hunt game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that, in two-player, repeated stag hunt games with centralized punishment or reward, making the institutional mechanism transparent increases cooperation rates, especially when punishment is highlighted. While efficiency is not directly measured, the increase in mutual cooperation (the payoff-dominant outcome) suggests that efficiency would also increase. The results are most relevant for coordination games adjacent to public goods games, rather than standard PGGs. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment and making its mechanism salient is likely to increase efficiency relative to a control with no such information, especially when the punishment is strong enough to change the equilibrium structure.

- source: Crockett, M. J., Apergis-Schoute, A., Herrmann, B., Lieberman, M., Müller, U., Robbins, T. W., & Clark, L. (2013). Serotonin Modulates Striatal Responses to Fairness and Retaliation in Humans. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that lowering serotonin via ATD increases costly punishment (rejection of unfair offers) in the ultimatum game (second-party context), but tends to decrease costly punishment in third-party punishment games. The behavioral effects are accompanied by neural changes: ATD reduces ventral striatal responses to fairness and increases dorsal striatal responses during retaliation. The results suggest that serotonin modulates the drive for retaliation rather than fairness preferences. The games are one-shot, with no repeated interaction, and the main outcomes are behavioral and neural, not payoff-based. There is no direct analysis of efficiency or group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and neurobiological mechanisms underlying costly punishment, showing that serotonin depletion increases second-party punishment but decreases third-party punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and the games are not public goods games but rather ultimatum and third-party punishment games. The findings may inform predictions about the psychological drivers of punishment in similar environments, but do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design features (one-shot, no communication, binary accept/reject, known costs of punishment) are informative for mapping to game dimensions, but the lack of efficiency outcomes limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Pansini, R., Shi, L., & Wang, R. W. (2016). Women Tend to Defect in a Social Dilemma Game in Southwest China. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, women in Southwest China defect more and cooperate less than men, a pattern that contrasts with previous findings from Western and other Asian samples. Both genders earned less when they punished, and there was no significant gender difference in overall monetary earnings. The use of punishment did not increase individual earnings, and the paper does not analyze group efficiency or total welfare. The main findings are about behavioral strategies and gender differences, not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in a dyadic iterated Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, punishment is used more frequently than in comparable Western samples, but its use is associated with lower individual earnings. The design is not a public goods game but a two-player PD, so direct prediction for PGG efficiency is limited. The main decision support is that punishment, as implemented here, does not increase individual payoffs and may reduce them. The paper does not report group efficiency or the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited.

- source: Bone, J. E., Wallace, B., Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2015). The Effect of Power Asymmetries on Cooperation and Punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma with punishment, punishment did not increase cooperation rates. Instead, cooperation from a partner was more effective at eliciting cooperation than punishment. Punishment, especially from strong players, sometimes provoked retaliation and further defection. Weak players never retaliated against punishment, but both weak and strong players retaliated against strong punishers. Players were more likely to opt out after being punished or when facing a strong partner. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, and the authors explicitly state their focus is on behavioral rather than payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma games with punishment, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation and may even have detrimental effects on cooperative behavior, especially when power asymmetries exist. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that, at least in two-player, all-or-nothing, binary-choice games with possible opting out, punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency and may worsen behavioral outcomes. Caution should be used in generalizing to multi-player or continuous-contribution public goods games.

- source: Böckler, A., Tusche, A., & Singer, T. (2016). The Structure of Human Prosociality: Differentiating Altruistically Motivated, Norm Motivated, Strategically Motivated, and Self-Reported Prosocial Behavior. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that prosocial behavior is multidimensional, with four robust factors: altruistically motivated, norm motivated (including punishment and reciprocity), strategically motivated, and self-reported prosociality. Punishment behavior (in second and third person punishment games) loads on the 'norm motivated' factor, indicating its role in norm enforcement. The study replicates standard findings in economic games (e.g., people punish unfairness, give more in dictator than in punishment games, help more when reciprocated), but does not analyze efficiency or group payoff.
