The factor structure is stable across two large samples. Altruistic prosociality is associated with lower negative affect and higher cognitive skills. Gender, age, and income show differential associations with the factors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment behavior is a distinct, norm-motivated component of prosociality, separable from altruistic and strategic motivations. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design details of the punishment games (one-shot, anonymous, fixed punishment effectiveness) are informative for mapping to game dimensions, but the lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task. The study is most useful for understanding the motivational structure underlying punishment and cooperation, not for quantifying efficiency effects.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Schiller, B., Rieskamp, J., Gianotti, L. R., & Knoch, D. (2014). Diminishing parochialism in intergroup conflict by disrupting the right temporo-parietal junction. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is more severe toward outgroup norm violators than ingroup violators (parochial punishment), especially in cases where the outgroup member defects against an ingroup member. Disrupting the right TPJ with rTMS reduces this parochial punishment, making third-parties treat outgroup and ingroup violators more similarly. This effect is mediated by a reduction in the motive to retaliate and a reduction in mentalizing bias (the tendency to take the perspective of ingroup more than outgroup members). The manipulation does not affect punishment in intragroup constellations or in non-defection cases. The study does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying parochial punishment in third-party punishment games, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a Prisoner's Dilemma for norm violation and a third-party punishment stage, but does not involve repeated public goods contributions or measure group efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding when and why punishment is more severe toward outgroup members, and how neural interventions can reduce this bias. However, the results cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no efficiency or payoff data are reported. The punishment technology (1 point spent reduces target's payoff by 3) is specified, but the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned) and motivational (retaliation, perspective-taking), not payoff-based.

- source: Crockett, M. J., Özdemir, Y., & Fehr, E. (2014). The Value of Vengeance and the Demand for Deterrence. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study demonstrates that both retributive and deterrence motives drive costly punishment: participants punish norm violators even when the punishment is hidden and cannot deter future violations, but punishment is stronger when it can be communicated. Second-party punishment (when the punisher is the direct victim) is stronger than third-party punishment (when the punisher is an observer). The findings suggest that retributive motives can lead to punishment that does not increase social welfare and may be inefficient. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be motivated by retribution alone, leading to costly punishment even when it cannot improve future cooperation or group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency or group payoff, especially when punishment is driven by retributive motives rather than deterrence. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment. The design is adjacent to public goods games, using a trust game with punishment, so findings may transfer in terms of understanding motives but not in terms of efficiency outcomes.

- source: de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., Rijkhoff, S. A. M., & van Dijk, E. (2013). Equality as a benchmark for third-party punishment and reward: The moderating role of uncertainty in social dilemmas. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishers and rewarders use the equality rule as a benchmark for sanctioning decisions only when the size of the common resource is certain. Under certainty, those who take more than an equal share are punished much more, and those who take an equal or smaller share are rewarded more. Under uncertainty, the amount of punishment or reward is linearly related to the amount harvested, with no sharp benchmark. The emotional basis for punishment is negative affect (anger), while for reward it is positive affect. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or common resource games, as it does not report any payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides informative context about how the clarity of the cooperation norm (equality rule) and environmental uncertainty affect the application of punishment and reward. In settings where the cooperation norm is clear and known, punishment is more sharply targeted at norm violators; under uncertainty, punishment is more diffuse. This may indirectly inform predictions about the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation or efficiency, but the paper does not provide direct evidence for such effects.

- source: Ibanez, M., & Martinsson, P. (2013). Curbing coca cultivation in Colombia - A framed field experiment. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that both increased risk of punishment (eradication) and increased legal profits (subsidies) reduce investment in illegal coca cultivation, but the effect is highly heterogeneous. About one third of participants have high moral costs and do not need external incentives, while two fifths are very hard to deter. Only about a quarter are responsive to policy levers. The supply of coca is inelastic to policy changes unless eradication risk is high (>30%). Carrots (subsidies) are more effective in areas with better market integration, while sticks (punishment) are more effective in depressed areas. The study provides cost estimates for both policies and suggests that optimal policy should mix both, tailored to local conditions. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (investment choices), not group efficiency or total welfare as in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence on how exogenous punishment (risk of eradication) and reward (subsidy) policies affect individual investment in a socially harmful activity, with a design adjacent to public goods games. The findings support that punishment can reduce undesirable behavior, but the effect is highly dependent on individual heterogeneity. The results are most useful for predicting how changes in punishment and reward parameters might affect behavioral outcomes (e.g., investment in the public bad), but less directly for predicting group efficiency as defined in PGGs. The cost-effectiveness analysis and elasticity estimates can inform models that link punishment/reward design to behavioral and, indirectly, payoff outcomes, especially in field-like or framed settings with real-world stakes.

- source: Goeschl, T., & Jarke, J. (2016). Second and third party punishment under costly monitoring. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that when monitoring is free, almost all punishers are 'targeted punishers' who punish defectors more than cooperators. As monitoring becomes costly, the share of targeted punishers drops sharply, and more subjects either do not punish at all or punish indiscriminately. The average punishment for defectors falls substantially as monitoring costs rise, and the difference in punishment between defectors and cooperators (the 'incentive to cooperate') is much lower under costly monitoring. These patterns are similar for both second and third party punishment, with some subtle differences in how subjects switch between punisher types across roles. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the authors argue that lower monitoring costs should lead to more efficient outcomes by making punishment more effective at supporting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the cost of monitoring (i.e., the ability to condition punishment on observed behavior) is a critical moderator of the effectiveness of punishment in social dilemma games. When monitoring is costly, punishment is less targeted and less effective at creating incentives for cooperation. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that in games where monitoring is costly or imperfect, enabling punishment will have a weaker (or possibly negligible) effect on efficiency compared to settings with free or perfect monitoring. For prediction tasks, the presence and cost of monitoring should be considered a key dimension when estimating the efficiency impact of punishment. However, because the study does not report actual efficiency or payoff outcomes, its direct predictive value for efficiency is limited.

- source: Galbiati, R., Schlag, K. H., & van der Weele, J. J. (2013). Sanctions that signal: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that exogenous sanctions increase effort and beliefs about others' effort, with a stronger effect on effort than beliefs. Endogenous sanctions, where a principal chooses to sanction after observing first-round behavior, are less effective, especially for subjects who initially chose high effort. For low initial effort players, both types of sanctions have similar effects. The signaling effect—where the imposition of a sanction is interpreted as a signal that others are not cooperating—can counteract the incentive effect of sanctions for cooperators. The main analysis is on effort and beliefs, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that sanctions can increase effort in a minimum-effort game, but the effect depends on whether sanctions are exogenous or endogenous and on subjects' prior behavior. The signaling effect can reduce the effectiveness of sanctions for cooperators. For prediction, this suggests that the context and perceived informational content of punishment mechanisms may moderate their effect on efficiency, especially in coordination settings. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the findings should be used as informative but indirect evidence.

- source: Bigoni, M., Camera, G., & Casari, M. (2013). Strategies of cooperation and punishment among students and clerical workers. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing personal punishment increases cooperation rates in indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games for both students and workers. Students show a larger increase in cooperation and are more likely to adopt conditional cooperation strategies (e.g., forgiving or unforgiving trigger strategies) when punishment is available, while workers shift more toward unconditional cooperation. Workers are less likely to use personal punishment than students. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from the data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing personal punishment increases cooperation rates in indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games, with the effect being more pronounced among student subjects. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates and strategy adoption, not efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games (pairwise PDs in groups of four with random matching), so caution is needed in generalizing to standard PGGs.

- source: Fabbri, M., & Carbonara, E. (2017). Social influence on third-party punishment: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that social information about peer punishment decisions leads third-party punishers to revise their own punishment levels toward the peer average. This effect is robust and stronger when conformity requires increasing punishment, even though this reduces the punisher's own payoff. The addition of a normative social influence channel (emoticon feedback) does not significantly change punishment behavior compared to informational social influence alone. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral conformity in punishment decisions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that social information about peer punishment can substantially alter third-party punishment behavior, increasing conformity to peer norms. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public-goods-like environments. The design is a modified dictator game with third-party punishment, not a public goods game, and the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, conformity), not efficiency. The findings may be relevant for understanding how social information moderates punishment behavior, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Zhang, Y., Yu, H. B., Yin, Y., & Zhou, X. L. (2016). Intention Modulates the Effect of Punishment Threat in Norm Enforcement via the Lateral Orbitofrontal Cortex. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that the intention behind punishment threat critically shapes norm compliance: when a partner intentionally retains the right to punish, participants allocate less to the partner (reduced norm compliance), but when the partner intentionally waives the right, participants allocate more (increased norm compliance and positive affect). When the punishment threat is unintentional (computer-decided), these effects are weaker or absent. The rLOFC is shown to mediate these effects, as brain stimulation modulates the behavioral response to intention. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the game is a modified dictator game rather than a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the social context and perceived intention behind punishment can strongly affect norm compliance (proxied by allocation), but does not report efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that in environments where punishment is perceived as self-serving or intentional, punishment may reduce cooperation or norm compliance, while unintentional or third-party punishment may be less detrimental or even neutral. For prediction, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may depend on whether punishment is perceived as legitimate or self-interested, but the lack of efficiency data and the use of a dictator game limit direct applicability to public goods game efficiency predictions.

- source: Bone, J. E., McAuliffe, K., & Raihani, N. J. (2016). Exploring the Motivations for Punishment: Framing and Country-Level Effects. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment behavior in a two-player stealing game is strongly influenced by country of origin. US-based participants are more likely to punish in response to both losses and disadvantageous inequality, while India-based participants are more punitive overall and engage in more antisocial punishment, but only increase punishment when experiencing disadvantageous inequality. Methodological factors such as stake size and punishment cost (within the tested range) do not significantly affect punishment rates. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and all outcome measures are behavioral (punishment frequency) rather than payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or total earnings. However, it provides informative evidence about how punishment behavior varies with country of origin and with the presence of disadvantageous inequality, which may be relevant for understanding when punishment is likely to be used. The design is adjacent to PGGs (two-player stealing game with punishment), but not a PGG or threshold public goods game. The findings suggest that punishment rates and motives can vary substantially by context and population, which may moderate any efficiency effects in related games.

- source: D'Orsogna, M. R., Kendall, R., McBride, M., & Short, M. B. (2013). Criminal Defectors Lead to the Emergence of Cooperation in an Experimental, Adversarial Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that the availability of the Informant strategy (criminals who also report/punish other criminals) is necessary for the emergence and stability of high cooperation in an adversarial game. When Informants are allowed, the system reliably transitions to and maintains a high-cooperation state (Utopia), regardless of initial conditions or parameter profile. When Informants are not allowed, the system converges to high defection (Dystopia). The results are robust across different initializations, parameter profiles (costs of reporting), and levels of behavioral inertia. However, the study does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare as outcome variables.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability of a punishment mechanism (specifically, the Informant strategy) is crucial for the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in adversarial games. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral strategies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The game is adjacent to PGGs, and the behavioral results suggest that enabling punishment (especially by defectors) can transform a high-defection environment into a high-cooperation one, which would likely increase efficiency if measured. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of this inference.

- source: Cooper, D. J., & Kühn, K. U. (2014). Communication, Renegotiation, and the Scope for Collusion. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that communication, especially when it allows explicit threats of punishment, is critical for sustaining collusion in repeated games with incentives to cheat. Limited message spaces (even those allowing contingent punishment messages) do not sustain collusion, while open chat does. Renegotiation (chat between periods) further increases collusion, likely due to the role of verbal (non-monetary) punishment. Explicit threats of punishment significantly reduce cheating, and sending promises of trustworthy behavior also reduces cheating, though to a lesser extent. The effect of communication is not simply due to increased trust or appeals to mutual benefit, but specifically to the ability to coordinate on credible punishment strategies. The results are robust to various econometric specifications.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment depends critically on the communication environment. Open-ended chat that allows explicit threats of punishment leads to higher and more stable cooperation (collusion) than limited or structured message spaces. However, the game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured as a share of maximum group payoff. The findings suggest that, in environments where punishment is possible, its effectiveness is greatly enhanced by the ability to communicate explicit threats, and that the structure of communication channels is a key moderator. The results also highlight that behavioral outcomes (collusion, cheating) can diverge from what would be predicted by monetary incentives alone, due to the role of verbal and social punishment.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2016). Looking Under the Hood of Third-Party Punishment Reveals Design for Personal Benefit. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is driven by the punisher's inference that a dictator who mistreats a recipient is also likely to mistreat the punisher. When punishers have information about how they themselves are treated, this information dominates their punishment decisions, and information about third-party treatment is largely ignored. The results support a deterrence-based account of punishment motivation. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. Its findings are relevant for understanding the psychological motivations for punishment, especially the conditions under which punishment is likely to be deployed, but it does not address whether punishment increases or decreases group efficiency. The design is a modified dictator/third-party-punishment game, not a standard public goods game, and no efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency.

- source: Delton, A. W., & Krasnow, M. M. (2017). The psychology of deterrence explains why group membership matters for third-party punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment is strongly influenced by group membership: punishers assign the most punishment when an outgroup dictator treats an ingroup recipient poorly, and the least when an ingroup dictator treats an outgroup recipient poorly. This pattern is best explained by deterrence psychology, where punishers infer that outgroup dictators who mistreat ingroup members are likely to mistreat the punisher as well. The group norm maintenance theory (fairness or parochialism norms) does not fit the observed punishment patterns. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on punishment behavior and inferences about personal valuation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence about the psychological mechanisms underlying third-party punishment and how group membership moderates punishment behavior. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most useful for understanding when and why punishment is likely to be assigned, especially in relation to group membership, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment interventions.

- source: Bone, J. E., & Raihani, N. J. (2015). Human punishment is motivated by both a desire for revenge and a desire for equality. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment is motivated by both a desire for revenge (punishment occurs even when it cannot restore equality) and a desire for equality (when possible, punishment is tailored to equalize payoffs). Punishment is more likely when stealing creates disadvantageous inequality and when punishment is effective. However, the outcomes measured are behavioral (punishment frequency and amount), not efficiency or group payoff. The experiment is a two-player, one-shot, anonymous game with no communication, and does not use a public goods game or its direct variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about how punishment behavior is shaped by the effectiveness of punishment and the presence of inequality, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar multi-player settings. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment will be used and how it will be targeted, especially regarding the importance of punishment effectiveness (fee-to-fine ratio) and inequality, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not report efficiency or welfare, so its value for the prediction task is limited to qualitative insights about punishment motivation and targeting.

- source: Rommel, J. (2015). What can economic experiments tell us about institutional change in social-ecological systems?. *Environmental Science & Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing third-party punishment of traders (who cause negative externalities) leads to a significant reduction in market entry rates compared to the baseline, especially for sellers. About 86% of observers are willing to punish, spending on average more than half their endowment to do so. The conditional entry treatment also reduces market entry, as about a quarter of participants condition their entry on others' behavior, reflecting norm conformity. The main outcome is the proportion of participants who choose to trade (i.e., cause the externality), not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings show that punishment can enforce social norms and reduce harmful market behavior, but the study does not measure efficiency as defined in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability of costly punishment reduces the frequency of norm-violating behavior (trading that causes negative externalities) in a large market setting. However, because the outcome is market entry rate (not group efficiency or total payoff), the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency in public goods games. The punishment mechanism is effective at deterring harmful behavior, especially among sellers, and most observers are willing to punish at a cost. For prediction tasks, the main signal is that enabling punishment reduces the rate of norm violation, but the mapping to efficiency or group payoff in standard PGGs is indirect. The market structure, one-shot design, and externality to a third party differ from standard PGGs, so caution is needed in transferring quantitative effects.

- source: Güney, S., & Newell, B. R. (2013). Fairness overrides reputation: the importance of fairness considerations in altruistic cooperation. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that rejection of unfair offers (costly punishment) in the Mini Ultimatum Game is primarily driven by fairness considerations, particularly the perceived intentions of the proposer. When intentions are not assessable, outcome fairness still motivates some punishment, but to a lesser degree. The opportunity for reputation building and future interaction (i.e., repeated, non-anonymous play) does not significantly increase costly punishment when fairness considerations are strong, but can increase punishment when intentions are ambiguous. The study does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the psychological drivers of costly punishment in ultimatum-like bargaining games are primarily fairness-based, not reputation-based, and that the presence of repeated interaction or reputation opportunities does not necessarily increase punishment or change behavioral outcomes when fairness motives are strong. However, because the study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment occurs, not its aggregate payoff consequences.

- source: Myers, C. D. (2016). Participation and punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that when punishment is possible, participation (turnout) increases dramatically (from 60% to 88%). Punishment is overwhelmingly directed at abstainers, and abstainers incur a net loss from being punished. Egoist subjects are more responsive to punishment than pro-social subjects, leading to a more egoist composition among participants when punishment is available. However, because participation is costly and does not increase the value of the prize, increased participation does not improve group efficiency or welfare. The study does not report treatment effects on efficiency or group payoff, and the design makes clear that increased participation is not efficiency-enhancing.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment increases participation in a binary-choice, team-based contest game where participation is individually costly and does not increase group welfare. However, it does not provide evidence that punishment increases efficiency or group payoff; in fact, the design ensures that increased participation is efficiency-reducing. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment depends critically on the structure of the game: in games where participation is not efficiency-enhancing, punishment may increase costly participation without improving (and possibly reducing) efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral responses (participation/cooperation) to punishment, not for predicting efficiency outcomes in standard public goods games.

- source: Engel, C., Goerg, S. J., & Yu, G. N. (2016). Symmetric vs. Asymmetric Punishment Regimes for Collusive Bribery. *American Law and Economics Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that when punishment is asymmetric (bribers punished less than recipients), bribers are much more likely to report to authorities if recipients accept the bribe but do not grant the favor. This threat is anticipated by recipients, who are then more likely to grant the favor, and bribers are more likely to offer bribes. These effects are robust across countries (Germany and China) and to framing (neutral vs. corruption). The main outcomes are behavioral (bribe offers, reporting, granting favors), not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that asymmetric punishment, while intended to deter corruption, may actually facilitate collusive bribery by making enforcement of corrupt deals easier.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that lowering the cost of punishment (making it cheaper for one party to punish another) increases the use of punishment and, in this context, increases the frequency of cooperation in corrupt deals. However, because the game is not a public goods game or direct variant, and because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The main implication is that punishment cost and effectiveness are critical moderators of behavioral outcomes, but the translation to efficiency is not directly tested here.

- source: Meier, S., Pierce, L., Vaccaro, A., & La Cara, B. (2016). Trust and in-group favoritism in a culture of crime. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment mechanisms increase cooperation rates in one-shot prisoner's dilemma games in both high- and low-Mafia neighborhoods, but do not close the gap in trust or cooperation between the two. In high-Mafia schools, punishment intensifies in-group favoritism, leading to higher cooperation with in-group members but not with out-group members. In low-Mafia schools, punishment does not increase in-group favoritism and may even reduce it. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. The main outcomes are behavioral: trust, cooperation, and in-group bias.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing punishment in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma increases cooperation rates, but the effect on efficiency is not reported. The design is adjacent to a public goods game (binary PD, not multi-person PGG), so results may partially transfer. The effect of punishment is context-dependent: in environments with strong in-group norms (high-Mafia), punishment increases in-group favoritism and does not resolve overall trust/cooperation deficits. For prediction tasks, this suggests that punishment may increase cooperation rates but not necessarily efficiency, especially in settings with strong parochial norms. The absence of efficiency or group payoff data limits direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2017). Effects of Gender on Costly Punishment. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that costly punishment is used frequently in a two-player prisoner's dilemma with a punishment option, primarily as moralistic punishment (punishing defection after cooperating). Participants spend more money punishing defecting female partners than male partners, but this is due to higher cooperation rates with female partners, not a direct gender bias in punishment. Female participants are more likely to cooperate with and punish female partners, while male participants do not differentiate by partner gender. The presence of punishment by partners increases cooperation rates, but the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, nor does it compare efficiency with and without punishment in a way that would allow direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costly punishment is used in a two-player prisoner's dilemma, and that the opportunity to punish is strongly influenced by cooperation rates, which in turn are affected by gender dynamics. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, nor does it provide a control (no-punishment) condition for direct comparison. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation when the threat of being punished exists, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits its direct utility for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games or their variants. The design is adjacent to PGGs, and the behavioral findings may inform models of punishment and cooperation, but not efficiency prediction per se.

- source: Yudkin, D. A., Rothmund, T., Twardawski, M., Thalla, N., & Van Bavel, J. J. (2016). Reflexive Intergroup Bias in Third-Party Punishment. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three experiments, participants assigned to a third-party role punished out-group transgressors more harshly than in-group transgressors, especially under conditions promoting reflexive judgment (fast response or cognitive load). The effect is robust across different group identity manipulations (sports teams, nationality). The punishment is costless to the punisher and only removes money from the transgressor. The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare. The findings are about behavioral bias in punishment assignment, not about the impact of punishment on cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological determinants of punishment assignment (intergroup bias, reflexivity) in a third-party punishment context, but does not provide data on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on cooperation. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games but is a one-shot, three-player 'stealing and punishment' scenario with costless punishment. Thus, the findings are not directly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. The main relevance is for understanding when and how punishment is assigned, not its effect on group welfare.

- source: Schroeder, K. B., Pepper, G. V., & Nettle, D. (2014). Local norms of cheating and the cultural evolution of crime and punishment: a study of two urban neighborhoods. *Peerj*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a 3PP game, residents of a high-crime, low-trust neighborhood (B) are more likely to steal and less likely to punish theft than those in a low-crime, high-trust neighborhood (A). Perceived cheating (descriptive norm violation) is higher in B and predicts both more theft and less expectation of punishment. Experimentally manipulating perceived norms in B increases expectation of punishment. The cost of punishment (subjective value of £2) and trust both moderate willingness to punish. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but mean payoffs are similar across neighborhoods. The results support the idea that local norms and trust shape antisocial behavior and punishment, with potential feedback loops.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. While it does not use a standard public goods game or directly analyze efficiency, it shows that punishment is less likely to be used in low-trust, high-norm-violation environments, and that expectation of punishment can be increased by manipulating perceived norms. The cost of punishment and trust are important moderators. For prediction, this suggests that punishment may be less effective at increasing efficiency in environments with low trust and high perceived norm violation, and that the subjective cost of punishment matters. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the evidence is indirect and should be used with caution.

- source: Ouss, A., & Peysakhovich, A. (2015). When Punishment Doesn't Pay: Cold Glow and Decisions to Punish. *Journal of Law & Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across a series of lab experiments, the authors show that individual punishment decisions are driven by 'cold glow' (private satisfaction from punishing) rather than by optimizing social efficiency. When punishment costs are shared by the group, individuals assign higher punishments than when they pay the cost themselves, resulting in over-punishment and excessive social cost. When the probability of apprehension is low, punishers do not increase punishment to compensate, so deterrence is insufficient and social efficiency is not achieved. When multiple punishers can assign punishment, their decisions are not crowded out by others' punishments, leading to excessive total punishment. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, taking behavior), but the implications for efficiency are discussed in terms of deviation from optimal deterrence and social cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on institutional details: (1) If punishment costs are externalized (group pays), enabling punishment can reduce efficiency due to over-punishment and excessive social cost. (2) If the probability of apprehension is low, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency because punishers do not adjust their behavior, leading to under-deterrence. (3) When multiple punishers are present, lack of crowding out can lead to excessive total punishment and lower efficiency. The findings suggest that simply enabling punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the effect is moderated by cost structure, probability of apprehension, and the number of punishers. For prediction, the presence of group-funded punishment, low apprehension probability, or multiple punishers should be expected to reduce or even reverse the efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Brañas-Garza, P., Espín, A. M., Exadaktylos, F., & Herrmann, B. (2014). Fair and unfair punishers coexist in the Ultimatum Game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both prosocial and antisocial individuals engage in costly punishment in the Ultimatum Game, as measured by rejection of unequal offers. Prosocial punishers are those who also behave fairly in the Dictator Game, while antisocial punishers are unfair as dictators but still reject unequal offers in the Ultimatum Game. The coexistence of these types is robust across two large-scale experiments (one with a representative city sample, one with university students). The paper highlights that antisocial punishment may undermine the efficiency and stability of social systems, but does not provide direct experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it focuses on the motivations behind punishment in the Ultimatum Game. However, it offers important contextual evidence that not all punishment is prosocial or norm-enforcing; antisocial punishment can exist and may have negative implications for efficiency in broader social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency, especially if antisocial motives are prevalent. The findings are most relevant for understanding heterogeneity in punishment motives, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Koenig, B. L., & Riley, C. M. (2017). To what reference point do people calibrate cost-free, third-party punishment?. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three experiments, participants assigned preferred punishments (fines) to a perpetrator in a theft scenario, with victim loss and perpetrator gain manipulated independently. Both victim loss and perpetrator gain influenced punishment preferences, with participants tending to select the larger of the two as a reference point. On average, preferred punishments exceeded the sum of victim loss and perpetrator gain. A substantial minority of participants preferred punishments much larger than this outcome differential, while a small minority preferred no punishment. When asked about compensation for the victim (rather than punishment for the perpetrator), participants' preferred amounts were lower and rarely exceeded the outcome differential. The study demonstrates that third-party punishment preferences are not simply proportional to harm or gain, but often exceed both, and that there is considerable heterogeneity in punitive preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it is informative about the psychological calibration of punishment preferences in third-party contexts, showing that people often prefer punishments that more than offset both victim loss and perpetrator gain. This suggests that, in environments where third-party punishment is possible and costless, punishment levels may be set high, potentially deterring transgressions more strongly than would be predicted by models based solely on harm or gain. The findings are most relevant for understanding the psychological underpinnings of punishment, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Lönnqvist, J. E., Rilke, R. M., & Walkowitz, G. (2015). On why hypocrisy thrives: Reasonable doubt created by moral posturing can deter punishment. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in ultimatum games, proposers can reduce the likelihood of being punished for unfair offers by claiming that a random process (coin flip) determined the outcome. This effect holds even when responders do not believe the claim and is observed for both direct victims (responders) and third-party observers (judges). The reduction in punishment is not explained by responders' self-interest or gratitude. The studies do not report on group efficiency or total earnings, but the findings suggest that moral posturing can deter punishment and thus may allow unfair behavior to persist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of plausible deniability (moral posturing) can reduce the frequency of punishment in bargaining games, even when punishment is available and even if the claim is not believed. However, the study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most relevant for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, especially the role of uncertainty or doubt about intent, rather than the direct effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Roberts, S. C., Vakirtzis, A., Kristjánsdóttir, L., & Havlícek, J. (2013). Who Punishes? Personality Traits Predict Individual Variation in Punitive Sentiment. *Evolutionary Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that individual differences in willingness to punish a transgressor in a public-goods-like scenario are predicted by personality traits, especially lower Agreeableness. Other traits (Conscientiousness, Extraversion) have weaker or more limited associations. The study uses hypothetical scenarios where participants indicate their anger, desire to punish, and willingness to pay to punish a free-rider, but no actual economic game is played and no payoff or efficiency data are collected. The findings are relevant for understanding who is likely to engage in costly punishment, but do not inform the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or variants. It does not manipulate or measure any game design dimensions relevant to efficiency, nor does it report control or treatment efficiency. Its findings are about individual differences in punitive sentiment, not about the consequences of punishment for group outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to predict efficiency effects of punishment based on game design dimensions.

- source: Ruff, C. C., Ugazio, G., & Fehr, E. (2013). Changing Social Norm Compliance with Noninvasive Brain Stimulation. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that the threat of punishment increases norm compliance (higher transfers) in a modified dictator game, and that noninvasive brain stimulation of the rLPFC can causally modulate this effect. Anodal tDCS (increasing excitability) increases sanction-induced compliance (larger transfer difference between punishment and baseline rounds), while cathodal tDCS (decreasing excitability) reduces it. The effect is stronger in social (human) punishment contexts than in non-social (computer) contexts. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral compliance rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment opportunities increases norm compliance in a dictator-like game, and that this effect is modulated by neural mechanisms. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The behavioral results (increased compliance under punishment) are consistent with the broader literature, but the lack of payoff data limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2014). The cost of cowardice: punitive sentiments towards free riders in Turkana raids. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that Turkana participants are significantly more likely to express punitive sentiments (both direct and indirect) toward hypothetical warriors who free ride (display cowardice) in combat, compared to those who fail to contribute due to inability (lack of skill). Direct sanctions include criticism and corporal punishment, while indirect sanctions include withholding social support, lending, and marriage opportunities. The findings support the idea that both direct punishment and indirect reciprocity are important mechanisms for sustaining large-scale cooperation in acephalous societies. However, the study does not measure actual payoffs, group efficiency, or the effect of punishment on group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punitive motivations toward free riders are present in a real-world, high-stakes collective action context, and that both direct and indirect sanctions are socially endorsed. However, because the study does not measure efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in a controlled game, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. The findings are most useful for understanding the psychological and social underpinnings of punishment in collective action, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes based on game design dimensions.

- source: Lopez, A. C. (2017). The Evolutionary Psychology of War: Offense and Defense in the Adapted Mind. *Evolutionary Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that (1) willingness to participate in collective aggression is predicted by expected group benefit in defense (public good) and by expected personal benefit in offense (private/club good); (2) punitive sentiment toward nonparticipants is more strongly triggered by willingness to participate in defense than in offense, supporting the idea that punishment is used to enforce participation in public goods contexts; (3) reward sentiment is also modulated by conflict type, with willingness to participate predicting reward sentiment in offense, and expected benefit predicting it in defense; (4) sex differences exist, with males generally more willing to participate, but labor sufficiency cues can increase female participation in defense; (5) labor sufficiency/insufficiency manipulations affect both willingness to participate and punitive sentiment, especially in defense. However, all outcomes are self-reported attitudes or intentions in hypothetical scenarios, not actual behavior or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment is more likely to be used and to have a motivational effect in public goods (defense) contexts than in private/club goods (offense) contexts, and that the effectiveness of punishment as a recruitment/enforcement tool depends on the structure of the collective action problem and cues about labor sufficiency. However, it does not provide any direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, group payoff, or realized cooperation in public goods games or similar environments. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment will be more or less effective as a cooperation-enforcing mechanism, especially in relation to the publicness of the good and the perceived need for labor, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The study is best used for mechanism and moderator hypotheses, not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Bshary, R. (2015). Third-party punishers are rewarded, but third-party helpers even more so. *Evolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishers are more likely to be rewarded by bystanders than those who do nothing in response to selfish behavior, but third-party helpers are rewarded even more frequently. Bystanders' justifications indicate that helping is always seen as positive and deserving of reward, while punishment is more controversial. The context (whether the target of punishment was selfish or fair) matters for how punishment is perceived. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods or repeated game. The findings are about the reputational consequences of third-party punishment and helping, not about their effects on cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The design is a one-shot dictator game with third-party intervention and bystander reward, not a PGG or repeated game. No efficiency or payoff-based outcomes are reported. The main relevance is for understanding reputational incentives for punishment and helping, not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, except as very weak, indirect context about reputational motivations for punishment.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Li, Y., Fermin, A. S. R., Kanai, R., Takagishi, H., Matsumoto, Y., Kiyonari, T., & Sakagami, M. (2017). Behavioural Differences and Neural Substrates of Altruistic and Spiteful Punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that individuals who engage in costly punishment in the TPPG (altruistic punishers) also behave more pro-socially in a variety of non-strategic games, while those who only reject unfair offers in the UG (spiteful punishers) behave more selfishly. The distinction is supported by neural data: spiteful punishers have larger left caudate nucleus and nucleus accumbens volumes. The paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it manipulate the presence or absence of punishment in a public goods or social dilemma context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or social dilemma games. It does, however, suggest that the motivations behind punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) are heterogeneous and that not all punishment is associated with pro-social or efficiency-enhancing behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment may not always increase efficiency, depending on the underlying motives of punishers, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Balafoutas, L., Nikiforakis, N., & Rockenbach, B. (2014). Direct and indirect punishment among strangers in the field. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that (1) direct punishment of norm violators is not rewarded by third parties in one-shot field interactions; (2) indirect punishment, operationalized as withholding help from a norm violator, is common and significantly reduces the likelihood that violators receive help; (3) the availability of indirect punishment opportunities crowds out the use of direct punishment; and (4) there are notable sex differences in the application of direct and indirect punishment. The experiment does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, but provides evidence on the behavioral mechanisms of norm enforcement in natural settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its main contribution is to show, in a natural field setting, that indirect punishment (withholding help) is more common and preferred over direct punishment when both are available, and that direct punishment is not socially rewarded. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that in environments where indirect punishment is possible and not costly, direct costly punishment may be less prevalent, but the paper does not inform how this affects group efficiency or payoff. The design is adjacent to PGGs, as the norm violation (littering) is a negative externality, but there is no explicit group payoff or contribution structure. Thus, the paper is more informative about behavioral mechanisms than about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Brethel-Haurwitz, K. M., Stoycos, S. A., Cardinale, E. M., Huebner, B., & Marsh, A. A. (2016). Is costly punishment altruistic? Exploring rejection of unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game in real-world altruists. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that extraordinary real-world altruists (altruistic kidney donors) do not engage in more costly punishment (rejection of unfair offers) in the Ultimatum Game than controls. However, individuals with higher self-reported normative altruism (SRA) are more likely to reject unfair offers, indicating that costly punishment is associated with norm sensitivity rather than altruism per se. The study provides evidence that costly punishment in the UG is better understood as norm-enforcing behavior rather than altruistic behavior. No direct analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare is conducted.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding the motivations behind costly punishment in the UG, suggesting that such punishment is driven by norm enforcement rather than altruism. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers little direct guidance on how punishment affects efficiency, as it does not report or analyze payoff-based outcomes. However, it does reinforce that the presence of punishment in UG-like games is not necessarily linked to increased altruism or prosociality, and that behavioral outcomes (like rejection rates) may not translate to efficiency gains.

- source: Duersch, P., & Müller, J. (2015). Taking punishment into your own hands: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a substantial minority of subjects are willing to pay to personally punish an unfair allocator, even when punishment is guaranteed to occur regardless of who enacts it. Bids for personal punishment are significantly higher than for a dummy (non-punishment) auction, indicating a specific demand for personal retribution. Winning the right to punish personally increases self-reported happiness. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and the game structure is not a public goods game or close variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding the psychological motives behind punishment, especially the value placed on personal retribution, but do not inform predictions about efficiency changes when punishment is enabled in PGG-like environments. The design is a one-shot real-effort allocation game with a punishment stage, not a repeated or multi-player public goods game. Therefore, it should not be used to predict efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Eriksson, K., Stringing, P., Andersson, P. A., & Lindholm, T. (2017). Costly punishment in the ultimatum game evokes moral concern, in particular when framed as payoff reduction. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across a series of online experiments, the authors find that framing costly punishment as 'reduction' (explicitly paying to reduce the other's payoff) leads to less frequent use of punishment, greater moral concern about using punishment, and a stronger association with punitive motives, compared to the standard 'rejection' framing in the ultimatum game. The reduction frame is more likely to be seen as immoral and as punishment, while rejection is more often seen as a quest for fairness. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its findings are limited to the ultimatum game, which is structurally adjacent but not equivalent to the public goods game. The main outcomes are behavioral (frequency of punishment, motives, moral judgments), not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, except as a caution that framing and perceived morality can influence the use of punishment. It does, however, highlight that the way punishment is framed can substantially affect its use, which may be relevant for interpreting behavioral outcomes in other game contexts.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Liebe, U. (2016). Generosity is a sign of trustworthiness-the punishment of selfishness is not. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that individuals who punish selfishness in a dictator game are not more trustworthy nor more trusted in a subsequent trust game, regardless of whether punishment is second-party or third-party. In fact, second-party punishers are trusted less than non-punishers. Generosity in the dictator game, however, is a strong predictor of trustworthiness and is perceived as such by others. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and belief-based measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it uses a binary dictator game with punishment and a trust game, and reports only on behavioral outcomes (punishment, generosity, trust, trustworthiness, beliefs). The findings suggest that punishment, at least in this context, does not enhance trust or trustworthiness, and by implication may not improve efficiency, but this is not directly tested. The design and results may be informative for understanding the signaling value of punishment versus generosity, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs.

- source: Hilbig, B. E., Thielmann, I., Klein, S. A., & Henninger, F. (2016). The two faces of cooperation: On the unique role of HEXACO Agreeableness for forgiveness versus retaliation. *Journal of Research in Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in the Uncostly Retaliation Game, participants are much more likely to retaliate (punish) after being treated unfairly, and that this retaliation is predicted by lower Agreeableness (HEXACO AG), but not by Honesty-Humility (HH). Retaliation after fair treatment (antisocial punishment) is rare and is negatively predicted by Honesty-Humility. The game structure ensures that punishment is uncostly to the punisher, isolating the psychological motive for retaliation. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported. The results are robust across two experiments and are not explained by social value orientation or inequality aversion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar multi-player, repeated games. Its main contribution is to show that the psychological trait of Agreeableness predicts individual differences in uncostly punishment (retaliation) after unfair treatment in a two-player, one-shot allocation game. The game design is adjacent to public goods games in that it involves punishment for norm violations, but it lacks the group, repeated, and efficiency elements central to the prediction task. The findings may inform models of individual punishment behavior, but not efficiency outcomes.

- source: Balafoutas, L., Nikiforakis, N., & Rockenbach, B. (2016). Altruistic punishment does not increase with the severity of norm violations in the field. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that, in a natural field setting where confederates commit small or large littering violations, the rate and intensity of direct punishment by bystanders do not increase with the severity of the violation. Similarly, indirect punishment (withholding help) is not more likely for larger violations. This is despite survey evidence that people feel stronger negative emotions and believe large violations should be more strongly reprimanded. The authors attribute the lack of increased punishment to a higher perceived risk of counter-punishment for more severe violations. The study does not measure or report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral responses to norm violations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in real-world one-shot norm violation settings, the presence and intensity of altruistic punishment do not scale with the severity of the violation, in contrast to laboratory public goods games. However, because the study does not use a public goods game or report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. Its main relevance is in highlighting that field punishment behavior may not match lab findings, especially regarding the responsiveness of punishment to violation severity. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not provide payoff data or manipulate standard PGG dimensions.

- source: White, S. F., Brislin, S. J., Sinclair, S., & Blair, J. R. (2014). Punishing unfairness: Rewarding or the organization of a reactively aggressive response?. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that as participants punish unfair offers more severely, there is increased activation in brain regions associated with reactive aggression (PAG, dmPFC, AIC) and decreased activation in reward-related regions (vmPFC, PCC). The behavioral data show that participants spend more to punish as offers become more unfair. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The findings challenge the view that punishment in social exchange games is motivated by reward, instead supporting a reactive aggression interpretation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or social exchange games. It is relevant for understanding the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying punishment behavior, but does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes.
