Therefore, it should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment based on game design dimensions. Its main value is in informing mechanistic or motivational models of punishment, not payoff-based outcomes.

- source: Kurz, T., Thomas, W. E., & Fonseca, M. A. (2014). A fine is a more effective financial deterrent when framed retributively and extracted publicly. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The field experiment demonstrates that the effectiveness of financial punishment in deterring undesired behavior (lateness) depends strongly on both the framing (retributive vs. compensatory) and the public/private nature of the sanction. Retributive and public fines significantly increase punctuality compared to compensatory or private fines, which are no more effective than no fine. A follow-up scenario study shows that compensatory fines reduce the perceived immorality of transgression, while retributive fines do not. The results support a theory that the moral framing and public visibility of punishment are key moderators of its behavioral impact.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the framing and public administration of punishment can substantially moderate its behavioral effectiveness, even when the material cost is held constant. While the context is not a public goods game, the findings suggest that in collective action settings, punishment will be more effective at promoting cooperation if it is framed as retributive (moral) and administered publicly. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so direct prediction of efficiency effects is not possible. The results are most useful for informing the design of punishment mechanisms (e.g., public vs. private, moral vs. compensatory framing) rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Berger, J. (2016). The Sanctioning Dilemma: A Quasi-Experiment on Social Norm Enforcement in the Train. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that peer-sanctioning (negative sanctioning of a norm violator) occurs in about 50% of interventions. Contrary to the classic diffusion of responsibility hypothesis, the likelihood of sanctioning increases with the number of passengers present. Passengers who are closer to the norm breaker and those whose activities require silence are more likely to sanction. The likelihood of sanctioning decreases as the train approaches its destination. No significant difference in sanctioning rates is found between silent-area and non-silent-area train cars. The study does not measure or report any payoff, efficiency, or group welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their variants. While it is conceptually adjacent—framing norm enforcement as a second-order public good and using volunteer's dilemma theory—the outcomes are strictly behavioral (sanctioning occurrence and timing). There is no measurement of group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, nor any manipulation of punishment cost, effectiveness, or related game design dimensions. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, though it may provide some indirect, conceptual support for the importance of heterogeneity and group size in norm enforcement contexts.

- source: Konishi, N., Oe, T., Shimizu, H., Tanaka, K., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2017). Perceived Shared Condemnation Intensifies Punitive Moral Emotions. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that perceived shared condemnation increases moral outrage and, to a lesser extent, moral disgust, while reducing empathy for norm violators. These emotional shifts are associated with greater willingness to punish, both in hypothetical scenarios and in self-reported real-life interventions. The experimental manipulation in Study 2 shows that providing information about others' condemnation causally increases moral outrage and willingness to impose a fine. However, the studies do not use economic games, do not measure actual punishment behavior with real stakes, and do not report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar economic games. It does, however, suggest that social cues about shared condemnation can increase willingness to punish, which may be relevant for understanding psychological drivers of punishment in group settings. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the idea that punishment is more likely when people believe others will also punish, but it does not address how this affects group efficiency, payoff, or cooperation rates in economic games.

- source: Berger, J., & Hevenstone, D. (2016). Norm enforcement in the city revisited: An international field experiment of altruistic punishment, norm maintenance, and broken windows. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that direct and indirect norm enforcement behaviors (including costly punishment) occur in natural, anonymous, one-shot interactions, but at lower rates than in laboratory experiments. Enforcement is more common in Switzerland than in New York City. The presence of disorder (litter) reduces the likelihood of both direct and indirect enforcement. Making it easier to maintain the norm (by being close to a trashcan) increases norm maintenance behaviors. Subtle sanctions and norm maintenance are more frequent than direct confrontation. Individual characteristics (in-group status, age) also predict enforcement. The study does not report any efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, so its findings are not directly transferable to predictions about group efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costly punishment and other forms of norm enforcement are less frequent in real-world, anonymous, one-shot settings than in laboratory public goods games. It also shows that environmental context (disorder, opportunity structure) and location matter for the frequency of enforcement. However, because the study does not measure group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value for prediction is as indirect, contextual evidence about the real-world prevalence and moderators of costly punishment, suggesting that laboratory findings may overestimate the frequency and possibly the efficiency impact of punishment in naturalistic settings.

- source: Mathew, S. (2017). How the Second-Order Free Rider Problem Is Solved in a Small-Scale Society. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that Turkana adults express strong disapproval toward second-order free riders (those who do not punish norm violators), as well as toward those who punish excessively, unjustly, or without proper group consensus. Punishment is seen as legitimate only when collectively decided and administered by a preordained group. These meta-norms help regulate peer punishment and may solve the second-order free rider problem in this society. However, the evidence is based on responses to hypothetical scenarios, not on observed behavior or economic outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that strong social norms and meta-norms can support the legitimacy and regulation of peer punishment in a real-world society, potentially solving the second-order free rider problem. However, because the study does not measure efficiency, group payoff, or actual economic outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of meta-norms and collective legitimacy for the functioning of peer punishment systems.

- source: Eriksson, K., Andersson, P. A., & Strimling, P. (2016). Moderators of the disapproval of peer punishment. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment of norm violators is typically disapproved of by observers, especially when the punishment is severe or appears aggressive. This disapproval extends to viewing the punisher as a problem for the group and the act as inappropriate. Disapproval is moderated by the observer's own trait aggression (less disapproval among more aggressive raters), the severity of punishment (more severe = more disapproval), and whether the punishment is collective or individual (collective = slightly less disapproval). Economic sanctions are disapproved of less than physical ones, and unselfish economic sanctions (where the punisher does not profit) are disapproved of less than selfish ones. Verbal sanctions are also disapproved of, even when effective. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any related outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding social approval and the perceived legitimacy of peer punishment, but not for predicting how punishment affects group efficiency or earnings. The design is adjacent to PGGs (common resource, norm violation, sanctioning), but the outcomes are purely attitudinal and perceptual. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency, though it may inform hypotheses about the social acceptability of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Wang, L., & Murnighan, J. K. (2017). The Dynamics of Punishment and Trust. *Journal of Applied Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that punishment increases observers' trust in the punisher when the punished individual is seen as deserving, but decreases trust when the punishment is undeserved or too severe. Medium or large punishment for deserving targets increases trust, but large punishment for less deserving targets decreases trust. Punishers who are perceived as acting out of self-interest (e.g., who benefit from the punishment) are trusted less than altruistic punishers. These effects are robust across scenario-based and behavioral lab studies. However, the paper does not report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and all dependent variables are trust-related or perception-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods or social dilemma games with or without punishment. Its findings are relevant for understanding how punishment affects trust in the punisher, especially as a function of deservingness, punishment magnitude, and punisher motives. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency from game design plus control efficiency), this paper offers no direct evidence, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It may be useful for understanding psychological or reputational effects of punishment, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Eriksson, K., Strimling, P., Andersson, P. A., Aveyard, M., Brauer, M., Gritskov, V., Kiyonari, T., Kuhlman, D. M., Maitner, A. T., Manesi, Z., Molho, C., Peperkoorn, L. S., Rizwan, M., Stivers, A. W., Tian, Q. R., Van Lange, P. A. M., Vartanova, I., Wu, J. H., & Yamagishi, T. (2017). Cultural Universals and Cultural Differences in Meta-Norms about Peer Punishment. *Management and Organization Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that across eight countries, punishment of norm violators in a resource-sharing scenario is judged as more appropriate when enacted by the group rather than by an individual. There is substantial cross-cultural variation in the appraisal of punishers versus non-punishers: in more individualistic countries (USA, Netherlands, Sweden, Japan), punishers are rated less favorably than non-punishers, while in more collectivistic countries (Pakistan, UAE, Russia, China), this difference is absent or reversed. These patterns hold for both retributive and restorative forms of punishment. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any related payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are limited to social appraisals and meta-norms about punishment, not behavioral or payoff outcomes. However, it may inform predictions about the acceptability or likelihood of punishment being used in different cultural contexts, which could indirectly affect the implementation or effectiveness of punishment in real-world or experimental settings. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only contextual or indirect evidence, not direct evidence about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment.

- source: Gordon, D. S., & Lea, S. E. G. (2016). Who Punishes? The Status of the Punishers Affects the Perceived Success of, and Indirect Benefits From, Moralistic'' Punishment. *Evolutionary Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The studies find that observers expect only high-status (HS) individuals to engage in and succeed at moralistic punishment of norm violations. HS punishers are seen as more likely to be successful, more dominant, and more likely to be chosen for leadership roles. Low-status (LS) individuals are not expected to punish, and even if they do, they do not gain the same reputational or leadership benefits. The perceived benefits of punishment (e.g., increased likability, dominance, or leadership nomination) are conditional on both the status of the punisher and the success of the intervention. The work does not address actual behavioral outcomes in economic games, nor does it report on efficiency or payoff effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding social expectations and reputational dynamics around punishment, especially the role of status, but do not inform quantitative predictions about group efficiency or payoff outcomes when punishment is enabled. The results may be contextually useful for interpreting when and why punishment is expected or effective in real-world or experimental settings, particularly regarding heterogeneity in punishment costs or effectiveness due to status, but they do not provide data on efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Gordon, D. S., Madden, J. R., & Lea, S. E. G. (2014). Both Loved and Feared: Third Party Punishers Are Viewed as Formidable and Likeable, but These Reputational Benefits May Only Be Open to Dominant Individuals. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that third party punishment is perceived as both a dominant and likable act, distinguishing it from other aggressive behaviors. However, only dominant individuals are seen as able to punish effectively and at low cost, with reduced risk of retaliation and greater ability to use non-violent (social) punishment. Subordinate individuals are perceived as less likely to succeed and at higher risk if they attempt punishment. The reputational benefits of punishment (increased likability and dominance) are thus mainly accessible to dominant individuals. These findings are based on observer judgments in hypothetical scenarios, not on actual behavior in economic games or real payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that heterogeneity in the cost and effectiveness of punishment (due to dominance/status) may be important moderators of the impact of punishment on cooperation and norm enforcement. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that the effectiveness and social acceptability of punishment may depend on the perceived status of the punisher, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental data on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not for direct prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Adams, G. S., & Mullen, E. (2013). Increased Voting for Candidates Who Compensate Victims Rather than Punish Offenders. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that people prefer to vote for candidates who compensate victims (or both compensate and punish) over those who only punish perpetrators or do not respond to injustice. This preference is robust across different types of transgressions (genocide, bike theft, domestic violence) and is mediated by perceptions of warmth. Perceived effectiveness of the response is measured subjectively, not as a monetary or group payoff. The prevalence of the transgression and political orientation do not moderate these effects. The studies do not involve economic games, group cooperation, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. The outcomes are about third-party voting preferences and trait perceptions, not about how punishment affects cooperation, contributions, or efficiency. While the paper discusses punishment and compensation as responses to norm violations, it does not manipulate or measure the game design dimensions relevant to public goods games, nor does it report any payoff-based outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bigoni, M., Spagnolo, G., & Valbonesi, P. (2014). Sticks and Carrots in Procurement: An Experimental Exploration. *B E Journal of Economic Analysis & Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that explicit incentives—whether framed as penalties (punishment) or bonuses (reward)—strongly increase total surplus (efficiency) in a repeated buyer-seller game with multitasking and endogenous matching. In the absence of explicit incentives, surplus and effort are low. When explicit incentives are available and used, effort in the contractible dimension rises sharply, while effort in the non-contractible dimension falls only slightly, resulting in a net efficiency gain. There is no significant difference in surplus between bonus and penalty framing, though buyers prefer penalties because they can extract a larger share of the surplus, sometimes through exploitative contracts. The effect of punishment (penalties) on efficiency is robust and positive.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (penalty contracts) in a repeated, competitive, multitask environment with endogenous matching and incomplete contracts leads to a substantial increase in efficiency (surplus) compared to a control without explicit incentives. The effect is similar for both punishment and reward, but buyers prefer punishment due to distributional advantages. The design is not a standard PGG but is adjacent: it is a repeated, group-based, real-effort game with endogenous matching and both contractible and non-contractible tasks. The findings support the prediction that, in similar environments, enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is robust to the presence of repeated interaction, endogenous matching, and multitasking.

- source: Normann, H. T., Rösch, J., & Schultz, L. M. (2015). Do buyer groups facilitate collusion?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: In repeated Cournot triopoly experiments, buyer groups that can exclude members (closed buyer groups) enable targeted punishment of high-output (deviating) firms, which facilitates collusion and reduces industry output toward the joint-profit maximizing (collusive) level. This reduces efficiency and welfare compared to baseline or open buyer groups (which cannot exclude). Communication, especially when used for explicit quantitative agreements, further increases collusion and reduces output. Open buyer groups without exclusion do not significantly differ from baseline in terms of output or efficiency. The exclusion mechanism is used mainly against high-output firms, consistent with punishment of deviations. Overall, mechanisms that enable punishment (exclusion) or communication (especially explicit agreements) reduce efficiency by facilitating collusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling targeted punishment (via exclusion from a buyer group) in a repeated Cournot game reduces efficiency by facilitating collusion, as measured by lower industry output and welfare. The effect is more pronounced when communication is allowed, especially with explicit quantitative agreements. In the absence of exclusion or communication, buyer groups do not significantly affect efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (exclusion), communication, and the structure of the game (repeated, triopoly, continuous output) are key moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion or similar targeted sanctions, and less so for standard PGGs with costly punishment tokens.

- source: Lumeau, M., Masclet, D., & Penard, T. (2015). Reputation and social (dis)approval in feedback mechanisms: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both private and public feedback mechanisms increase trust (amount sent) and trustworthiness (return rate) in a repeated trust game compared to a baseline with no feedback. Public feedback (where ratings are observable to future partners) is more effective than private feedback (where ratings are only seen by the recipient) in increasing these behaviors. The difference is attributed to both a higher frequency of ratings and a stronger behavioral response to public ratings. When a cost is introduced for assigning ratings, the effects persist but are weaker, especially for private feedback. The study concludes that reputation mechanisms (public feedback) are essential for sustaining high levels of cooperation and implied efficiency in environments with imperfect information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a feedback mechanism (a form of non-monetary sanction or reward) increases cooperation and implied efficiency in trust-game-like environments. Publicly observable feedback (reputation) is more effective than private feedback (social (dis)approval only). The results are robust to the introduction of a small cost for assigning feedback. For prediction tasks, the presence and observability of feedback mechanisms should be expected to increase efficiency, with public/reputational mechanisms having a larger effect than private ones. However, the results are from a trust game, not a PGG, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs should be cautious, but the qualitative direction is likely similar.

- source: Gervais, M. M. (2017). RICH Economic Games for Networked Relationships and Communities: Development and Preliminary Validation in Yasawa, Fiji. *Field Methods*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a Fijian village, participants are willing to spend significant portions of their endowment to reduce the earnings of others in a costly reduction game (CRG), with 66% of deciders reducing at least one target. The mean amount spent on reductions was 45% of the available stake. Target earnings varied widely, and some participants ended with negative aggregate earnings (before post-hoc adjustment). The motives for reduction included both punitive and leveling (egalitarian) reasons. Compared to anonymous-target punishment games in neighboring villages, the rate of punitive behavior is higher in the CRG, likely due to the integration of recipient identities. However, the study does not directly measure the effect of punishment on group efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, nor does it use a standard public goods game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a context where punishment is possible and recipient identities are known, individuals are willing to pay substantial costs to reduce others' payoffs, often for leveling or punitive reasons. The cost-to-impact ratio is clearly specified (0.5 FJ$ cost to reduce 2 FJ$ from a target). However, because the game is not a standard public goods game and there is no control condition without punishment, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The high willingness to punish and the resulting wide variation in earnings suggest that punishment can lead to significant reductions in aggregate payoffs, especially when used for leveling rather than norm enforcement. The study is most informative for settings where punishment is possible, costly, and can be used for reasons beyond norm enforcement, particularly in small, close-knit communities.

- source: Johnson, D. B. (2016). (Please Don't) Say It to My Face! The Interaction of Feedback and Distance: Experiments with Vulgar Language. *Kyklos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The possibility of non-monetary punishment via unrestricted feedback increases proposer offers in the lab (where social distance is low), but not in online settings (where social distance is high). In the lab, known possible feedback leads to significantly higher offers, suggesting that the threat of negative feedback (punishment) can increase pro-social behavior and potentially improve monetary outcomes. However, in online/anonymous settings, the same feedback mechanism does not affect offers. The effect of punishment via feedback is thus moderated by social distance/proximity. The paper does not directly measure group efficiency as in public goods games, but the findings are relevant for understanding when non-monetary punishment can improve monetary outcomes in bargaining environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment mechanisms (here, non-monetary feedback) in increasing efficiency or pro-social monetary outcomes depends strongly on social distance and context. In environments with low social distance (e.g., lab, face-to-face), the threat of punishment can increase offers and thus improve monetary outcomes. In high social distance or anonymous environments (e.g., online), punishment has little or no effect on offers. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive only when social distance is low and the punishment is salient to the decision-maker. The findings are adjacent to public goods games but provide informative indirect evidence about the context-dependence of punishment effects.

- source: Jung, Y. B., Hall, J., Hong, R. Y., Goh, T., Ong, N., & Tan, N. (2014). Payback: Effects of relationship and cultural norms on reciprocity. *Asian Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that reciprocity norms (reward and punishment) are shaped by relationship and culture. Collectivists are more generous to friends after positive treatment and less likely to punish after negative treatment (even to strangers), sometimes offering an 'olive branch' (positive or neutral response to negative treatment). Individualists are more likely to punish norm violators (destroy money at a cost to themselves) regardless of relationship. The main dependent variable is the amount given or destroyed, not group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not use a public goods game or a repeated multi-player setting, but rather a dyadic hybrid trust-dictator game with a single round (though participants believed there would be five rounds).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral drivers of punishment and reward in dyadic trust/dictator game settings, especially the influence of culture and relationship on willingness to punish at a cost. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or multi-player repeated setting. The findings may inform expectations about the likelihood and intensity of costly punishment or reward in similar dyadic games, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes from enabling punishment in public goods games. The evidence is most relevant for understanding the psychological and cultural moderators of punishment behavior, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Ansink, E., Tesfaye, A., Bouma, J., & Brouwer, R. (2017). Cooperation in watershed management: A field experiment on location, trust, and enforcement. *Resource and Energy Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The enforcement (punishment) treatment, which penalizes trustees for not returning at least the amount sent by trustors, increases the amount returned but does not increase trust (investment by trustors). There is no significant effect of enforcement on average payoffs. Framing the game in terms of soil conservation reduces trust. Trust (as measured in the game) is a strong predictor of actual soil conservation investments in the field. The study finds no evidence that enforcement increases cooperation or efficiency in this context, and suggests that enforcement may crowd out trust. The trust game is not a public goods game but is related as a social dilemma with externalities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a punishment/enforcement mechanism in a trust game does not increase trust or average payoffs, and may crowd out intrinsic motivation for cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in trust-game-like environments with high baseline trust, adding punishment may not improve efficiency and could even reduce voluntary cooperation. However, the game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured as a share of maximum possible group payoff. The enforcement mechanism is probabilistic and only applies to the trustee role. The findings are most relevant for settings where trust and reciprocity are central, and less directly transferable to standard PGGs.

- source: Mendoza, S. A., Lane, S. P., & Amodio, D. M. (2014). For Members Only: Ingroup Punishment of Fairness Norm Violations in the Ultimatum Game. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that in the ultimatum game, participants are more likely to reject marginally unfair offers from in-group members than from out-group members, even though rejection is costly to both parties. This pattern is interpreted as stricter enforcement of fairness norms within groups. The effect is most pronounced for offers that are only slightly unfair (e.g., $8/$20), and is stronger among participants with higher in-group identification. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, and the outcome is measured in terms of behavioral responses (accept/reject) rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and social moderators of costly punishment in a bargaining context (ultimatum game), specifically the role of group membership and social identity. However, it does not provide data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or report on most game design dimensions relevant to public goods games. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment is more likely to be used (e.g., in-group contexts, marginal norm violations), but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or welfare in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Gingins, S., Werminghausen, J., Johnstone, R. A., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2013). Power and temptation cause shifts between exploitation and cooperation in a cleaner wrasse mutualism. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: Cleaner wrasse strategically adjust their foraging behavior in response to the threat of early termination (sanction) and temptation to cheat. When the threat of early termination is high and the temptation to cheat is low, cleaners behave more cooperatively (eat more less-preferred items, which is analogous to providing better service). When the temptation to cheat is high and the threat is low, cleaners exploit the situation (eat more preferred items). The non-cleaner control species does not adjust its behavior in response to these manipulations. The study provides evidence that simple partner control mechanisms (sanctions) can maintain cooperation in asymmetric interactions, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that the presence of a sanction mechanism (threat of early termination) can shift behavior from exploitation to cooperation, especially when the temptation to cheat is low. However, the outcomes are behavioral and not directly about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in analogous public goods games, enabling a sanction mechanism may increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency would depend on the cost structure and whether the sanction is costly or not. The study is most informative for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which sanctions can promote cooperation, but less directly informative for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Landeo, C. M., & Spier, K. E. (2015). Incentive contracts for teams: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that long-term team interaction (infinitely repeated games) increases both the likelihood of team cooperation and the principal's payoff, especially in the absence of communication. Communication between agents also increases cooperation and the principal's payoff in short-term (one-shot) settings, but is a less effective substitute for ongoing interaction. Long-term teams enable the principal to induce cooperation with lower sharing rules, reducing the cost of cooperation and increasing efficiency. However, if the principal chooses the lowest possible sharing rule, agents may engage in costly punishment (shirking) as negative reciprocity, which can reduce efficiency. The effects are robust across endogenous (human principal) and exogenous (computer) environments, but negative reciprocity is stronger when the principal is a human.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling ongoing interaction (repeated games) among team members increases efficiency and allows for cooperation at a lower cost to the principal, especially when communication is not available. Communication can substitute for repeated interaction to some extent in one-shot games, but does not further increase efficiency when repeated interaction is already present. The findings suggest that, in environments structurally similar to this experiment (team production with effort complementarity and binary effort choices), introducing repeated interaction or communication will increase efficiency, but the effect of communication is limited when repetition is already present. The results also highlight that punitive responses (shirking as punishment) can reduce efficiency if the incentive structure is perceived as unfair, especially with human principals. For prediction, the presence of repeated interaction (long-term teams) is a strong positive moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency, while communication is a weaker, context-dependent moderator.

- source: Li, L. F., & Xiao, E. (2014). Money Talks: Rebate Mechanisms in Reputation System Design. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when feedback reporting is costly, market efficiency (proportion of efficient trades and participant earnings) declines. Introducing a rebate mechanism, where sellers can offer a partial rebate to buyers for leaving feedback, increases efficiency—especially when sellers frequently offer rebates. The rebate acts as a signal of seller cooperativeness, increasing buyers' willingness to purchase and sellers' willingness to ship. The effect is robust even when the rebate does not fully cover the reporting cost. The more often sellers offer rebates, the higher the efficiency and earnings for both buyers and sellers. If sellers do not offer rebates, efficiency can be even lower than in the no-rebate, high-cost condition. Over time, sellers learn to offer rebates more frequently, further improving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing a mechanism for sellers to precommit to (partially) covering buyers' feedback costs increases efficiency in a repeated trust game with feedback, especially when sellers frequently use the mechanism. The effect is strongest when the rebate is offered often, and the mechanism works even if the rebate is less than the full reporting cost. For prediction, if a game design allows for a precommitment rebate mechanism and sellers are likely to use it, efficiency should be higher than in a comparable game with high feedback costs and no rebate. The control efficiency (with high feedback cost and no rebate) is a lower bound; the treatment efficiency (with rebate) will be higher, approaching the efficiency of the low-cost or automatic feedback conditions as rebate frequency increases. The findings are most directly relevant to trust/reputation games with feedback as a public good, and less directly to standard PGGs or punishment games, but the mechanism is analogous to reward/precommitment interventions in PGGs.

- source: Riedl, A., Rohde, I. M. T., & Strobel, M. (2016). Efficient Coordination in Weakest-Link Games. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated weakest-link games, groups with fixed interaction structure (all interact with all) rapidly converge to inefficient equilibria, especially as group size increases. In contrast, when players can choose their interaction neighbourhoods (i.e., whom to interact with), groups quickly achieve and sustain near-optimal efficiency and welfare, even in groups of 24. The key mechanism is that high-effort players exclude low-effort players, who then increase their effort to regain inclusion. This exclusion is often costly to both parties, resembling costly punishment, but the effect persists even when exclusion is not materially costly (i.e., in a 'self-protection' treatment). The results are robust to severe reductions in information, alternative payoff functions, and large group sizes. The findings suggest that enabling endogenous partner choice is a powerful mechanism for achieving efficient coordination in weakest-link environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling endogenous neighbourhood choice (partner selection) in weakest-link games leads to large, robust increases in group efficiency and welfare, compared to fixed-group baselines. The effect is observed across a range of group sizes (8 and 24), information conditions, and payoff structures. The mechanism is exclusion of low-effort members, which induces higher effort and thus higher group payoffs. Importantly, the effect does not depend on exclusion being materially costly (punishment per se is not required). For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of endogenous partner choice is a critical moderator of the efficiency impact of exclusion/punishment-like mechanisms in weakest-link (and possibly related public goods) environments. However, the results are for weakest-link (minimum effort) games, which are adjacent but not identical to standard linear public goods games.

- source: Lacomba, J. A., Lagos, F., Reuben, E., & van Winden, F. (2014). On the escalation and de-escalation of conflict. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in one-shot or randomly matched (Strangers) conflict games, conflict expenditures are high and escalate over time, leading to low efficiency (mean earnings well below the endowment). When losers can destroy resources (akin to punishment), conflict expenditures decrease, and in the 'Resistance' game (where destruction can be conditioned on the winner's appropriation), mean earnings are significantly higher than in the baseline. However, destruction itself is wasteful and only increases efficiency if it deters appropriation and reduces conflict. In repeated interaction (Partners), a substantial minority of groups achieve long-lasting peace (zero conflict, high efficiency), but most do not. Peace is achieved through costly signaling (not fighting, low appropriation), not through destruction. Overall, the effect of punishment-like destruction on efficiency is mixed: it can increase efficiency by reducing conflict, but can also reduce it if destruction is frequent. The design dimensions (matching protocol, post-conflict options) strongly moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, the ability of losers to destroy resources) on efficiency in conflict games is highly context-dependent. In one-shot or random-matching settings, allowing punishment (destruction) can reduce conflict expenditures and, if destruction is conditional on appropriation, can increase efficiency. However, if destruction is frequent, efficiency may not improve. In repeated games, the possibility of peace (high efficiency) depends more on costly signaling and restraint than on punishment. For prediction, the presence of a punishment mechanism (destruction) is likely to reduce conflict and increase efficiency only if it credibly deters appropriation and is not overused. The matching protocol (repeated vs. random) and the structure of post-conflict options are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is low in these games; enabling punishment can increase efficiency in some variants (notably Resistance/RE), but not always.

- source: Barker, J. L., & Barclay, P. (2016). Local competition increases people's willingness to harm others. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that when competition is local (i.e., participants compete for prizes within small groups), people are more willing to pay a cost to harm others, resulting in lower group and individual payoffs (efficiency). In contrast, under global competition (competing against all participants), less money is spent on harming, and payoffs are higher. This effect holds whether endowments are earned or given. The findings are robust across three studies, including one where participants could harm both ingroup and outgroup members: under local competition, harming is targeted at ingroup members (the direct competitors), further reducing within-group efficiency. The results are interpreted as a 'tragedy of the commons' driven by local competition, where wasteful harming reduces overall welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in economic games where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs (money-burning), the structure of competition (local vs global) is a critical moderator of efficiency. When competition is local, efficiency is much lower due to increased costly harming, even when the cost-effectiveness of harming is high (burning $1 to reduce $4). The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of local competition (small group contests) should be expected to strongly reduce efficiency when punishment/harming is possible, compared to global competition. The results are robust to whether endowments are earned or given, and to whether harming is targeted at ingroup or outgroup members. However, the game is not a standard public goods game or punishment game: the main action is costly harming, not punishment for norm violation or contribution decisions. Still, the efficiency implications are directly relevant for settings where costly sanctions are possible.

- source: Camera, G., & Casari, M. (2014). The Coordination Value of Monetary Exchange: Experimental Evidence. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated, anonymous helping game with four players, introducing a fiat money-like mechanism (tickets) does not increase the maximum attainable efficiency but changes how cooperation is coordinated. In the baseline (no tickets), realized efficiency is 48.2% of the maximum. With tickets, overall efficiency is 46.8%, not significantly different from baseline. However, when ticket transfer is feasible, cooperation and efficiency rise to 61.4%; when not feasible, they drop to 12.5%. The presence of tickets leads to the emergence of a monetary exchange strategy, increases the predictability of cooperative behavior, and redistributes surplus from frequent defectors to frequent cooperators. The main effect of the monetary mechanism is to facilitate coordination and reduce strategic uncertainty, not to increase aggregate efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a monetary-like mechanism (tickets) in a repeated, anonymous, decentralized helping game does not increase overall efficiency compared to a baseline with only decentralized punishment, but it does change the distribution and predictability of cooperation. The effect of the monetary mechanism is highly contingent on whether the mechanism is feasible in a given match. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of a monetary or token-based exchange mechanism may not increase group efficiency if the baseline already allows for decentralized punishment and the efficiency frontier is not expanded. However, such mechanisms can facilitate coordination, increase the stability of cooperation, and redistribute surplus toward cooperators. The results are most relevant for environments where punishment is decentralized and impersonal, and where direct reciprocity and reputation are absent.

- source: Agranov, M., Fréchette, G., Palfrey, T., & Vespa, E. (2016). Static and dynamic underinvestment: An experimental investigation. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that underprovision of the public good is substantial in both static and dynamic settings, with dynamic inefficiency (the additional inefficiency due to intertemporal linkage) increasing as the survival rate of the public good rises. When the public good is highly durable, dynamic inefficiency accounts for a large share of total inefficiency. Subjects use both minimum winning coalition (MWC) strategies and strategic cooperation, including informal punishment (exclusion from coalitions) to reward or punish past behavior. However, the game does not include a formal punishment technology (e.g., costly punishment as in standard PGGs). Welfare losses are decomposed into static and dynamic components, and the results show that dynamic inefficiency is empirically significant and increases with the strength of the dynamic link. The findings are robust across different strategy types and are consistent with theoretical predictions, though the magnitude of inefficiency is somewhat less than predicted by theory.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how dynamic linkages and coalition bargaining affect efficiency in public good provision, especially in legislative or committee settings. However, it does not manipulate or include a formal punishment mechanism as in standard PGGs. Instead, it studies endogenous, informal punishment via coalition exclusion. Therefore, while the results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in dynamic, coalition-based public goods environments, they are only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of adding formal punishment to a standard PGG. The main lesson is that efficiency is sensitive to dynamic linkages and coalition rules, and that informal punishment can partially substitute for formal mechanisms, but the quantitative effect of formal punishment cannot be inferred from this study.

- source: Martin, J. M., Gonzalez, C., Juvina, I., & Lebiere, C. (2014). A Description-Experience Gap in Social Interactions: Information about Interdependence and Its Effects on Cooperation. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that providing more information about interdependence in a two-player, 200-round IPD increases both mutual cooperation and joint payoffs (efficiency). Four information conditions were tested: No-Info (players unaware of the other), Min-Info (aware of other but cannot see their actions), Mid-Info (can see other's actions and outcomes), and Max-Info (can also see the full payoff matrix). Joint performance (sum of points) was lowest in No-Info and Min-Info, higher in Mid-Info, and highest in Max-Info. The effect of information on performance is fully mediated by mutual cooperation. Punishment (defection after defection) is high in all conditions and does not vary with information. The results suggest that awareness and transparency of interdependence are key to achieving higher efficiency in repeated social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated dyadic social dilemmas structurally similar to the IPD, increasing the amount of information about interdependence (especially showing others' actions and the payoff matrix) leads to higher efficiency (joint payoff). For prediction, if a public-goods-like game allows players to see others' actions and/or the payoff structure, one should expect higher efficiency than in games where such information is hidden. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, simultaneous-move games without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, but the mechanisms (reciprocation, not punishment) may generalize to other settings. The paper does not manipulate punishment cost or technology, nor does it include explicit costly punishment or reward options, so its direct predictive value is limited for games with those features.

- source: Hilbe, C., Röhl, T., & Milinski, M. (2014). Extortion subdues human players but is finally punished in the prisoner's dilemma. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a 60-round repeated prisoner's dilemma, human subjects played against computer opponents using either extortionate or generous zero-determinant (ZD) strategies. Extortionate strategies succeeded in obtaining higher payoffs than their human co-players, but both parties earned less in absolute terms than in the generosity treatments. Human players punished extortion by reducing cooperation, even at a cost to themselves, leading to lower overall efficiency. Generous strategies, in contrast, elicited higher cooperation and resulted in higher payoffs for both parties. The study demonstrates that implicit costly punishment (withholding cooperation) can limit the success of exploitative strategies and reduce group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated dyadic prisoner's dilemma games without explicit punishment mechanisms, the presence of exploitative (extortionate) strategies leads to lower efficiency than more cooperative (generous) strategies. Human players respond to perceived unfairness by reducing cooperation, which acts as an implicit costly punishment and reduces total payoffs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling mechanisms that allow for punishment (even implicit, via withholding cooperation) can reduce efficiency if they are used in response to exploitation. The findings are most directly relevant to two-player, repeated, all-or-nothing contribution games with hidden horizon and no explicit communication or punishment options. The results highlight the importance of fairness concerns and the potential for costly punishment to reduce group welfare, even in the absence of formal punishment institutions.

- source: Ahn, T. K., Balafoutas, L., Batsaikhan, M., Campos-Ortiz, F., Putterman, L., & Sutter, M. (2016). Securing property rights: A dilemma experiment in Austria, Mexico, Mongolia, South Korea and the United States. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: This paper studies a property rights dilemma game in which subjects allocate effort among production, theft, and protection. Three institutional treatments are compared: (1) no collective protection (NCP, baseline), (2) voluntary collective protection (VCP), and (3) collective protection by majority vote (VOTE, mandatory if chosen). Efficiency, measured as group earnings relative to the social optimum, is lowest in NCP (24.6%), higher in VCP (36.6%), and highest in VOTE (48.0%). The VOTE treatment achieves the highest efficiency when the mandatory scheme is adopted, but this occurs in only 64% of opportunities. The efficiency gains from collective protection are robust across five countries, but the magnitude varies with national trust levels. The results show that institutional mechanisms for collective protection (analogous to public enforcement or taxation) can substantially increase efficiency in social dilemmas involving appropriation, but their effectiveness depends on both institutional design and cultural context.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of introducing a collective protection or sanctioning institution in a social dilemma with appropriation (theft), this paper provides direct evidence that such institutions increase efficiency, with mandatory (voted) collective protection yielding the largest gains. The efficiency increase from baseline to voluntary collective protection is modest but significant, while the increase to mandatory collective protection is larger but not maximal due to incomplete adoption and suboptimal implementation. The design dimensions most relevant are the presence and type of collective protection, the method of funding (voluntary vs. mandatory), and the cultural context (trust). While the game is not a standard PGG, the efficiency results are closely analogous and informative for settings where punishment or public enforcement is introduced. The findings suggest that enabling effective, enforceable collective protection (akin to punishment) is likely to increase efficiency, but the realized gains may be less than theoretical predictions due to behavioral and cultural factors.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Barclay, P. (2016). Exploring the trade-off between quality and fairness in human partner choice. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: When given a choice between a rich-stingy and a poor-fair partner in a modified Dictator Game, choosers often select the fair partner, even at a cost to their own expected pay-off. This tendency is stronger when the wealth of partners is unstable. When both partners are equally fair or unfair, choosers prefer the richer partner, aligning with maximizing expected pay-off. The findings indicate that fairness is prioritized over ability (wealth) in partner choice, especially under uncertainty, and that individuals are willing to incur personal costs to avoid rewarding unfairness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in environments where individuals can choose partners, people may sacrifice efficiency (personal or group payoff) to avoid unfair partners, especially when partner quality is unstable. While not a public goods game, the findings are relevant for predicting that the introduction of partner choice or similar sanctioning mechanisms may not always increase efficiency—if individuals use these mechanisms to punish unfairness even at a cost to themselves. The results suggest that the effect of punishment or exclusion on efficiency may be less positive or even negative when individuals are motivated by fairness rather than payoff maximization.

- source: dos Santos, M., Placì, S., & Wedekind, C. (2015). Stochasticity in economic losses increases the value of reputation in indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that stochasticity in economic losses does not increase mean helping rates or mean group earnings, but it does increase the payoff advantage of having a good reputation. Under stochastic losses, generous players are helped more and selfish players are helped less, leading to a steeper positive relationship between generosity and final earnings. In stable environments, this relationship is absent. Thus, stochasticity increases the efficiency of reputation-based cooperation by making investment in reputation more beneficial, even though overall group efficiency does not increase.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in repeated helping games with indirect reciprocity, environmental stochasticity (randomness in losses) increases the payoff return to cooperative behavior (reputation), even if mean group efficiency does not change. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, reputation-based exclusion or helping) on efficiency may be more positive in environments with stochastic payoffs or risks, because the social system more effectively rewards cooperators and penalizes defectors. However, the game is not a standard public goods game and does not feature explicit costly punishment; rather, it is an indirect reciprocity helping game. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation or exclusion is the main sanctioning mechanism, and where environmental risk is present.

- source: D'Exelle, B., & Riedl, A. (2013). Resource Allocations and Disapproval Voting in Unequal Groups. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Disapproval voting as a punishment mechanism in unequal groups does not consistently improve efficiency or prosocial allocations. Instead, its effectiveness is highly sensitive to institutional details. When villagers have only private information about their share and voting is public, disapproval rates are low and representatives often use a minimal coalition (exclude-one) strategy, which avoids disapproval and maintains higher group earnings, though at the cost of excluding the poorest villager. When allocations are common knowledge or voting is secret, disapproval rates are higher, leading representatives to frequently switch to the exclude-all strategy, which is inefficient as it maximizes the representative's payoff at the expense of the group. The punishment mechanism can thus backfire, reducing efficiency, especially when disapproval is frequent. The study highlights the importance of information structure and voting rules in moderating the effects of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment mechanisms (here, disapproval voting) on efficiency in resource allocation games is not universally positive and depends critically on the design dimensions, especially information disclosure and the publicity of voting. In settings where punishment is possible but disapproval is rare (private information, public voting), efficiency is higher, but this is achieved by excluding the least powerful group member. In settings where punishment is more likely (common information, secret voting), efficiency is lower due to frequent costly disapproval and representatives' defensive strategies. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the effect is moderated by how information and punishment are structured. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not reported, but the treatment effects indicate that punishment can reduce efficiency if it triggers defensive, exclusionary, or retaliatory behavior by the allocator.

- source: Rand, D. G., Fudenberg, D., & Dreber, A. (2015). It's the thought that counts: The role of intentions in noisy repeated games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with implementation errors, making intentions observable (explicit-intentions treatment) leads to significantly higher cooperation rates and group payoffs than when intentions are unobservable (implicit-intentions control). The cooperation and payoff levels in the explicit-intentions treatment are statistically indistinguishable from those in error-free games. Most subjects use simple, intention-based strategies, and the negative impact of noise on cooperation is eliminated when intentions are observable. The effect holds for both high and low benefit/cost ratios. Expected payoffs for the most common intention-based strategies are the highest among all strategies, and the overall group payoff is maximized in the explicit-intentions and no-error conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with noise, making intentions observable can fully restore efficiency (group payoff) to the level seen in error-free games. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the negative effect of noise on efficiency can be mitigated or eliminated if the game design allows players to observe each other's intentions. While the setting is not a public goods game per se, the findings are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with binary choices and noise. The paper does not include explicit punishment mechanisms, but the analysis of punitive and tolerant strategies provides indirect evidence about sanctioning behavior. The main design dimensions relevant for prediction are the presence of noise, observability of intentions, and the benefit/cost ratio. Other dimensions (e.g., player count, chat, reward, punishment cost) are not varied or present.

- source: Diekmann, A., Jann, B., Przepiorka, W., & Wehrli, S. (2014). Reputation Formation and the Evolution of Cooperation in Anonymous Online Markets. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: Using large-scale eBay auction data, the study shows that sellers with more positive feedback (reputation) are more likely to sell their items and receive higher prices, while negative feedback reduces both outcomes. The effect of negative feedback is stronger than that of positive feedback. Participation in the feedback system is high, and feedback behavior is consistent with strong reciprocity and altruism, which helps maintain the reputation system. The reputation system thus mitigates trust problems and supports cooperation in one-shot, anonymous exchanges, increasing overall market efficiency and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that reputation-based feedback systems in anonymous, one-shot exchange environments (adjacent to PGGs) increase efficiency by incentivizing cooperative behavior. While not a direct PGG or punishment experiment, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing or strengthening reputation/feedback mechanisms in similar environments: efficiency (measured as successful trade and higher prices) increases when reputation mechanisms are active and well-participated. The effect is stronger for negative feedback (punishment) than for positive feedback (reward). The study also highlights the importance of high participation in the feedback system, which is sustained by reciprocity and altruism. For prediction, the presence of a reputation/feedback mechanism (even if not formal monetary punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a baseline with no such mechanism.

- source: Nakashima, N. A., Halali, E., & Halevy, N. (2017). Third parties promote cooperative norms in repeated interactions. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third party intervention in repeated adversarial interactions (modeled as a Peacemaker Game) increases cooperation rates among adversaries. Early intervention is more effective at establishing persistent cooperative norms than late intervention. Third parties are more likely to intervene when adversaries have a history of cooperation, and adversaries are more likely to cooperate following third party intervention. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and intervention rates), not efficiency or group payoff. The study provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which combine reward and punishment can transform competitive repeated interactions into more cooperative ones, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling third-party interventions (which combine reward and punishment) can increase cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation and intervention rates, not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as suggestive rather than directly predictive for efficiency outcomes. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games, and the intervention mechanism is not a standard peer punishment or reward system. The results suggest that early, consistent intervention is more effective at establishing cooperation than late intervention, and that the presence of a third party who can both reward and punish can transform group behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that enabling such mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency is not directly reported.

- source: Gillenkirch, R. M., & Kreienbaum, H. (2017). What guides subjective performance evaluation: Incentive alignment or norm enforcement?. *Review of Managerial Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that superiors are primarily motivated by norm enforcement: they pay more for information that allows them to reward high and punish low effort, and their allocation decisions reflect a willingness to punish norm violations. Subordinates increase their effort when they know their effort can be observed and evaluated. The study does not directly report group efficiency or total surplus, but higher effort levels in conditions with more informative evaluation suggest that group output and subordinate payoffs increase when norm enforcement is possible. However, the focus is on behavioral mechanisms (effort, information acquisition, allocation) rather than on efficiency as a formal outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling punishment (via subjective evaluation and information acquisition) increases effort and likely increases group output in a team production setting with a free rider problem. However, it does not report efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, nor does it use a standard public goods game. The design is adjacent to PGGs: two subordinates choose effort (costly), output is the sum of efforts plus noise, and a superior (not a group member) allocates the output. The 'punishment' is implemented via allocation decisions, not explicit costly punishment. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that mechanisms enabling norm enforcement (punishment) can increase effort and likely improve group outcomes, but the lack of direct efficiency data and the non-standard game structure limit its direct applicability to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Halevy, N., & Halali, E. (2015). Selfish third parties act as peacemakers by transforming conflicts and promoting cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) The possibility and higher probability of third-party intervention increase cooperation rates among disputants in both interpersonal and intergroup contexts. (2) Third parties' willingness to intervene is highly sensitive to the risk and potential rewards of intervention; reducing risk or increasing potential gain increases intervention rates. (3) Despite the potential for increased collective gains, third parties often intervene less frequently than would be optimal, leading to missed opportunities for higher group payoffs. (4) The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as primary outcomes, but discusses them in the context of missed opportunities and the structure of the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the introduction of a punishment/reward mechanism (via third-party intervention) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma-like settings, even when the underlying game is not a standard public goods game.
