However, because the primary outcomes are behavioral (cooperation/intervention rates) and not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that the design of punishment/reward mechanisms (including the risk and incentive structure for enforcers) is critical for their effectiveness. The evidence is most relevant for adjacent settings where third-party intervention is possible, but less directly transferable to standard PGGs with endogenous peer punishment.

- source: Salmon, T. C., & Serra, D. (2017). Corruption, social judgment and culture: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing social observability and allowing for social judgment (informal sanctions in the form of approval/disapproval messages) reduces the likelihood of corrupt behavior (offering or accepting bribes) among participants who identify with low-corruption cultures, but not among those who identify with high-corruption cultures. The effect is driven by differences in the likelihood of social disapproval: participants from high-corruption cultures are less likely to disapprove of corrupt acts. The Victim Knows treatment (where the victim is informed but cannot judge) has little effect. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and the design is a one-shot, role-based bribery game rather than a repeated public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of informal social sanctions (social judgment) on norm-violating behavior in a one-shot, negative-externality game. While not a public goods game, it is relevant for understanding the limits of informal punishment/sanction mechanisms, especially their dependence on cultural context. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of informal sanctions in reducing norm violations (and thus potentially increasing efficiency) is highly contingent on prevailing social norms and expectations of disapproval. However, because the game is not repeated, does not feature voluntary contributions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct predictive value for public goods game efficiency is limited. It is most informative for settings where informal social sanctions are the main enforcement mechanism and where cultural heterogeneity is a concern.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Mui, V. L. (2014). Coordinating Resistance Through Communication and Repeated Interaction. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that repetition alone only modestly reduces leader DAC (transgression) rates, as leaders can punish beneficiaries who resist, deterring future resistance. Communication between responders, even when non-binding and private, substantially increases the rate of coordinated resistance and reduces leader DAC. The combination of repetition and communication is most effective at reducing DAC. The study also shows that hiding responder identities from the leader (so the leader cannot target challenging beneficiaries) increases resistance. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total surplus, or group payoff as a main outcome, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The results are most relevant for understanding how communication and repeated interaction affect coordination in social dilemmas with endogenous roles and targeted punishment, rather than standard public goods games with peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the importance of communication and repeated interaction for coordinated resistance in social dilemmas with endogenous roles. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that communication between potential punishers (or resisters) can be as important as, or more important than, repetition for deterring exploitative behavior. The results highlight that the ability to target punishers (e.g., identifying who punished) can undermine the effectiveness of resistance. For prediction tasks in public goods games, the main lesson is that communication channels and anonymity of punishment/reward can strongly moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, efficiency. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes, but it does suggest that communication is a powerful moderator.

- source: Kuwabara, K. (2015). Do Reputation Systems Undermine Trust? Divergent Effects of Enforcement Type on Generalized Trust and Trustworthiness. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Reputation systems, as a form of diffuse, non-material enforcement, reinforce generalized trust and trustworthiness more than contractual (material punishment) or relational (repeated partner) enforcement. After exposure to reputational enforcement, participants are more trusting and trustworthy in subsequent unenforced exchanges than after exposure to contracts or repeated partners. Contractual enforcement, in contrast, can undermine intrinsic motivation to trust or be trustworthy once enforcement is removed. These effects are robust across multiple studies and are supported by both behavioral and self-report measures. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the experimental paradigm is the investment game, not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment-like enforcement (contracts) and reputation systems on subsequent cooperation, but not on efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that material punishment (contracts) can undermine intrinsic motivation for cooperation, while reputation systems can reinforce it. However, because the outcomes are trust/trustworthiness (not efficiency) and the game is not a public goods game, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The evidence is more relevant for understanding how enforcement mechanisms affect willingness to cooperate after enforcement is removed, rather than direct effects on group efficiency during enforcement.

- source: Leppänen, I., & Hämäläinen, R. P. (2017). Emotions in a Repeated Cournot Duopoly Game: A Psychophysiological Experiment. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that emotional responses are closely tied to reciprocal adjustments in a repeated Cournot duopoly game. When a player observes that the other did not cooperate (i.e., the other increased production), the player is more likely to make an upward adjustment (interpreted as punishment), and the size of this adjustment is positively correlated with emotional arousal. Disgust is associated with advantageous inequity (own one-sided gain), and positive affect is associated with mutual gains. The study provides evidence that emotions play a role in motivating reciprocal (including punitive) behavior in repeated social dilemmas, but it does not directly measure or report the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. The Cournot duopoly is adjacent to public goods games, and punishment is implemented as endogenous behavioral adjustment rather than as a discrete, explicit punishment option.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the emotional and motivational underpinnings of reciprocal (including punitive) behavior in repeated social dilemmas with continuous strategy spaces. It suggests that endogenous punishment-like responses (upward adjustments after defection) are emotionally motivated and can be costly for both players, potentially reducing efficiency. However, because the study does not manipulate explicit punishment mechanisms or report efficiency outcomes as a function of punishment, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding the psychological processes underlying endogenous punishment in adjacent game structures, but should not be used as direct quantitative evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Cuesta, J. A., Gracia-Lázaro, C., Ferrer, A., Moreno, Y., & Sánchez, A. (2015). Reputation drives cooperative behaviour and network formation in human groups. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that providing information about others' past actions (reputation/memory) leads to higher and more stable cooperation rates in a dynamic network Prisoner's Dilemma. When no memory is available, cooperation decays and the network becomes highly connected (as players have no reason to avoid defectors). With memory, players break links with defectors, leading to lower connectivity but higher cooperation. The effect is stronger as the memory window increases. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The 'punishment' mechanism is the breaking of links, not a costly punishment as in standard PGG experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of reputation-based partner selection (link breaking) on cooperation in dynamic network games. While the setting is an iterated Prisoner's Dilemma (not a PGG), and the 'punishment' is not costly, the results suggest that allowing players to sanction defectors by breaking ties (when reputation is available) can sustain higher cooperation. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not measured, and the game is not a PGG, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The main implication is that information about others' behavior (reputation) and the ability to avoid defectors can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not quantified.

- source: Karagonlar, G., & Kuhlman, D. M. (2013). The role of Social Value Orientation in response to an unfair offer in the Ultimatum Game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two lab experiments, the paper finds that Prosocials (as measured by SVO) are less likely to reject an unfair offer (20% of the pie) in the Ultimatum Game than Proselfs. This difference is not due to differences in fairness perceptions or expectations, but is mediated by more positive emotions and more positive attributions toward the proposer among Prosocials. The findings support the view that emotion regulation and cognitive reappraisal, rather than altruistic punishment, drive acceptance of unfair offers among Prosocials. The study does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The Ultimatum Game is structurally different from public goods games, and the outcome of interest is the accept/reject decision rather than group payoff or efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding individual differences in responses to norm violations and costly punishment, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The study does, however, suggest that motivations for punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) may differ by individual orientation, which could be contextually relevant for interpreting punishment effects in other games.

- source: Xiang, T., Lohrenz, T., & Montague, P. R. (2013). Computational Substrates of Norms and Their Violations during Social Exchange. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that social norm expectations, manipulated via preconditioning to low, medium, or high offers in a repeated ultimatum game, strongly affect rejection rates for identical offers. Subjects preconditioned to low offers (unfair) are less likely to reject medium offers than those preconditioned to high offers (hyper-fair), even though the offers are objectively the same. Subjective feelings about offers and neural responses (in regions such as vmPFC, striatum, and anterior insula) track these norm prediction errors. The results support a model in which norm violations, rather than simple payoff inequality, drive punishment behavior (rejection), and that these processes are reflected in both behavior and brain activity. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, and the outcome of interest is not efficiency but rather the psychological and neural mechanisms of norm-based punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, specifically regarding how expectations and norm adaptation modulate punishment (rejection) behavior. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment (here, costly rejection) on behavior is highly sensitive to context and prior expectations, which may inform models of punishment in other social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as contextual or mechanistic evidence about the psychological drivers of punishment, not as a quantitative source for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Camera, G., Casari, M., & Bigoni, M. (2013). EXPERIMENTAL MARKETS WITH FRICTIONS. *Journal of Economic Surveys*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The new experiment shows that allowing free-form chat among randomly matched strangers in an indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma leads to much higher cooperation rates than either no communication or structured, anonymous public messages. Structured communication does not significantly improve cooperation or coordination relative to no communication, likely due to the prevalence of deceptive use of messages. The study finds that public messages are informative about intentions, but deception dilutes their effectiveness. The experiment does not include a punishment treatment, but the literature review summarizes prior findings that peer punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, especially in small groups. The main outcomes reported are behavioral (cooperation rates, communication patterns), not payoff-based (efficiency or group earnings).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. The experimental design is an indefinitely repeated, random-matching prisoner's dilemma among strangers, which is adjacent to public goods games. The main manipulated dimension is communication: free-form chat vs. structured messages vs. no communication. The findings suggest that free-form communication can strongly increase cooperation, but structured, anonymous communication does not. The experiment does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The literature review section summarizes prior work showing that peer punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, but these are not tested in the new experiment. Thus, this paper is most informative about the effect of communication (chat) and monitoring (public/anonymous) on cooperation, and only indirectly about punishment or efficiency. It should be used as contextual or indirect evidence for prediction, especially regarding the role of communication and monitoring, but not as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Bednarik, P., Fehl, K., & Semmann, D. (2014). Costs for switching partners reduce network dynamics but not cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing costs for forming new links in dynamic networks reduces the rate at which participants break links (i.e., switch partners). However, cooperation rates in the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma remain high across all dynamic treatments, regardless of the cost level, and are significantly higher than in static networks where no partner switching is allowed. The ability to break links—even if rarely used—appears to be enough to maintain high cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of these dynamics on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with dynamic partner networks, the introduction of costs for switching partners reduces network dynamism but does not reduce cooperation rates. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment or partner-switching mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that the mere threat of partner switching can sustain cooperation, but the efficiency implications (e.g., total group payoff relative to maximum possible) remain unaddressed.

- source: Harth, N. S., & Regner, T. (2017). The spiral of distrust: (Non-)cooperation in a repeated trust game is predicted by anger and individual differences in negative reciprocity orientation. *International Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that senders who receive feedback about low back transfers from trustees experience increased anger, which in turn leads to reduced cooperation (lower amounts sent) in subsequent rounds. Individuals with a high negative reciprocity orientation are more prone to anger and are less willing to cooperate, even in the absence of anger-eliciting feedback. The interaction between anger and negative reciprocity orientation further reduces cooperation. The absence of feedback results in lower anger and relatively higher cooperation, especially among those low in negative reciprocity orientation. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it studies a repeated trust game without any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (amount sent/cooperation) and emotional (anger), not payoff-based. However, it provides indirect evidence that negative emotions and individual differences in reciprocity orientation can reduce cooperation in repeated interactions, especially when feedback about others' behavior is available. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only weakly informative and should not be weighted heavily unless the prediction context is specifically about trust games or the psychological mechanisms of cooperation.

- source: Zhen, S. S., & Yu, R. J. (2016). Tend to Compare and Tend to Be Fair: The Relationship between Social Comparison Sensitivity and Justice Sensitivity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds a positive correlation between social comparison sensitivity and justice sensitivity, both in behavioral tasks (UG and SCG) and in self-report inventories. In the UG, participants are more likely to reject disadvantageous offers, and this tendency is associated with their behavior in the SCG. The results suggest that social comparison motives underlie fairness-related decisions, particularly the rejection of unfair offers. However, the study does not include a public goods game, does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a separate stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a modified ultimatum game (dyadic, proposer-responder) and a social comparison game, focusing on individual differences in fairness and comparison sensitivity. There is no group interaction, no punishment stage as in PGGs, and no efficiency or group payoff data. The findings may be relevant for understanding motives behind rejection (which can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment), but the lack of group-level payoff outcomes and absence of a PGG or punishment-enabled/control design means this paper offers only indirect, limited support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Han, X., Cao, S. N., Shen, Z. S., Zhang, B. Y., Wang, W. X., Cressman, R., & Stanley, H. E. (2017). Emergence of communities and diversity in social networks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in static network structures (both regular and random bipartite networks), proposers form stable local communities with diverse offers, while in randomly rewired networks, proposers become homogeneous. The diversity in offers (SD of p) is much higher in structured networks. Responders' behavior is heterogeneous but not affected by network structure. The mechanism is that rational proposers best-respond to their local responder 'leaders,' whose heterogeneous minimum acceptance levels drive local agreement. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or surplus, and does not include a public goods game or explicit costly punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experiment uses the ultimatum game, where 'punishment' is the rejection of offers, not a costly sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (offer diversity, community formation), not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. It may provide some indirect context about how local interaction structure and heterogeneity can affect behavioral diversity, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Jones, M. T. (2014). Strategic complexity and cooperation: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Increasing the complexity of strategies required to sustain cooperation in an indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma reduces the frequency of cooperation and increases the prevalence of simple selfish strategies. This effect is observed both when the cooperate action and when the defect action are made state-dependent, increasing the information processing required for cooperation or punishment, respectively. The study also finds that higher cognitive ability is associated with greater cooperation in the baseline, but this advantage is reduced when complexity is increased. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral measures such as cooperation rates and strategy inference.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the cognitive or strategic complexity of sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas reduces cooperation rates, and by implication, would likely reduce efficiency or group payoff if measured. However, the study is not a public goods game and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on endogenous punishment via repeated game strategies (e.g., Grim Trigger). The findings are most relevant for predicting how complexity or cognitive burden might moderate the effect of punishment or cooperation-supporting mechanisms in repeated games, but do not provide direct evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games. Use this paper as informative indirect evidence about the role of complexity and cognitive ability in sustaining cooperation, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Brevers, D., Noël, X., Ermer, E., Dabiri, D., Verbanck, P., & Kornreich, C. (2013). Unfairness sensitivity and social decision-making in individuals with alcohol dependence: A preliminary study. *Drug and Alcohol Dependence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Alcohol-dependent individuals are more likely to reject unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game compared to healthy controls, suggesting greater emotional reactivity to unfairness and reduced ability to make self-advantageous decisions in social contexts involving frustration. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and the design does not include a punishment phase or a repeated public goods structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The Ultimatum Game is adjacent to the target environment, as rejection of unfair offers can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment, but the context is one-shot, dyadic, and does not involve group cooperation or repeated interactions. The findings may inform understanding of individual differences in punishment sensitivity, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or the effect of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.

- source: Tanaka, H., Ohtsuki, H., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2016). The price of being seen to be just: an intention signalling strategy for indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that participants in a dyadic donation game use a costly signalling option (abandoning a resource) to manage their reputation, especially after justified defection or implementation error. Others interpret this signal as a sign of cooperative intent and are more likely to cooperate with signallers. The signalling option allows players to maintain a good reputation without requiring second-order information. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that such signalling can stabilize cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The experimental design is a dyadic donation game, not a multi-player PGG, and the 'punishment' is a costly self-imposed signal (abandoning a resource) rather than a punishment directed at others. The main outcomes are behavioral (signalling and cooperation rates), not efficiency or payoff. However, the findings suggest that providing players with a costly signalling option can help maintain cooperation in the absence of second-order information, which may be relevant for understanding mechanisms that stabilize cooperation in related environments. The results do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Ma, L. K., Tunney, R. J., & Ferguson, E. (2014). Gratefully Received, Gratefully Repaid: The Role of Perceived Fairness in Cooperative Interactions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that recipients of unconditional (free) help perceive the donor as more genuinely helpful, feel more gratitude, and report a higher tendency to reciprocate in the future compared to recipients of conditional (repayment-required) help. Among those who received conditional offers and accepted, 38% defaulted (either partially or fully) on repayment, suggesting a behavioral response to perceived unfairness. The effect of offer conditionality on willingness to reciprocate is serially mediated by perceived helpfulness and gratitude. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and the game is a one-shot, dyadic trust game rather than a repeated public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how conditionality (akin to punishment or sanctions) affects willingness to reciprocate and actual repayment behavior in a one-shot, dyadic trust game. It does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that perceived fairness and the framing of help (conditional vs. unconditional) can influence cooperation and defaulting, which may be relevant for understanding psychological mechanisms in cooperation, but the results cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public goods games with punishment enabled or disabled.

- source: Schram, A., & Charness, G. (2015). Inducing Social Norms in Laboratory Allocation Choices. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that providing impartial advice about what one 'ought to do' (shared understanding) only affects behavior when choices are made public, i.e., when social (not just moral) norms are salient. In the advice + public condition, dictators are significantly less selfish than in other conditions. Advice alone (private) does not change behavior, nor does public observability alone without advice. The effect is interpreted as the creation of a social norm, enforced by the threat of social disapproval (shame), not by material punishment. There is no direct measurement or analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus. The findings are relevant for understanding norm induction and the role of observability, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or similar environments. It does not implement punishment, rewards, or repeated interaction, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide informative context about how social norms (via advice and observability) can shift behavior toward less selfish choices in allocation games. For prediction tasks, the main takeaway is that nonmaterial social sanctions (public observability) combined with shared understanding can induce prosocial behavior, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes and the lack of a PGG or punishment mechanism limit its direct applicability.

- source: Frey, V., & de Rijt, A. V. (2016). Arbitrary Inequality in Reputation Systems. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when trustors can observe the outcomes of others' exchanges (information sharing), they are more likely to repeatedly select the same trustee, leading to 'reputation cascades' and increasing inequality in how often trustees are chosen. This effect is strongest when there is a risk of trust abuse (the 'trust problem' condition). The paper shows that these cascades are arbitrary, as trustees with similar trustworthiness can end up with very different exchange volumes. While the paper discusses payoffs in the game structure, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The main focus is on behavioral dynamics of trust and reputation, not on efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of information sharing and reputation systems on behavioral outcomes in trust-based exchange games. It does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods or punishment games, as it does not report these outcomes. However, it suggests that increased information sharing can increase trust and exchange, but also leads to arbitrary inequality in participation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as the game structure and outcomes differ from standard PGGs and do not include punishment or efficiency as primary outcomes.

- source: Rand, D. G., Brescoll, V. L., Everett, J. A. C., Capraro, V., & Barcelo, H. (2016). Social Heuristics and Social Roles: Intuition Favors Altruism for Women but Not for Men. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that promoting intuition (via time pressure, conceptual priming, or cognitive load) increases altruistic giving in the Dictator Game for women, but not for men. This effect is further moderated by gender role identification: women who identify with masculine attributes become less altruistic when deliberating, while those who identify with feminine attributes are unaffected by deliberation. Men are generally less altruistic, and their giving is not significantly affected by cognitive processing mode or gender role identification. The study does not involve strategic interaction, punishment, or group efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is the Dictator Game, which is a unilateral giving game with no strategic interaction, no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and no group payoff or efficiency concept. While the paper discusses social norms and the internalization of altruism (and mentions that women are punished more for failing to be altruistic), it does not experimentally manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report any efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as very indirect context about gender, social heuristics, and norm compliance.

- source: Kandori, M., & Obayashi, S. (2014). Labor union members play an OLG repeated game. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that cooperation (helping behavior) among union members is sustained not by explicit punishment, perfect monitoring, or reputation, but by a focal-point equilibrium where members help others in expectation of reciprocal help when they need it. Most members leave the union after their own disputes are resolved, indicating that helping is conditional and self-interested rather than purely altruistic. The equilibrium is supported by direct observation and mutual monitoring during joint activities, but not by formal sanctions or payoff-based incentives. The paper provides qualitative evidence and a theoretical model, but does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar laboratory environments. It provides qualitative, contextual evidence about how cooperation can be sustained in a real-world OLG setting with limited observability, but does not report or manipulate efficiency, payoff, or formal punishment mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the range of mechanisms that can support cooperation in loosely knit groups, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Glowacki, L., & von Rueden, C. (2015). Leadership solves collective action problems in small-scale societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in both the Tsimane and Nyangatom societies, leaders tend to have more 'capital' (age, strength, social connections), and that leadership is more likely to become institutionalized in larger, denser, or more conflict-prone communities. Leadership is associated with improved group coordination and conflict mediation, and leaders often incur higher costs (e.g., risk in battle, time spent mediating). However, there is no direct evidence that leaders or leadership increase group efficiency or total payoffs in a way that maps onto public goods game outcomes. The paper notes that leader-based monitoring and sanctioning may reduce free-riding and improve the efficiency of reward or punishment allocation, but this is discussed in general terms and not measured as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for understanding the social and ecological conditions under which leadership, monitoring, and sanctioning emerge to solve collective action problems. It provides indirect support for the idea that leadership (and by extension, centralized punishment or monitoring) can improve group coordination and reduce free-riding, especially as group size and conflict increase. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on the efficiency impact of punishment in controlled game environments, this paper offers only contextual and theoretical guidance, not direct empirical input.

- source: Simpson, B., Harrell, A., & Willer, R. (2013). Hidden Paths from Morality to Cooperation: Moral Judgments Promote Trust and Trustworthiness. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three experiments, the authors show that (1) making moral judgments of others' immoral behavior increases subsequent trustworthiness in a trust game (measured by amount returned), (2) this effect is mediated by increased self-perceived moral identity, and (3) observers trust and prefer to interact with strong moral judges, perceiving them as more moral and trustworthy. The behavioral outcomes are individual-level trust and trustworthiness, not group-level efficiency or payoff. The design does not include public goods games, explicit punishment, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are adjacent: it shows that making moral judgments (not material punishment) can increase trustworthiness in trust games, and that observers trust moral judges more. However, there is no manipulation of punishment, no public goods game, and no group payoff or efficiency outcome. The results may be relevant for understanding how non-material social judgments can affect prosocial behavior, but they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Lyle, H. F. (2017). Volver a Nuestras Raices: The Reemergence and Adaptation of Traditional Forms of Andean Reciprocity. *Journal of Latin American and Caribbean Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in the absence of formal or institutionalized punishment, collective action in the community is maintained through a combination of symbolic capital (reputation for generosity, skill, and reliability) and indirect social exclusion (withholding support from free riders). Households that invest more in collective action have larger and more robust support networks, suggesting positive assortment among prosocial individuals. Free riding is tolerated to some extent, and there is no evidence of direct punishment or sanctions. The system is sustained by the interplay of modified traditional reciprocity practices (ayni and minga), which have adapted to local needs. There is no direct measurement of efficiency or group payoff, but the system appears stable and resilient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as there is no experimental manipulation of punishment, no formal sanctions, and no measurement of efficiency or group payoff. However, it does suggest that in real-world settings, cooperation can be maintained without formal punishment through social mechanisms like reputation and indirect exclusion. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful as contextual evidence that alternative mechanisms (symbolic capital, social networks) can substitute for formal punishment, but it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Wismer, S., Pinto, A. I., Vail, A. L., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2014). Variation in Cleaner Wrasse Cooperation and Cognition: Influence of the Developmental Environment?. *Ethology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: Cleaner wrasse from continuous (socially complex) reefs performed better in laboratory cooperation and cognition tasks than those from patch (socially simple) reefs. Specifically, they were more likely to feed against their preference (cooperate), respond to image scoring (bystander) situations, and learn to prefer ephemeral (visitor) options in a biological market task. Juveniles from both environments performed similarly and better than adults from simple environments, suggesting ontogenetic (learning/experience) effects. No differences in aggressiveness or boldness were found between habitats. The study suggests that social environmental complexity shapes the development and expression of cooperative and cognitive behaviors, but does not directly address efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it uses game-theoretic tasks and discusses mechanisms like punishment, partner switching, and image scoring, the outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, learning) rather than payoff-based. The findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how social environment complexity moderates the development of cooperative strategies, but do not quantify efficiency or group payoff. Thus, the paper offers only adjacent, indirect support for prediction tasks focused on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Montoya, L. A., Montoya, I., & González, O. D. S. (2015). Lessons from collaborative governance and sociobiology theories for reinforcing sustained cooperation: a government food security case study. *Public Health*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that sustained interagency cooperation in public policy (here, food security in Bogotá) depends on (1) strong sponsorship (e.g., from central government or consensus), (2) stability of staff in the coordination system, and (3) mechanisms to reward or punish collaboration levels. The absence of these factors leads to decreased collaboration. The paper draws analogies from sociobiology (e.g., reciprocal altruism, punishment, reward) and collaborative governance theory, suggesting that similar mechanisms are relevant in human institutional settings. However, the findings are qualitative and do not include quantitative measures of efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is adjacent to the prediction task, as it discusses the importance of reward and punishment mechanisms for sustaining cooperation in real-world, multi-agent institutional settings, and draws on sociobiological theory. However, it does not manipulate or measure game design dimensions, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main value is in supporting the general idea that mechanisms for reward and punishment can help sustain cooperation, especially when other supports (like sponsorship or staff stability) are present. It does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Artavia-Mora, L., Bedi, A. S., & Rieger, M. (2017). Intuitive help and punishment in the field. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that time delay (deliberation) reduces the likelihood of helping strangers and norm violators in the field, with helping rates dropping by 18-27 percentage points depending on the scenario. However, time delay does not significantly affect the rate of direct punishment of norm violators. The study also finds that time delay increases self-reported fear of retaliation for punishing. The design is a one-shot, non-strategic, individual-level field experiment, not a public goods game or its direct variant, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar strategic environments. Its design lacks group interaction, repeated rounds, contribution choices, or payoff structures analogous to PGGs. The main outcomes are binary helping or punishment actions in response to norm violations, not group payoffs or efficiency. While the findings may be contextually informative about the psychological underpinnings of prosocial and punitive behavior, they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. The study is best used as contextual or mechanistic background, not as direct input for efficiency prediction models.

- source: Kaltwasser, L., Hildebrandt, A., Wilhelm, O., & Sommer, W. (2016). Behavioral and neuronal determinants of negative reciprocity in the ultimatum game. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both prosociality (as measured by social value orientation) and assertiveness predict the likelihood of rejecting unfair offers in the ultimatum game, supporting both the strong reciprocity and status defense models of negative reciprocity. Neural data (feedback negativity) show that individuals with higher negative reciprocity have stronger neural responses to proposer fairness. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not manipulate punishment as a treatment variable. The findings are limited to the behavioral and neural correlates of negative reciprocity in the UG, not to efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is an ultimatum game, not a public goods game, and the main outcome is the rejection rate of unfair offers (a form of costly punishment), not group payoff or efficiency. While the study is relevant for understanding individual motivations for costly punishment (negative reciprocity), it does not inform how punishment affects group efficiency or payoff under different game design dimensions. Thus, it should not be weighted strongly for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Raihani, N. J. (2014). Hidden altruism in a real-world setting. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The main empirical finding is that donors to an online fundraising platform are more likely to conceal their identity when making donations that are either much lower or much higher than the norm for a given appeal. The paper interprets this as evidence that individuals may hide norm-violating cooperative behavior (especially high donations) to avoid social costs such as punishment or ostracism, as has been observed in public goods game experiments. However, the study does not directly measure punishment, sanctions, or efficiency, and the context is real-world charitable giving rather than a controlled public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that social punishment or ostracism can target both low and high contributors in cooperative settings, which may limit the extent of visible over-contribution. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that social costs for norm-violating contributions (including over-contribution) may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes. Its main value is in highlighting the potential for negative social responses to both under- and over-contribution.

- source: Kim, M., & Sivan, E. W. Z. (2017). Faking It Is Hard to Do: Entrepreneurial Norm Enforcement and Suspicions of Deviance. *Sociological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that when norm enforcement is mandated (i.e., actors are required to respond to deviance), enforcers are perceived as more sincere and less likely to be suspected of ulterior motives, even in high-accusability contexts. In contrast, when enforcement is entrepreneurial (i.e., voluntary and not mandated) and the context is high accusability (where actors have reason to fear being accused of deviance), enforcers are viewed with greater suspicion and are more likely to be seen as having ulterior motives. In low-accusability contexts, entrepreneurial enforcement does not arouse suspicion. The study does not measure or report on efficiency, group payoff, or any economic outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are about social perceptions of norm enforcers, not about behavioral or payoff consequences of punishment. The design is adjacent to PGG-like environments in that it involves norm enforcement and public goods logic, but it is not an economic game and does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs, but may be useful for understanding psychological or reputational moderators of norm enforcement.

- source: Sääksvuori, L., & Ramalingam, A. (2015). Bargaining under surveillance: Evidence from a three-person ultimatum game. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that third-party surveillance (the ability of an inactive third-party to observe pre-play chat between proposer and responder) does not affect the allocation of resources in a three-person ultimatum game. Whether the right to observe is exogenously granted or endogenously purchased, and whether the third-party can signal their presence, has no significant effect on the amount allocated to the third-party. Third-parties are willing to pay for the right to observe, and are over-optimistic about the effect of their observation, but their presence does not change outcomes. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The design is a one-shot three-person ultimatum game with pre-play chat and optional third-party monitoring. The main finding is that third-party surveillance alone (without punishment or sanctioning power) does not affect allocations or outcomes. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, suggesting that mere monitoring or transparency, in the absence of sanctions, may not improve efficiency or resource allocation.
