- source: Panchanathan, K., Frankenhuis, W. E., & Silk, J. B. (2013). The bystander effect in an N-person dictator game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds a strong bystander effect in an N-person dictator game: when there is only one dictator, recipients receive significantly more than when there are two or three dictators. The average transfer as a fraction of the 'equal share' drops from 57% (one dictator) to 36% (two or three dictators). Recipient payoffs are about 50% higher in the one-dictator condition than in the two- or three-dictator conditions. This effect persists even when uncertainty about others' actions is removed (strategy method) and when dictators can communicate and coordinate (bargaining method). The results suggest that most participants transform the situation into a prisoner's dilemma, leading to low transfers in groups. The paper does not study punishment or sanctions, but discusses the possibility that introducing punishment could alleviate the bystander effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the number of potential contributors in a many-to-one helping game (adjacent to PGG) reduces efficiency/welfare, even when communication or strategy methods are used. The absence of punishment or sanctions means the paper does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does establish a robust baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency declines with group size. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar games without punishment, efficiency will be lower as group size increases. It also suggests that communication alone may not be sufficient to restore efficiency. However, since punishment is not present, the paper cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, only to set a control baseline.

- source: Liu, G. F., Lin, C. D., & Xin, Z. Q. (2014). The Effects of Within- and Between-Group Competition on Trust and Trustworthiness among Acquaintances. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both within-group and between-group competition increase trust (investment) among acquaintances in an investment game. However, within-group competition reduces trustworthiness (return ratio) and leads to lower payoffs for trustors but higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trustee payoffs but does not significantly affect trustworthiness or trustor payoffs compared to the non-competition condition. The results suggest that competition type differentially affects efficiency for trustors and trustees, and that within-group competition can undermine overall group efficiency by harming trustor payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, introducing within-group competition can increase trust but reduce trustworthiness, leading to lower payoffs for trustors and higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trust and trustee payoffs but does not harm trustor payoffs. Since the study does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that competitive structures can have asymmetric effects on efficiency depending on the role. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence or absence of competition (and its type) can moderate efficiency outcomes, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs.

- source: DeAngelo, G., Ferrell, P., & McCannon, B. (2017). Sources of deviant behavior: Contrasting alternative explanations in the laboratory. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that individuals who experience deteriorating social interactions (declining trust) in a repeated Trust Game are more likely to extract more from a common resource in a subsequent game, reducing group efficiency. Similarly, individuals' expectations about the typical behavior of others (empirical social norms) strongly predict their own extraction behavior. The study does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related outcomes. Instead, it suggests that interventions targeting social history or social norms may be more effective than sanctions in reducing deviant behavior and improving group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in the experimental design. However, it provides strong evidence that social history and social norms are important determinants of efficiency in common resource dilemmas. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment and for considering alternative interventions.

- source: McClung, J. S., Placì, S., Bangerter, A., Clément, F., & Bshary, R. (2017). The language of cooperation: shared intentionality drives variation in helping as a function of group membership. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper introduces a joint foraging game (the 'egg hunt') in which pairs of participants can help each other at a cost to themselves, with helping not explicitly instructed. The design manipulates group membership (in-group vs out-group) and the ability to talk (talking vs no talking). Both in-group membership and the ability to talk significantly increased costly helping and overall group success (total screws collected, which determined monetary payoffs). Shared intentionality in conversation was identified as the key psychological mechanism mediating the effect of group membership on helping. Costly helping was costly to the helper but increased total group payoff. There were no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. The game is adjacent to public goods or prisoner's dilemma games, but differs in that helping is not explicitly structured and there is no punishment or reward system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that enabling communication (chat) and making group membership salient can increase efficiency (total group payoff) in joint action games where helping is possible but not enforced. The effect is mediated by shared intentionality in communication. While the game is not a standard public goods game and does not include punishment, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in games where communication and group identity are manipulated. The absence of punishment means the paper does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that communication alone can have a strong positive effect on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the importance of chat/communication and group identity, but does not provide evidence about punishment, punishment cost, or related dimensions.

- source: Chiang, Y. S. (2015). Good Samaritans in Networks: An Experiment on How Networks Influence Egalitarian Sharing and the Evolution of Inequality. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that voluntary sharing in networks can reduce inequality, but the extent depends on network topology. When networks connect the very rich and very poor (Lattice_Hetero, SF_Negative), local inequality is salient and motivates more redistribution, leading to significant reductions in the Gini coefficient. In other network structures (Full, Lattice_Homo, SF_Positive), inequality is not significantly reduced. The study does not involve public goods provision, punishment, or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency as the ratio of group payoff to the fully cooperative benchmark. The findings are most relevant to how network structure moderates voluntary redistribution and the evolution of inequality.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency in the sense required for the downstream prediction task. The findings are about voluntary redistribution and inequality in networked settings, which is adjacent but not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The paper may provide some contextual insight into how network structure affects redistribution, but it does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Grujic, J., Gracia-Lázaro, C., Milinski, M., Semmann, D., Traulsen, A., Cuesta, J. A., Moreno, Y., & Sánchez, A. (2014). A comparative analysis of spatial Prisoner's Dilemma experiments: Conditional cooperation and payoff irrelevance. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In spatial Prisoner's Dilemma experiments on lattices, cooperation rates start high but quickly decline to low, nonzero levels (15-35%). (2) Defectors consistently earn more than cooperators, with a significant negative correlation between cooperation and earnings. (3) The behavioral rule most consistent with the data is moody conditional cooperation, where players' probability to cooperate depends on their own previous action and the number of cooperating neighbors. (4) There is no evidence that spatial structure (network topology, number of neighbors, or system size) promotes higher cooperation or changes the behavioral rule. (5) Payoff-based imitation rules (imitate-the-best, Fermi rule) do not explain the observed behavior. (6) The paper does not study punishment, rewards, or sanctions, and does not report group efficiency relative to the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide indirect evidence that in the absence of punishment, cooperation rates and group payoffs remain low in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma games, and that spatial structure alone does not increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper may help establish a baseline for control (no-punishment) efficiency in spatial PD-like environments, but offers no information about the effect of enabling punishment. The behavioral findings (moody conditional cooperation) may inform models of baseline behavior in the absence of incentives.

- source: Horiuchi, S. (2015). Emergence and Collapse of the Norm of Resource Sharing Around Locally Abundant Resources. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that workers' effort (reciprocity) increases with their own wage (direct reciprocity) and, when their own wage is unobservable, also with the coworker's wage (indirect reciprocity via signaling). When both wages are observable, the coworker's wage has no effect on effort, indicating that indirect reciprocity is driven by signaling about one's own treatment, not by altruism toward the coworker. There is no evidence that inequality aversion explains the observed patterns. The main outcome is effort, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is adjacent in that it studies reciprocity and information in a gift-exchange game, but the main outcome is effort, not efficiency, and there is no punishment mechanism. The findings may inform predictions about the role of information and signaling in reciprocity, but not about punishment or efficiency. Thus, its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency is limited.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Hoffman, M., Nowak, M. A., & Rand, D. G. (2016). Uncalculating cooperation is used to signal trustworthiness. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that uncalculating cooperation is used as a signal of trustworthiness: participants are more likely to cooperate without calculating costs when their decision process is observable to others, and such behavior leads to greater trust and actual trustworthiness in a subsequent trust game. The findings support a reputation-based account of uncalculating cooperation. However, the study does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency in public goods or similar group cooperation games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings about reputation and uncalculating cooperation may be of theoretical interest for understanding motivations for cooperation, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Blake, P. R., Rand, D. G., Tingley, D., & Warneken, F. (2015). The shadow of the future promotes cooperation in a repeated prisoner's dilemma for children. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Children aged 10-11 cooperate significantly more in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games than in one-shot games, demonstrating sensitivity to the 'shadow of the future' and the potential for direct reciprocity. Conditional cooperation strategies are observed, with girls and children with fewer conduct problems being more forgiving and cooperative. The study does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It is relevant for understanding how repeated interaction (direct reciprocity) increases cooperation in dyadic games among children, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency. Thus, it offers only indirect, contextual support for the prediction task, mainly by illustrating that repeated interaction alone (without punishment) can increase cooperation rates in dyadic settings.

- source: Capraro, V., & Cococcioni, G. (2015). Social setting, intuition and experience in laboratory experiments interact to shape cooperative decision-making. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that, among Indian participants (a population presumed to have internalized non-cooperative strategies), promoting intuition (via time pressure) versus deliberation (via time delay) does not affect cooperation rates among inexperienced subjects. However, experienced subjects (those with prior exposure to economic games) are more cooperative than inexperienced ones, but only under time pressure. The main dependent variable is the percentage of endowment transferred in a continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a two-person continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma without punishment or reward options. The findings are only indirectly relevant: they suggest that cooperation rates (and thus, by implication, group payoffs) are low in non-cooperative settings without institutional support or experience, and that experience with economic games can increase cooperation under intuitive conditions. However, since no punishment mechanism is present, and no efficiency or group payoff is reported, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. It may be useful for contextualizing baseline cooperation levels in non-cooperative settings.

- source: Romano, A., Balliet, D., Yamagishi, T., & Liu, J. H. (2017). Parochial trust and cooperation across 17 societies. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that people cooperate more with ingroup than outgroup members in a trust game, but reputational concern (common knowledge of group membership) increases cooperation with both ingroup and outgroup members. Men show slightly more ingroup favoritism than women, but social value orientation predicts general cooperation rather than parochialism. There is little cross-societal variation in ingroup favoritism, and this variation is not explained by material security, religiosity, or pathogen stress. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. It is relevant for understanding how reputational concerns (a form of indirect social incentive) can increase cooperation, but not for payoff-based or punishment-based interventions. Its design and findings are adjacent to the prediction task, offering only indirect context for how social information and group identity affect cooperation.

- source: Dunning, D., Anderson, J. E., Schlösser, T., Ehlebracht, D., & Fetchenhauer, D. (2014). Trust at Zero Acquaintance: More a Matter of Respect Than Expectation of Reward. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that trust in zero-acquaintance trust games is driven more by internalized moral norms (especially a norm of respect) than by expectations of reward or risk tolerance. Participants report that they 'should' trust more than they 'want' to, and this sense of obligation predicts trust behavior. Making trust decisions public does not increase trust, suggesting the norm is moral rather than social. When the trust game is altered so that the recipient's character is not at stake (e.g., outcome determined by coin flip), trust rates drop. The paper does not study public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes, and thus does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that in trust games without punishment, trust rates can be high due to internalized moral norms, even when efficiency or payoff maximization would not predict such behavior. The findings are only indirectly relevant to public goods games, and not at all relevant to punishment or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., Lergetporer, P., & Sutter, M. (2016). Cooperation and discrimination within and across language borders: Evidence from children in a bilingual city. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that children cooperate more with in-group members (same class), less with out-group members of the same language, and least with out-group members of a different language. Both in-group favoritism and language group discrimination are observed, with the latter emerging mainly among older German-speaking children. Cooperation increases with age, and girls cooperate more than boys. Beliefs about partner cooperation are higher for in-group partners and are positively correlated with own cooperation. No punishment, sanctioning, or reward mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods or prisoner's dilemma games. It is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms. However, it provides contextual evidence on baseline cooperation rates and discrimination in the absence of punishment, which may be relevant for understanding control conditions in similar environments. The absence of punishment or reward means it cannot inform the treatment effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Corgnet, B., Espín, A. M., & Hernán-González, R. (2015). The cognitive basis of social behavior: cognitive reflection overrides antisocial but not always prosocial motives. *Frontiers in Behavioral Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across two lab studies (US and Spain), individuals with higher CRT scores are more likely to make choices consistent with 'mild' altruism (increasing others' payoffs at low/no cost), while those with lower CRT scores are more likely to act out of egalitarian or spiteful motives. The studies use non-strategic, anonymous allocation tasks (not public goods or social dilemma games) and do not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are directly measured or reported. The findings suggest that cognitive reflection is associated with a specific pattern of social preferences, but do not inform about the effects of punishment or efficiency in public goods contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a public goods or social dilemma design. The findings are about individual cognitive style and social motives in non-strategic allocation games. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about treatment efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Brodbeck, F. C., Kugler, K. G., Reif, J. A. M., & Maier, M. A. (2013). Morals Matter in Economic Games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across four lab experiments, participants induced to adopt a Unity moral motive (via framing or subliminal priming) allocated more money to another participant in a one-shot risk-sharing game than those induced to adopt a Proportionality motive. This effect was only present in interpersonal (DSG) and not solitary (SIG) settings. The study does not use a public goods game or any punishment/sanction mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are about the psychological determinants of solidarity behavior (unconditional giving) in risk-sharing contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not manipulate or measure punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. However, it provides adjacent evidence that psychological framing (moral motives) can strongly affect other-regarding behavior in one-shot risk-sharing games. The design is not a PGG, and the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for the prediction task is limited.

- source: Lamba, S., & Mace, R. (2013). The evolution of fairness: explaining variation in bargaining behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in one-shot, anonymous ultimatum games, proposer offers vary significantly across villages, but responder behavior is highly uniform: almost all offers are accepted, and responders' self-reported minimum acceptable offers are very low. There is no evidence for cultural conformity or coordinated expectations at the ethno-linguistic group level. The findings suggest that local environmental factors, rather than group-level fairness norms, may drive variation in bargaining behavior. The study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about variation in bargaining behavior and the lack of coordinated expectations in the ultimatum game may be contextually informative for understanding heterogeneity in social preferences, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Diekmann, A., Przepiorka, W., & Rauhut, H. (2015). Lifting the veil of ignorance: An experiment on the contagiousness of norm violations. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when subjects are informed about the extent of others' norm violations (cheating in a dice game), their own rate of norm violation increases in subsequent rounds, compared to a control group with no such information. This effect is robust to whether the information is about an external group or the current session. The effect is not due to punishment, as the design eliminates any possibility of sanctions. The effect is moderated by subjects' prior beliefs: underestimators of norm violations increase cheating when informed, while overestimators may decrease it. The study provides evidence for the contagiousness of norm violations via conditional norm compliance, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as there is no punishment, no group payoff, and no public good. The findings are relevant for understanding how information about norm violations can erode compliance, but not for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and outcomes are adjacent to the prediction task, offering insight into norm dynamics but not into payoff-based outcomes or the role of sanctions.

  findings: The study finds that in the absence of punishment or differential allocation of collective goods, individuals who contribute more to collective action gain reputational benefits and larger social support networks. These networks are associated with improved health outcomes for households. The results support the idea that reputational and network benefits can help sustain cooperation in collective action settings, even when free riding is not punished. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or surplus, and does not use a public goods game or experimental design.

However, it does not provide data on efficiency or payoff outcomes as defined in public goods games, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment. Therefore, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. While one included study uses a public goods game, the outcome is individual generosity, not group efficiency.   findings: The study finds that in sociable weavers, individual investment in cooperative nest construction (measured as time spent and items added) is positively predicted by genetic relatedness to the group.   dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that children's cooperation rates are positively associated with parental education and parents' perception of their child's superiority. Asymmetric payoffs in mutual cooperation do not affect children's cooperation rates. There is a positive but statistically insignificant alignment between parent and child cooperation. Children from higher-educated families have better-calibrated beliefs about others' cooperation. No evidence is provided on efficiency or group payoff, and no punishment or sanctioning is present in the design.

The absence of punishment and lack of payoff-based outcomes mean it is not directly useful for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may provide weak contextual evidence about baseline cooperation rates in young children, but not about efficiency or the effect of punishment.

- source: Swakman, V., Molleman, L., Ule, A., & Egas, M. (2016). Reputation-based cooperation: empirical evidence for behavioral strategies. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that people condition their helping decisions on both first-order (recipient's past helping) and second-order (context of recipient's past helping/defection) information. Donors are especially interested in 2nd-order information about defection, and tend to reward 'justified defection' (denying help to defectors). There is strong individual heterogeneity: some use only first-order information, some use both, and a minority are unconditional cooperators or defectors. The availability of 2nd-order information (free or costly) does not significantly increase overall helping rates compared to the control, though helping rates are numerically higher when 2nd-order information is available. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The game is an iterated indirect helping game with binary helping decisions, and the main outcomes are behavioral (helping rates, information use, strategy classification). While the findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms of reputation-based cooperation, they do not inform prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The only indirect relevance is that richer information about others' behavior can (non-significantly) increase helping rates, but this is not about efficiency or payoff, nor about punishment.

- source: Capraro, V., Smyth, C., Mylona, K., & Niblo, G. A. (2014). Benevolent Characteristics Promote Cooperative Behaviour among Humans. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that (1) benevolence—defined as unilaterally increasing another's payoff beyond one's own—exists in the population, (2) individuals who are more benevolent are more likely to cooperate in a subsequent one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma, and (3) priming participants to think benevolently increases their cooperation rate compared to priming malevolence. The study uses custom BT games and standard two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games, but does not include public goods games, punishment, or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings about benevolence and cooperation may be contextually relevant for understanding individual factors that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Song, F., & Zhong, C. B. (2015). You scratch his back, he scratches mine and I'll scratch yours: Deception in simultaneous cyclic networks. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in simultaneous cyclic networks, participants are more likely to engage in deception (lying about the endowment size) on behalf of others than when acting for themselves. This effect is present both in small (dyadic) and larger (20-30 person) cyclic networks, and is driven by the anticipation of indirect reciprocity. Breaking the reciprocity loop reduces deception. The study uses a modified ultimatum game, not a public goods game, and does not involve punishment or sanctions. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not involve punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a modified ultimatum game rather than a public goods game. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Gächter, S., Gerhards, L., & Nosenzo, D. (2017). The importance of peers for compliance with norms of fair sharing. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The presence of peers strongly influences what participants perceive as socially appropriate sharing, but this does not translate into corresponding changes in actual sharing behavior. There is substantial heterogeneity in both norm perceptions and willingness to comply with norms. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. It is relevant for understanding how peer information shapes norm perceptions and sharing behavior in dictator-like settings, but not for the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Everett, J. A. C., Faber, N. S., & Crockett, M. J. (2015). The influence of social preferences and reputational concerns on intergroup prosocial behaviour in gains and losses contexts. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants are more likely to help ingroup than outgroup members, and more likely to help when decisions are public (i.e., reputational concerns are present). However, ingroup favouritism is observed even in private (anonymous) conditions, suggesting a role for social preferences beyond reputation management. The effect of public/private decision setting is stronger in loss contexts than gain contexts, but the pattern of ingroup favouritism is similar in both. The cost/benefit (net expected value) of helping influences overall helping rates, but does not moderate the effect of group membership or decision setting. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its design is adjacent to PGGs in that it involves costly prosocial acts, but the outcome is individual helping rather than collective action. The findings may be relevant for understanding the role of social preferences and reputational concerns in prosocial behavior, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Alberti, F., & Güth, W. (2013). Studying deception without deceiving participants: An experiment of deception experiments. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that strong incentives lead participants in the experimenter role to deceive others, but that this deception does not significantly affect the behavior of allocators or recipients in subsequent rounds, even after debriefing. There is no evidence that deception changes generosity or acceptance thresholds. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not involve punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not involve punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and the game is a dictator game variant rather than a public goods game. The findings may be marginally informative for understanding the robustness of participant behavior to negative experiences (such as deception), but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions in PGGs.

- source: Capraro, V., & Marcelletti, A. (2014). Do good actions inspire good actions in others?. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that good actions (altruism or benevolence) do not inspire further good actions in anonymous, one-shot economic games. Specifically, being the recipient of a good action (either a split in a Dictator Game or a benevolent act in a Benevolence Game) does not significantly increase the probability of cooperating in a subsequent Prisoner's Dilemma or splitting in a subsequent Dictator Game. The results are robust to exclusion of participants who did not believe the other player was real or failed to provide a valid reason for their choice. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings on the lack of behavioral contagion of good actions in one-shot, anonymous settings may be contextually relevant for understanding baseline cooperation or altruism, but not for the prediction of efficiency under punishment treatments.

- source: Andrighetto, G., Grieco, D., & Tummolini, L. (2015). Perceived legitimacy of normative expectations motivates compliance with social norms when nobody is watching. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that communication (pre-play messages) makes social norms salient and increases both trust (A's willingness to enter the game) and trustworthiness (B's willingness to ROLL, i.e., act cooperatively). The presence of a costly EXIT option allows Bs to avoid public norm violation by deceiving As, helping to distinguish between motivations for compliance. The key result is that, when no monitoring or punishment is possible, compliance is driven by the perceived legitimacy of normative expectations rather than by guilt aversion or fear of losing esteem. The study does not report or analyze efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that, in the absence of punishment, communication and the salience of social norms can increase cooperative behavior (trust/trustworthiness) in a trust game. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that, even without punishment, norm compliance can be achieved through perceived legitimacy of normative expectations, but it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Pan, X. S., & Houser, D. (2013). Cooperation during cultural group formation promotes trust towards members of out-groups. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the degree of cooperation required during group formation (cooperative vs. independent production tasks) causally affects trust and reciprocity in subsequent trust games. Specifically, participants from cooperative production groups (CP) trust and reciprocate with out-group members at similar rates as with in-group members, showing little parochial altruism. In contrast, participants from independent production groups (IP) display significant in-group favouritism and out-group discrimination. These effects persist even after negative experiences (betrayal) in the trust game. The results are explained by differences in beliefs about out-group trustworthiness, shaped by the group formation process. No efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that the cooperative context of group formation can influence trust and reciprocity, but not in a payoff or efficiency framework. It should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: van Apeldoorn, J., & Schram, A. (2016). Indirect Reciprocity; A Field Experiment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper provides field evidence that downstream indirect reciprocity operates in an online community: members with a history of helping others are more likely to have their own requests for help accepted. This effect is robust across most subgroups. There is no evidence for upstream indirect reciprocity. The study does not involve a public goods game or any form of punishment or sanctioning, nor does it report on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not involve punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes, and the experimental design is not a PGG or close variant. Its findings on indirect reciprocity may be of theoretical interest for understanding cooperation, but provide no direct or indirect evidence for the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Glowacki, L., & Wrangham, R. W. (2013). The Role of Rewards in Motivating Participation in Simple Warfare. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The study finds a positive correlation between the number of cultural rewards available to warriors and the annual mortality rate from warfare across 20 small-scale societies. This supports the hypothesis that rewards (both material and immaterial, such as status) motivate individuals to participate in risky group conflict. Punitive sanctions for non-participation are found to be rare and not necessary to explain participation. The paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment in a public-goods-game-like environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main relevance is in discussing the motivational role of rewards (not punishment) in incentivizing participation in costly group activities (warfare), and in noting that explicit punitive sanctions are rare in the ethnographic record for simple warfare. The findings suggest that rewards can increase participation in risky group activities, but do not provide evidence about the efficiency or payoff consequences of punishment mechanisms in controlled game environments.

- source: Rand, D. G., & Epstein, Z. G. (2014). Risking Your Life without a Second Thought: Intuitive Decision-Making and Extreme Altruism. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who engage in extreme altruism overwhelmingly describe their decision-making as intuitive, fast, and automatic, rather than deliberative or reasoned. This is supported both by human ratings of interview statements and by linguistic analysis of inhibitory language. The results are robust even in cases where the altruist had time to deliberate. The study does not involve any economic games, public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.

It does not manipulate or analyze any of the game design dimensions of interest (e.g., player count, punishment, MPCR, etc.), nor does it report on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about intuitive decision-making in real-world extreme altruism may be of theoretical interest for understanding the psychology of cooperation, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Buckholtz, J. W., Martin, J. W., Treadway, M. T., Jan, K., Zald, D. H., Jones, O., & Marois, R. (2015). From Blame to Punishment: Disrupting Prefrontal Cortex Activity Reveals Norm Enforcement Mechanisms. *Neuron*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (DLPFC) plays a selective, causal role in punishment decision-making for norm violations. Disrupting DLPFC function via rTMS reduces the amount of punishment assigned to culpable agents, especially for low- to moderate-harm crimes, but does not affect blameworthiness ratings. fMRI results show that DLPFC activity is higher during punishment decisions than during blameworthiness judgments. The study supports an integration-and-selection model in which DLPFC integrates information about harm and culpability to determine punishment. However, the study does not use a public goods game or any economic game with payoffs, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not use such games or report any payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding the cognitive and neural mechanisms of punishment assignment in norm enforcement, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency or group payoff in economic games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context about punishment, but not for the downstream efficiency prediction task.

  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  findings: The study finds that Uzbek participants (from a less market-oriented economy) make higher offers in the ultimatum game and are more likely to return excess money in the revelation game than Kazakh participants (from a more market-oriented economy). The results suggest that pro-social behavior (fairness and honesty) is weaker in more market-oriented economies. However, the study does not use public goods games, does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

It does not use a PGG or variant, does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. - source: Bügelmayer, E., & Spiess, C. K. (2014). Spite and cognitive skills in preschoolers. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that preschool children with higher cognitive skills are more likely to display spiteful behavior in allocation tasks, especially boys. Spiteful behavior is operationalized as choosing to reduce another child's payoff, sometimes at a cost to oneself. The relationship is robust to controls for personality, empathy, and household characteristics. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or any aggregate welfare outcome. The experimental design is not a public goods game or a repeated group interaction, but rather a set of one-shot allocation decisions between the subject and an anonymous other.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it discusses spite as a motive related to punishment in the literature, the experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or group cooperation. The findings may be of indirect interest for understanding the psychological underpinnings of punitive or spiteful motives, but they do not inform the quantitative relationship between punishment and efficiency in PGG-like games. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Acedo-Carmona, C., & Gomila, A. (2015). Trust matters: a cross-cultural comparison of Northern Ghana and Oaxaca groups. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that cooperation in both regions is grounded in small, personal trust groups rather than being homogeneously distributed within cultural groups. Northern Ghana, with greater environmental scarcity, exhibits larger and more inclusive trust and cooperation networks, more openness to newcomers, and less emphasis on punishment for betrayal of trust compared to Oaxaca. In Oaxaca, trust and cooperation are more restricted to close relatives, and betrayal of trust is more likely to be punished or not forgiven. The paper argues that environmental scarcity fosters broader, more cohesive trust networks. However, the study does not use experimental games, does not manipulate punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use game-theoretic or experimental methods, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or related outcomes. The discussion of punishment is limited to informal, social responses to betrayal of trust, not to formal sanctioning mechanisms. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, though it may provide some anthropological context about the role of trust and informal punishment in real-world cooperation.

- source: Kasper, C., & Mulder, M. B. (2015). Who Helps and Why? Cooperative Networks in Mpimbwe. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that in a rural Tanzanian village, helping behavior is generally low and is best predicted by reciprocity (contingency), with kinship and affinal relationships also playing roles. Wealthier individuals are less likely to participate in reciprocal helping relationships and receive less help. Geographic proximity and age also influence helping patterns. The study discusses the erosion of traditional cooperation networks in the context of increasing market integration and inequality. Witchcraft accusations are mentioned as a form of social sanction, but there is no formal punishment system or experimental manipulation of sanctions. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not use a PGG or any formal game, nor does it measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. The study is observational, not experimental, and focuses on naturalistic helping behavior, not on the effects of introducing or varying punishment mechanisms. Witchcraft accusations are discussed as a form of social pressure, but not in a way that is structurally or functionally equivalent to PGG punishment treatments. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task. It may provide some adjacent context about the role of social sanctions in real-world cooperation, but not in a way that is mappable to the target prediction problem.

  findings: The study finds that both student status and self-selection (volunteering) can have small effects on social preference behaviors, but these effects are generally minor and not economically significant. Self-selected students (the typical lab subject pool) behave very similarly to the broader population in Dictator, Ultimatum, and Trust Games. The only significant differences are small and mainly concern strategic behavior and trust/reciprocity among non-students. The results support the external validity of using self-selected students for social preference experiments, but do not address public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.

It does not include any public goods game, punishment, or sanctioning treatment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings are only relevant to the representativeness of subject pools in social preference experiments using Dictator, Ultimatum, and Trust Games. It should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

In some cases, eyespots also increase the likelihood of actual conservation behavior (e.g., donations), though this effect is mixed. The effect of eyespots on conservation attitudes is mediated by increased aesthetic preference. The study does not address economic games, punishment, or group efficiency.

It does not manipulate or analyze any of the game design dimensions relevant to public goods games, nor does it report payoff-based outcomes. - source: Cai, W., Huang, X. Q., Wu, S., & Kou, Y. (2015). Dishonest behavior is not affected by an image of watching eyes. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that implicit reputation cues (images of watching eyes) do not reduce dishonest behavior in laboratory settings, even when dishonesty leads to higher monetary payoffs or reputational benefits. This null effect was consistent across different tasks and motivational framings. The study also finds that higher moral identity predicts less dishonesty, but the eyes image does not affect this. The pilot experiment replicates the known effect of eyes images on generosity in a dictator game, but the main experiments focus on dishonesty, not cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a PGG or close variant. The findings about implicit reputation cues and dishonesty may be of tangential interest for understanding the limits of non-punitive interventions, but do not inform the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Hackman, J., Munira, S., Jasmin, K., & Hruschka, D. (2017). Revisiting Psychological Mechanisms in the Anthropology of Altruism. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that in four rural Bangladeshi communities, perceived social closeness is a strong and independent predictor of helping behavior between households, even after controlling for genetic relatedness, affinal ties, and geographic proximity. The odds of helping are much higher for those rated as very close, and closeness explains a substantial portion of the association between helping and other variables. Friendship nominations also predict helping, but closeness remains a robust predictor even when both are included. The study does not use any experimental or game-theoretic design, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a PGG or any economic game, does not manipulate or analyze punishment, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about social closeness and helping in naturalistic social networks may be of theoretical interest for understanding cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGG-like games.
